ii 
 
PREFACE 
 
I, the Chairman of the Department-related Parliamentary Standing Committee on Health and 
Family Welfare, having been authorized by the Committee to present the Report on its behalf, 
hereby present this 170th Report of the Committee on Action Taken by the G overnment on the 
Recommendations/ Observations contained in the 163rd Report on ―Demands For Grants 2025 -26 
(Demand No. 46) of the Department of Health and Family Welfare (Ministry of Health and Family 
Welfare)‖.  
2. The 163rd Report of the Department -related Parliamentary Standing Committee on Health 
and Family Welfare was presented to the Rajya Sabha and laid on the Table of Lok Sabha on  12th 
March, 2025. The Action Taken Notes of the Government on the recommendations containe d in the 
Report was received from the Department in the Month of July, 2025.   
3.  The Committee made a total of 176 recommendations in the 16 3rd Report, out of which 96 
recommendations have been accepted by the Department and have been categorized under 
Chapter-I. There are 09 recommendations that the Committee does not desire to pursue in view of 
the Department‘s replies that have been categorized under Chapter-II. There are 65 
recommendations/ observations, in respect of which replies of the Department have not been 
accepted by the Committee and the Committee has made further recommendations thereon and has 
been categorized under Chapter-III. While 06 recommendations/observations in respect of which 
final reply of the Department has not been received, have been categorized under Chapter- IV. 
4. The Committee, in its meeting held on  9th December, 2025 considered the Draft Report and 
adopted the same. 
 
 
 
NEW DELHI                            PROF. RAM GOPAL YADAV  
9th December, 2025                                    CHAIRMAN 
Pausha, 1947 (Saka)     Department-related Parliamentary Standing  
      Committee on Health and Family Welfare

Welfare)‖. The Report on the subject was presented/laid to the House on 12th March, 2025.

1 
 
CHAPTER–I 
 
RECOMMENDATIONS/OBSERVATIONS THAT HAVE BEEN ACCEPTED BY 
THE GOVERNMENT 
 
1.1 Vision Mission and Objectives 
 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
1.1.1 The Committee takes note of the vision, mission and objectives of the Department of 
Health and Family Welfare which determines the roadmap for activities of the Department. 
The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Department should undertake the 
projects/schemes/plans in consonance with the vision and mission of the Department and 
execute the same with the intention to achieve the cherished objectives. The Committee 
believes that the Department would move forward with Management by Objective (MBO) 
rather than Management by Activities (MBA) to achieve the targets set in National Health 
Policy (NHP) 2017 and under specific projects.   
(Para 1.3.2 of the Report) 
Action Taken 
 
1.1.2 In line with the Committee‘s recommendation, the Key Deliverables (KDs) under 
National Health Mission aim to monitor progress against the objective of the programmes. In 
the recently concluded Mid Term review, all the KDs were discussed and areas where states 
need to focus were emphasized.  These interventions are closely monitored thro ugh 
measurable outputs and outcomes, ensuring alignment with the Department‘s strategic 
priorities and desired health impact, such as operationalization of Ayushman Arogya Mandir, 
integration of NCD services, digital health interventions, and capacity buil ding efforts. 
herefore, Department is moving forward with Management of Objective (MBO) approach by 
regular monitoring of the KDs and taking regular state reviews on progress on targets set at 
the beginning of every 2 years PIP period. 
 
 
1.2 Institutional Mechanism 
 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
1.2.1 The Committee is of the view that Institutions/Organisations are the organs of the 
Department which play pivotal role in the successful functioning of the Department, 
therefore, efficient and effective working of the organisations/institutions is indispens able for 
result-oriented implementation of the undertaken projects/schemes. The Committee, 
therefore, desires that the officers at the top of the Department should ensure to set up 
dynamic working equilibrium amongst various institutions in order to achiev e the objectives 
of the Department. The Committee feels that a streamlined monitoring mechanism with 
Always Better Control (ABC) would further ensure the desirable Rate of Returns (RoR). The 

2 
 
Committee, therefore, recommends that the Department should keep constant Strength, 
Weaknesses, Opportunities and Threats (SWOT) Analysis of its organs/institutions for better 
results. 
(Para 1.4.3 of the Report) 
Action Taken 
 
1.2.2 The Department conducts periodic evaluation to assess programme effectiveness and 
resources uti lization. There are clear and defined roles and responsibilities within the 
department. Regular meetings with the Institutions and other stakeholders are held to review 
the status of project works, operationalization of Institutes including recruitment, procurement 
alignment of activities with desired outcomes etc. within the Outcome Output Framework.  
Besides this, in the Senior officers‘ meetings, in the Ministry/ Department/Institutions 
regularly review alignment of departmental activities with desired outcomes. The Department 
acknowledges the Committee‘s insightful observations on the strategic relevance of 
institutional coherence and the emphasis on enhancing operational alignment through 
mechanisms such as the Always Better Control (ABC) framework and  periodic SWOT 
analysis.The NHM is now being modded as a flagship scheme and there is 100% Budget 
utilisation under this scheme, demonstrating strong absorption capacity and effective 
implementation. The recommendations of the Committee to make constant SW OT analysis 
for its organ institutions for better result are noted.   
 
 
1.3 Flagship Schemes 
 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
1.3.1 The Committee takes notes of the flagship schemes of the Department, successful 
implementation thereof ultimately rates the s uccess of the Department as a whole. The 
Committee, therefore, recommends that the Department should enhance the executing 
capacity of the implementing agency so that the absorbing capacity of the scheme is increased 
to the optimal level. This would ensure  the optimum utilisation of the allocated funds under 
the schemes which would enable the Department to seek the budgetary allocation as per the 
projected demand in the ensuing financial years. 
(Para 1.5.2 of the Report) 
Action Taken 
 
1.3.2 The budget provision and actual expenditure of some flagship schemes of the 
Department of Health and Family Welfare as enumerated in Para 1.5.1 is as under: -

1.3.3 It may be seen in above table that the actual expenditure during the financial year 
2024-25 was more than 100% in 2 main schemes. The higher allocation of funds has been 
kept in BE 2025-26 keeping in mind higher absorption capacity of funds under these schemes.  
 
1.3.4 Under PMSSY, the  budget is allocated with the objective of correcting regional 
imbalances in the availability of affordable/reliable tertiary healthcare services and augment 
facilities for quality medical education. The scheme has two compone nts, one is setting up of 
All India Institute of Medical Science Institute and secondly up -gradation of existing 
Government Medical Colleges/Institutes. So far, establishment of 22 new AIIMS and 75 up -
gradation Projects of existing Government Medical Colle ges/Institutions have been approved 
under the scheme.  Besides this, budget provision for establishment expenditure of 22 new 
AIIMS located at various places in different States has been provisioned  
 
1.3.5 Under PMJAYthe executing capacity of the implemen ting agencies has significantly 
strengthened, resulting in enhanced absorption and optimal utilization of the allocated funds 
under AB -PMJAY. Notably, most of the states have 100% of the implementation funds 
allocated, ensuring timely and effective utiliza tion. Rs. 6,637.46 crores was released to 
States/UTs as central share and total expenditure under this scheme was Rs 6982.79 crore 
including headquarter expenditure during financial year 2024-25 
 
1.3.6 Under PM-ABHIM, The Department makes financial support to all the States/UTs for 
the establishment of the Program Management Unit (PMUs) to provide support in enhancing 
the execution capacity and provide technical and administrative support to facilitate timely 
construction under the scheme. Training of HR No dal officials from all states are conducted 
regularly to enable States/UTs to undertake comprehensive planning, ensure efficient 
implementation of National Health Mission approvals and enhance absorbing capacity. 
Continuous review & monitoring has seen an increase in the utilization of funds. 
 
1.3.7 Under ABDM, the Department make 100% financial support for creating a National 
Digital Health Eco-System that supports universal health coverage in an efficient, accessible, 

4 
 
inclusive, affordable, timely and saf e manner through provision of a wide range of data, 
information and infrastructure services for ensuring the security, confidentiality and privacy 
of health-related personal information of the patient.  
  
1.3.8 The NHM is now being modded as a flagship sch eme and there is 100% Budget 
utilisation under this scheme, demonstrating strong absorption capacity and effective 
implementation. However, the recommendations of the Committee is acknowledged and 
noted for compliance. 
 
1.4 Staff Strength in the Department 
 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
1.4.1 The Committee acknowledges the increasing integration of technology in the health 
sector and emphasizes that modernizing healthcare services is essential for ensuring 
sustainable and affordable care. In this context, it is crucial for the workforce overseeing 
National health initiatives to actively engage with technological advancements. Therefore, the 
Committee recommends that the Ministry of Health and Family Welfare expand the use of 
technology and implement measure s to strengthen the digital proficiency of its officials and 
staff. 
(Para 1.6.3 of the Report) 
Action Taken 
 
1.4.2 The Department has already initiated steps to use technology and strengthen the digital 
proficiency of officials and staff by using e -office, VC meeting, Payment through PFMS and 
use of Government email etc.  To cater to the training needs of officials and staff, the 
Department had formulated and implemented the Annual Capacity Building Plan (ACBP) for 
the year 2024 -25 in consultation with all the Divisions of the Department. 13 numbers of 
courses related to digital technology and its use were included in ACBP 2024 -25. In the 
proposed ACBP for the year 2025 -26, there are 27 courses related to digital technology and 
its use being incorporated for  its employees to be taken up during the year.  Further, in iGOT 
platform in which all DoHFW employees are registered, many courses on new technology are 
available that officers take up voluntarily as part of their individual capacity building. 
1.5 Budgetary Allocation 
 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
1.5.1 The Department apprised the Committee that the budgetary allocation depends on the 
overall Government budgetary resources availability, the current demand by various 
Programme Divisions of the Department, availability of funds as per annual envelope of the 
schemes and pace of expenditure of the previous financial year. Ministry of Finance, 
accordingly, allocate ceiling of Budget Estimates. The Committee notes that a sum of Rs. 
95,957.87 crore has been allocated to the Department of Health and Family Welfare for 2025 -

5 
 
26, representing 1.89% of the total Union Budget estimate and 0.27% of GDP (at current 
prices). A review of past trends indicates that actual expenditure has often fallen short of the 
budgeted allocation, remaining at just 0.27% of GDP in both 2022-23 and 2023-24. 
(Para 2.1.3 of the Report) 
Action Taken 
 
1.5.2 The Budgetary Allocation of Rs. 95,957.87 crore for DoHFW for FY 2025 -26 central 
share and excludes those by the State Governments as matching share of schemes.  As per 
National Health Account Estimates, the Government Health Expenditure has increased from 
Rs. 1.30 lakh crore (2013 -14) to Rs. 4.34 lakh crore (2021 -22) which increased by 234%. 
From the year 2019 -20 onwards in comparison to  RE, the Department has been able to 
achieve expenditure percentage of more than 96%.  For the year 2023 -24 the expenditure 
percentage was 103.44% and for 2024 -25 the expenditure percentage was 100.83% 
(Provisional) of RE. Further, Department of Health and  Family Welfare (DoHFW) has taken 
up with States to prioritize allocation to health sector and enhance their health budget at least 
to 8% of total State budget. In addition, the 15 th Finance Commission for the grant period 
from 2020-21 to 2025 -26 has provi ded Rs. 70,051 crore Grants for health through the local 
Government. 
 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
1.5.3 The Committee notes the 10.83% increase in the Budget Estimate (BE) for 2025 -26 as 
compared to the Revised Estimate (RE) for 2024 -25, but the Committee expresses concern 
over trend of the health sector's budget allocation, as a percentage of GDP and the total Union 
Budget that has been consistently declining since 2022 -23. There is a need to accelerate the 
rate of improving healthcare to global standard and proportional to the rate of population 
growth. India being the most populous country, and given the inflationary pressures and the 
National Health Policy's target of increasing government health expenditure to 2.5% of GDP 
by 2025, the Committe e believes that the allocation to the health sector, particularly to the 
Department of Health and Family Welfare, should have been much higher. The Committee, 
therefore, urges the Department to take concrete steps to enhance the utilization of allocated 
funds effectively so that the targets set under a specific scheme are achieved. 
 
(Para 2.1.4 of the Report) 
Action Taken 
 
1.5.4 As per the National Health Accounts, there has been a steady increase in Government 
Health Expenditure, from 1.15% in 2013 -14 to 1.84% 2021-22 as a share of GDP. During the 
same period, Government Health Expenditure (GHE) as a share of Total Health Expenditure 
(THE) has significantly increased, from 28.6% in 2013 -14 to 48.0% in 2021 -22. The budget 
allocation for DoHFW has increased by 174% from Rs. 35,063 crore in 2013 -14 (BE) to Rs. 
95957.87 crore in 2025 -26 (BE).  The 15 th Finance Commission for the grant period from 
2020-21 to 2025 -26 has provided Rs. 70,051 crore Grants for health through the local 

6 
 
Government. Further, Department of Health and Family Welfare (DoHFW) has taken up with 
States to prioritize allocation to health sector and enhance their health budgets at least to 8% 
of total State budget. The Department has made all efforts for optimum utilization of allocated 
funds an d achieved expenditure of 100.83% with reference to RE allocation during the 
financial year 2024 -25. Thus, all efforts are being made to make full utilization of allocated 
funds. 
1.6 Budgetary Projection vis-a-vis Allocation and Expenditure 
 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
1.6.1 The Committee notes that, as of January 31, 2025, the Department had utilized 
81.32% of the total Revised Estimate, leaving 18.68% of the funds, approximately Rs16,000 
crores, available unspent for the remaining two months. The Minis try of Finance has 
repeatedly issued guidelines to regulate expenditure within a financial year. In its 143rd 
Report, the Committee recommended that the Department of Health and Family Welfare cap 
its expenditure in the final month of the financial year in  accordance with existing guidelines 
and closely monitor fund utilization during the last quarter. This is crucial to ensuring the 
prudent and efficient use of allocated funds. 
(Para 2.2.5 of the Report) 
Action Taken

1.6.3 The Department has achieved expenditure of 100.83% of Revised Estimate at the end

through strict monitoring of expenditure trends on daily basis. It was also ensured that the 
entire allocation should be optimally and efficiently utilized. 
 

7 
 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
1.6.4 Public health plays a crucial role in driving the country‘s economic development. 
Given the inadequacy and overstretched nature of public health infrastructure, investments in 
the health sector must be increased. With inflation expected to remain high in the near term, 
the Department should consider for requesting higher budgetary allocations for the health 
sector at the Revised Estimate stage and ensure the optimal utilization of allocated funds. 
(Para 2.2.6 of the Report) 
Action Taken 
 
1.6.5 The Department has agreed to the observations of the Committee.Public Health play a 
crucial role in driving the country‘s economic development. The budget allocation for the 
Department has been increased by 174% from Rs. 35,063 crore in 2013-14 (Budget Estimate) 
to Rs. 95,957.87 crore in 2025 -26 (Budget Estimate).  Further, the 15 th Finance Commission 
for the grant period from 2020 -21 to 2025-26 has provided Rs. 70,051 crore Grant for health 
through the local Government. With the long- term goal to better prepare against public health 
emergencies, Pradhan Mantri – Ayushman Bharat Health Infrastructure Mission (PM -
ABHIM) has been launched with the intent to enhance the capacity of primary, secondary and 
tertiary health care facilities/systems and institutes for identifying and managing any new and 
emerging diseases.  PM -ABHIM is Centrally Sponsored Scheme with some Central Sector 
component with a total outlay of Rs. 64,180 crore from 2021 -22 to 2025-26. The Department 
has achieved expenditure target of 103.44% and 100.83% with reference to Revised Estimates 
2023-24 and 2024-25 respectively.  Thus, optimum utilization of available resources is being 
done.  The allocation of 2025 -26 is Rs. 95,957.87 crore which is 9.47% increase  over Budget 
Estimate 2024 -25.  It is also pertinent to mention that Government of India allocation to 
Ministries/Departments are done on the basis of approved scheme envelops considering pace 
of expenditure of pervious financial years and demands from the  Implementing agencies and 
State/UT Governments.  It is also mentioned that the period of 15 th Finance Commission 
award is ending in 2025 -26 after which a revised allocation for all schemes is proposed, after 
third party audit.  However, the suggestion of C ommittee for requesting Ministry of Finance 
for higher budgetary allocation for health sector at RE stage is noted. 
1.7 Government Health Expenditure in India 
 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
1.7.1 National Health Policy (NHP), 2017 prescribed increasing Government Health 
Expenditure (GHE) as a share of GDP to 2.5% by 2025. Following the objective of NHP, 
2017, GHE, as a share of GDP, has increased from 1.35% in 2017 -18 to 1.84% in 2021 -22, 
indicating progress in health sector investment. The Committee notes that the upward trend in 
GHE is positive; however, further acceleration is required to meet the 2.5% GDP target by 
2025. The current pace of increase may not be sufficient to achieve this goal wit hin the 
remaining timeframe. A strategic focus on public health infrastructure, preventive care, and 

8 
 
efficient resource utilization will be crucial in ensuring long -term improvements in healthcare 
accessibility and quality. 
(Para 2.3.3 of the Report) 
 
Action Taken 
 
1.7.2 The Department of Health and Family Welfare has taken up the matter with States to 
prioritize allocation to health sector and enhance their health budgets at least to 8% of total 
State budget. The budget allocation for DoHFW has increased b y 102.64% from Rs. 47,353 
crore in 2017 -18 (Budget Estimate) to Rs. 95,957.87 crore in 2025 -26 (Budget Estimate). 
Further, the 15 th Finance Commission for the grant period from 2020 -21 to 2025 -26 has 
provided Rs. 70,051 crore Grants for health through the local Government.  The Central 
Government has taken several initiatives for supplementing the efforts of the States for 
providing quality and affordable healthcare services to the people. Under National Health 
Mission, the Government has taken many steps to wards universal health coverage, by 
supporting the State Governments in providing accessible & affordable healthcare to people.  
1.7.3 Further, the Government has launched four mission mode projects, namely PM -
Ayushman Bharat Health Infrastructure Mission (PM-ABHIM), Ayushman Arogya Mandir 
(AAMs), Pradhan Mantri Jan Arogya Yojana (PMJAY) and Ayushman Bharat Digital 
Mission (ABDM). Other notable initiatives are setting up of new AIIMS, upgrading of 
Government medical colleges under the Pradhan Mantri Swasthy a Suraksha Yojana 
(PMSSY), support to States/UTs for setting up of new medical colleges as well as for 
increasing UG and PG medical seats, provision of free drugs and free diagnostic facilities at 
Primary Health Centres (PHC)/ Community Health Centres (CHC ) and district hospital level, 
substantial increase in availability of government ambulances etc. 
1.7.4 The Government has taken several initiatives, which are expected to increase the 
Government Health Expenditure (GHE). The majority of these funding‘s are for strengthening 
infrastructure, which will have a long -term impact, and improving service delivery, thus 
improving healthcare accessibility and quality of care. Some of the important initiatives are: - 
(i) Free Drugs Service Initiatives. 
(ii) Free Diagnostics Service Initiatives 
(iii) Pradhan Mantri National Dialysis Program. 
(iv) National Ambulance Services under NHM. 
(v) Making health facilities IPHS compliant and NQAS Certified to provide quality 
healthcare to patients. 
1.7.5 Other on -going National Health programs under NHM like NTEP, NVBDCP, NP -
NCD, RMNCAH+N related programs (JSY, JSSK, IHIP, etc.). 
 

9 
 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
1.7.6 A close scrutiny of the Health Budget reflects the lack of priority assigned to health. 
The Committee observes that the declining trend in health budget allocation in terms of 
Budget Estimates (BE) as % of GDP (at Current Price) between 2020 -21 and 2024 -25 (refer 
to Table 2.3 above) do not align with the target set in National Health Policy. The Committee 
is of the opinion that adequate health financing forms the pillar of a well -functioning health 
system which is crucial for reduction of Out -of-Pocket Expenditure (OOPE) in health. The 
pandemic further reaffirmed the need of a sustainable health financing ecosystem especially 
in tackling a sudden public health crisis. The Committee recommends the Government to 
prioritize Health Sector in Budget Allocation by  increasing the annual budget allocation for 
healthcare to maintain the momentum and ensure sufficient resources for infrastructure, 
medical research, and service delivery. 
(Para 2.3.4 of the Report) 
Action Taken 
 
1.7.7 The figure titled ―Trends in Budgeta ry Allocation and Expenditure,‖ referred to as 
Table 2.3, includes estimates solely of the Department of Health and Family Welfare   and 
excludes those of the Department of Health Research (DHR) and budgetary allocations made 
by the State Governments as pa rt of their sharing ratio in schemes.  The budget allocation for 
DoHFW has increased by 174% from Rs. 35,063 crore in 2013 -14 (Budget Estimate) to Rs. 
95,957.87 crore in 2025-26 (Budget Estimate). As per National Health Account Estimates, the 
Government health expenditure has increased from Rs. 1.30 lakh crore (2013 -14) to Rs. 4.34 
lakh crore (2021 -22) which increased by 234%. Further, Department of Health and Family 
Welfare   has taken up with States to prioritize allocation to health sector and enhance the ir 
health budgets at least to 8% of total State budget. Further, the 15 th Finance Commission for 
the grant period from 2020 -21 to 2025 -26 has provided Rs. 70,051 crore Grants for health 
through the local Government. Thus, efforts are being made to prioriti ze resource allocation 
for healthcare. 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
1.7.8 The Committee, in its 126 th Report on DFG (2022 -23), had also recommended the 
Government for increasing its health expenditure to 5% of GDP by 2025. However, with such 
stagnant allocation to health and the slow pace of increase in allocation, the goal of achieving 
the NHP target of health expenditure to 2.5% seems a distant dream. 
 
(Para 2.3.5 of the Report) 
 
Action Taken 
 
1.7.9 As per the National Health Accounts, there has been a steady increase in Government 
Health Expenditure, from 1.15% in 2013 -14 to 1.84% 2021 -22 as a share of GDP.During the 

10 
 
same period, Government Health Expenditure (GHE) as a share of Total Health Expenditure 
(THE) has significantly increased, which was 29.0% in 2014 -15 and 48.0% in 2021 -22. The 
Government Health Expenditure (GHE) has increased from Rs. 1.30 l akh crore (2013 -14) to 
Rs. 4.34 lakh crore (2021 -22) which is an increase of 234%.  The Government Health 
Expenditure (GHE) as a percentage of General Government Expenditure (GGE) has increased 
from 3.78% (2013-14) to 6.12% (2021 -22). Thus there has been a steady increase in keeping 
in mind the initiatives of the Department and the overall resources availability of the 
government. 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
1.7.10 The Committee is of the view that strengthening of public health infrastructure, 
enhancing preventive and primary care services, leveraging Public -Private Partnerships 
(PPP), exploring additional revenue sources,  innovation in health, robust monitoring and 
evaluation mechanism to track the utilization of health funds and assess the impact  are crucial 
for establishing an affordable and accessible healthcare delivery system, especially for a 
developing economy like India. The need of the hour is to recognize health as a key sector for 
economic as well as social development. The Committee, therefore , strongly recommends the 
Government to make health a priority and increase its investment in the health sector.   
 
(Para 2.3.6 of the Report) 
Action Taken 
 
1.7.11 Department of Health and Family Welfare is initiating steps to use ‗Public Private 
Partnership model‘ for augmenting the health infrastructure in the country and for improving 
the delivery of health services. Towards this end, recently a dedicated PPP Cell has been 
created in the Department.  The PPP Cell is working with different Program  Divisions and 
State Governments to identify projects, which can be taken up in the PPP mode. The 
Department has written to the States/UTs to utilize schemes of the Department of Economic 
Affairs to sponsor PPP projects and to consider setting up a dedicated PPP Cell in their Health 
Departments to ensure focus on this matter and to help create an enabling environment for 
PPP mode of Infrastructure in the State/UT.‖ Regular monitoring of utilization of health funds 
is being done through use of different digi tal platform/portals. Regular interactions are held 
with different stakeholders (States) to assess implementation of schemes and do optimum 
utilization of available resources. The main objective of the National Health Policy 2017 is to 
improve health statu s through concerted policy action in all sectors and expand preventive, 
promotive, curative, palliative and rehabilitative services provided through the public health 
sector with focus on quality. 
 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
1.7.12 The Committee unders tands that increasing the public investment on health depends 
on several factors such as State Government‘s health budget depending on their allocations, 

11 
 
capacities and fund position, transfer to states on the recommendation on Finance 
Commission, allocati on made under Centrally Sponsored Schemes by Union Government 
based on total budgetary resources. The Committee appreciates that the 25 States and the 
Union Territories of Delhi and Puducherry as per the NHA Estimates for FY 2021 -22 have 
achieved the Natio nal Health Policy target of 8% of their budget allocated to health . The 
Committee recommends that other State/UT Governments should follow the same.  
 
(Para 2.3.13 of the Report) 
Action Taken 
 
1.7.13 Some of the initiatives undertaken to encourage the States to allocate a higher share of 
their budget to the health sector: - 
(i) Policy-based intervention: The National Health Policy 2017 has recommended 
increasing State health spending to more than 8% of their budget. 
(ii) Incentivizing states to spend more: The GoI adopted a conditionality framework under 
the National Health Mission. The framework follows Result Based Financing (RBF) 
where the States get more budgetary support if they perform well on the agreed 
indicators. 
(iii) Indian Public Health Standards (IPHS) 2022: These standards enable service provision 
of quality services through assured infrastructure, human resources, diagnostics, 
equipment, medicines etc. 
(iv) National Quality Assurance Standards (NQAS): With the commitment of GoI to 
achieve NQAS certification o f all facilities by Dec 2026, States are encouraged to 
expedite their development and adoption of standards to provide quality care to all. 
Other initiatives: -  
(v) It is agreed that higher budgetary allocations are essential to bolster  healthcare 
infrastructure and service delivery mechanisms across the country. 
(vi) Proposals for additional funding through the 16th Finance Commission are also being 
prepared by the MoHFW. 
 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
1.7.14 The Committee, in its 134 th Report, had highlighted the existing wide inter -State and 
intra-State disparity in healthcare infrastructure across the country. The Committee observes 
that States with poor financial resources and inefficient fiscal capacity face umpteen 
challenges in es tablishing a robust health infrastructure. The Committee, accordingly, 
reiterates its recommendation that the Union Government must persuade the remaining 

12 
 
State/UT Governments to achieve the prescribed budgetary health expenditure to 8% of State 
General Government Expenditure. 
(Para 2.3.14 of the Report) 
Action Taken 
 
1.7.15 The Government has undertaken the following initiatives to encourage the states to 
allocate a higher share of their budget to the health sector: -  
(i) The Department of Health and Family Welfare (DoHFW) has taken up with States to 
prioritize allocation to health sector and enhance their health budgets at least to 8% of 
total State budget. 
(ii) The GoI adopted a conditionality framework under the National Health Mission. The 
framework follows Result Based Financing ( RBF) where the States get more 
budgetary support if they perform well on the agreed indicators. 
(iii) Further, under National Health Mission, the Government has taken many steps 
towards universal health coverage, by supporting the State Gov ernments in providing 
accessible & affordable healthcare to people. The National Health Mission provides 
financial and technical support for improvement in health infrastructure, availability of 
adequate human resources to man health facilities, to improve  availability and 
accessibility to quality health care especially for the underserved and marginalized 
groups in rural areas. National Free Drugs Service Initiative and National Free 
Diagnostic Service has been rolled out to ensure the availability of esse ntial drugs and 
diagnostic facilities and reduce the OOPE of the patients visiting the public health 
facilities. To provide quality health care services especially for the underserved and 
marginalized groups in rural and undeserved areas, various initiativ es carried out 
under NHM by the Government in the country are operationalization of Ayushman 
Arogya Mandirs, support for National Ambulance Services, Mobile Medical Units, 
ASHAs, 24 x 7 Services and First Referral Facilities, Prime Minister's National 
Dialysis Programme, various activities under Reproductive &Universal Immunization 
programme. 
 
1.8 Central Government Health Scheme (CGHS) 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
1.8.1 The Committee acknowledges the Government's efforts to digitize healthcare 
resources through the Healthcare Management Information System (HMIS), establish new 
Wellness Centres in tier -II cities, and promote Ayush Centres. However, the examination of 
budget allocation and utilization under Revenue Head reflects a significant gap between the 
RE allocation of Rs. 2245.82 crore for CGHS and the actual expenditure of Rs. 1842.51 crore, 
representing only 82% utilization as of February 7, 2025. The Committee, the refore, 
recommends that the Department conducts a thorough and immediate review of the CGHS 
expenditure process, specifically focusing on identifying and addressing the bottlenecks 

13 
 
causing the underutilization of allocated funds. By streamlining administra tive procedures, 
improving procurement processes, and ensuring timely disbursement of funds, the 
Department can ensure that the allocated resources are effectively used to enhance healthcare 
services for beneficiaries, thereby maintaining the intended cont inuity between budget 
allocation and service delivery. 
(Para 3.1.4 of the Report) 
 
Action Taken 
1.8.2 The utilization of budget up to February 2025 was less because various orders that 
were placed under GeM Portal were not delivered till that time and also  bills for various 
projects were not yet settled. By the end of FY 2024 -25 out of the allocated Revised 
Estimates (RE) of Rs. 2245.82 crore under Revenue head, expenditure was Rs. 2079.95 crores 
i.e. 92.61% at the end of financial year 2024-25. 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
1.8.3 In its 155 th Report, the Committee observed that, as of August 2023, 26% of the 340 
allopathic wellness centres (spread across 80 cities) were concentrated in the Delhi NCR 
region. Additionally, only six states (excluding Delhi NCR) have more than ten CGHS 
centres, while Arunachal Pradesh and the Union Territories of Ladakh, Andaman & Nicobar 
Islands, and Lakshadweep have none. The Committee noted that CGHS services are unevenly 
distributed, limiting accessibility in many regions. The Committee, therefore, wishes to 
highlight that numerous cities and towns with a significant population of Central Government 
employees, pensioners, and their dependents lack CGHS wellness centres. As a result, 
beneficiaries—particularly retirees —have to travel long distances for medical tr eatment, 
causing financial strain and other hardships. To address this, the Committee urges the 
Ministry to proactively advocate for new CGHS centres in underserved areas, particularly in 
suburban regions with a substantial number of serving and retired be neficiaries. The 
Committee recommends that the Ministry engage with the Ministry of Finance for speedy 
approval of new CGHS Wellness Centres. Moreover, the Committee suggests that the 
Government should consider revising existing norms or introducing new on es to facilitate the 
establishment of wellness centres in rural, hilly, remote, and Northeastern regions. 
 
(Para 3.1.5 of the Report) 
 
Action Taken 
1.8.4 Approximately 29% of CGHS Wellness Centres are concentrated in Delhi NCR (i.e. 
99 out of 342) because out of total registered 47,43,192 beneficiaries across India, majority 
lies in Delhi NCR (i.e. 20,38,394) which is approximately 43% of the total beneficiaries.  
Moreover, the proposal to open 22 new Wellness Centres is also approved by this Ministry 
and is currently under discussion with Ministry of Finance. 
 
 

14 
 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
1.8.5 The Committee wishes to express an observation regarding queue -management of 
patients seeking treatment in the CGHS dispensaries. It has been seen that in some of the 
private/public establishments, the queue of patients is managed through electronic token 
system displaying the queue number of the patient with corresponding token slip with the 
patient. The Committee would like the Department to establish electronic t oken system in all 
dispensaries of CGHS displaying the name and queue number of the patient to facilitate better 
queue management of patients in order to avoid high patient load at the doctors‘ cabin and 
give convenience to critical patients waiting for their turn.  
 
(Para 3.1.15 of the Report) 
 
Action Taken 
1.8.6 The Queue management system (electronic token system) is installed in some of the 
CGHS centres. Instructions has been passed on to all the CGHS units to get the queue 
management system (electronic  token system) installed at the earliest. Moreover, in the new 
mobile software application, CGHS has introduced Queue Display in my CGHS app for 
convenience of CGHS beneficiaries. 
1.9 Safdarjung Hospital (SJH) & Vardhman Mahavir Medical College (VMMC) 
 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
1.9.1 The Committee takes note of delay in the construction of the lecture theatre and boys' 
hostel of the SJH and VMMC Institute, where tree transplantation work has been kept on hold 
in compliance of directions of the Hon‘b le High Court of Delhi. To address this issue, the 
Committee desires that the Department promptly pursue the matter with court and take 
necessary steps to resolve the litigation on priority. This will help prevent any adverse impact 
on project timelines. A dditionally, the Committee suggests that a review of such legal 
challenges be incorporated into policy -making processes to mitigate similar obstacles in 
future projects. 
 
(Para 3.2.4 of the Report) 
Action Taken 
 
1.9.2 The recent judgement of the Hon'ble Supreme Court of India in W.P. (Civil) No (s). 
4677/1985: M.C. Mehta Vs. Union of India & Ors: the apex court has directed the Tree 
Officer/Forest Dept. to take approval from the Central Empowered Committee (CEC) before 
granting permission for felling of 5 0 or more trees, Since, the total affected trees in the 
proposed auditorium site of V.M.M.C. are 60, the case has been taken up with CEC by the 
Forest Dept. of Govt. of NCT of Delhi for their approval. 
 

15 
 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
1.9.3 The Committee ac knowledges the increase in capital outlay from Rs119.15 crore in 
RE 2024-25 to Rs238.50 crore in BE 2025 -26. However, it notes that the shortfall in capital 
allocation during FY 2024 -25 adversely impacted the progress of key infrastructure projects, 
including the construction of the MCH block, Auditorium, Boys' Hostel at VMMC, Dwaraka 
Housing Project, Raj Nagar Staff Quarters, and Dharamshala Project. The Committee urges 
the Department, subject to the progress of pending court cases, to enhance the capital outlay 
further at RE stage for timely completion of all infrastructure projects in the FY 2025-26. 
 
(Para 3.2.5 of the Report) 
Action Taken 
1.9.4 Hospital is in follow up with Forest Department to expedite the approval for CEC for 
other relevant project. As soon as it will be done, allotment of additional funds at RE stage 
will be requested. 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
1.9.5 Given that the Dwaraka Housing Project has achieved 24% physical progress as of 
February 2025, with a tentative completion date of 31.03.2026, the Committee urges the 
Government to ensure the timely completion of all these projects. If additional funds are  
required, they should be provided at the RE stage to prevent further delays. Timely 
completion is essential to enhance facilities and improve the overall functioning of the 
Institute. The Committee in its 134 th and 143rd Reports had flagged t he issues related to slow 
pace of construction activities and recommended the Department to adhere to the timeline of 
27 months for completion of the Dwarka Project. 
(Para 3.2.6 of the Report) 
Action Taken 
1.9.6 Safdarjung Hospital is in constant discussions and follows up meeting with CPWD 
including site visit by Senior Officers to expedite the timely completion of the projects.

ABVIMS in BE 2025-26, particularly the 44.84% rise in capital outlay, which is essential for

medical facilities to global standards. The Committee, however, expresses concern over the 
trend of fund under -utilization. In FY 2023 -24, RML and ABVIMS was allocated BE 
amounting to Rs. 1272.18 crore which was increased to Rs. 1304.10 at RE st age, the actual 
expenditure was Rs. 1195.29 crore. Hence, despite an increased allocation at the RE stage, 
actual expenditure fell short by Rs108.81 crore. Similarly, in FY 2024 -25, the allocated 
budget of Rs. 1610 crore at BE stage was significantly reduc ed to Rs. 1220 crore at the RE 

16 
 
stage, particularly in capital outlay the decrease was by 58% (from Rs. 558.17 at BE stage to

estimate. This pattern highlights inefficienc ies in fund utilization, particularly under the 
capital head. Given the hospital's critical role in serving patients from both Delhi and other 
regions, the Committee underscores the urgent need for optimal fund utilization. 
(Para 3.3.3 of the Report)

dispute between CPWD and RAMACIVIL is still continuing.  Ho wever, CPWD has 
assured to procure the furniture as per Dr. RML Hospital‘s demand. 
(xxiii) Finishing work - delayed due to less deployment of labour, agency has been directed 
to increase the labour.

By implementing these measures, the Institute can improve efficiency, avoid further delays, 
and ensure timely project completion.

1.11 Lady Hardinge Medical College (LHMC) & Smt. S. K. Hospital 
 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
1.11.1 While examining the Demands for Grants for 2025 -26, the Committee observes that 
the total budgetary allocation of Rs. 750 crore at the Budget Estimate (BE) stage for 2024 -25, 
which included a capital outlay of Rs. 101 crore, was reduced to Rs. 630 crore at the Revised 
Estimate (RE) stage, with the capital outlay declining to Rs. 51.50 crore. When asked about 
the reasons for this variation, the Department attributed it to delays in obtaining app rovals 
from the competent authority in the early part of the financial year. The Committee is 
particularly concerned that the capital outlay was reduced by 49% at the RE stage in 2024 -25. 
To address this issue, the Committee recommends that the Department should streamline the 
approval process and establish a Standard Operating Procedure (SOP) to ensure efficiency 
and reduce delays. Additionally, monitoring of fund utilization and evaluation of 
infrastructure projects should be strengthened to enhance effective implementation 
(Para 3.4.2 of the Report) 
Action Taken 
 
1.11.2 The LHMC has gone through the proposal of the procurement which were under 
pipeline during Financial Year 2024 -25 & noted that the reasons for low pace of expenditure 
were as under:  
(i) In the beginning of this FY, there were sufficient number of proposals worth Rs. 50.00 
Cr, however, due to the implementation of Code of Conduct during Lok Sabha Elections 
2024, LHMC was directed not to place any bid for publishing leading to a significant 
delay of 02 months till the results of elections were not declared. 
(ii) The directions were received to incorporate the Integrity Pact in the bidding process 
along with 02 Independent External Monitors (IEMs). The IEMs were finally appointed 
during the last quarter of the Financial Year 2024-25. 
(iii) The GeM Portal was under Maintenance for 10 days. 
(iv) Custom Bid Opening period of 21 days also causing significant delays in the 
procurement. 
(v) Too long resolution period of GeM Helpdesk. 
 
1.11.3 The Hospital has noted for com pliance the observations of the Committee for 
strengthening the monitoring of funds utilization and evaluation of infrastructure projects to 
enhance effective implementation.

1.13 Pradhan Mantri Swasthaya Suraksha Yojana (PMSSY) 
 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
1.13.1 The Committee expresses concern over the significant reduction in the PMSSY 
Scheme's budget allocation from Rs. 2200 crore in Budget Estimates (BE) 2024 -25 to Rs. 
1736 crore in Revised Estimates (RE) 2024 -25. While noting the actual expenditure of Rs. 
1552.36 crore as of 31.01.2025, indicating 89.42% of the RE allocation and 70.56% of the 
BE, the Committee emphasizes the importance of consistent and adequate funding for the 
effective implementation of the PMSSY Scheme. The Committee, therefore, recommends t he 
Ministry to ensure the judicious and optimal utilization of the remaining allocated funds by 

22 
 
the end of the financial year. The Ministry should investigate and implement processes to 
increase the efficiency of spending, to ensure the maximum benefit fro m the allocated funds 
towards achievement of set targets/objectives/goals under PMSSY. 
(Para 3.6.3 of the Report) 
 
1.13.2 Recognizing the allocation of Rs. 2200 crore (Rs. 60 crore Revenue and Rs. 2140 
crore Capital) in BE 2025 -26, the Committee urges upon  the Ministry to even utilization of 
funds throughout the financial year, thereby avoiding significant reductions at the RE stage, 
unless absolutely necessary and supported by compelling reasons/justifications. The efficient 
utilization of funds is indispe nsable to ensure the effective implementation of the PMSSY 
Scheme and achieve its intended healthcare objectives. 
(Para 3.6.4 of the Report) 
Action Taken 
 
1.13.3 The budget allocation for PMSSY scheme was required to be reduced at the RE 2024 -
25 stage due to the following reasons: - 
i) Delay in finalization of land allotment by the State Govt. for AIIMS Darbhanga. 
ii) Delay in start of the AIIMS Madurai project due to delay in the grant of environment 
clearance from the State Govt. 
1.13.4 For AIIMS Darbhanga, the State Government had initially approved transfer of 200.02 
acre land in Darbhanga Medical College & Hospital (DMCH) campus. However, in March 
2023, the said allotment was cancelled by the State Government and an alternate e -site 
(measuring 187.44 acre) sit uated on Ekmi Shobhan Bypass, Anchal -Bahadurpur, Mouza -
Balia, Thana No. 120/2 was offered for establishment of AIIMS Darbhanga. A Central 
(Technical) Team was thereafter, constituted to assess the feasibility and suitability of the 
proposed site. Based on this recommendation, the land at Ekmi Shoban Bypass was 
transferred by the State Government in September 2024 to MoHFW. Thereafter the work of 
conducting Geotechnical investigations, Hydrological Study etc. was entrusted by the 
Ministry to IIT Delhi on 13. 09.2024. On 18.09.2024, M/S HSCC (India) Ltd. was appointed 
the Executing Agency (EA) for Pre -Investment Works and Main Works. Subsequently, a 
MoU was signed on 12.11.2024 between MoHFW and State Government of Bihar. In 
December 2024, Architectural & Desig n Consultant for the project was finalized by M/S 
HSCC. Further, the Executing Agency awarded the work or the construction of boundary 
wall of AIIMS Darbhanga on 16.04.2025.  
1.13.5 The Construction for AIIMS Madurai has now commenced after grant of 
environmental clearance from the State Environment Impact Assessment Authority (SEIAA), 
Tamil Nadu. Currently approx. 20% of the project work has been completed.  Against the RE 
of Rs. 1,736 crore for 2024 -25, a total of Rs. 1,680.52 crore i.e. approx. 97% of RE has been 
utilized. The Ministry expects that the construction activities in ongoing projects would 

23 
 
considerably step up in 2025 -26, and resultantly the funds allocated under Budget Estimate 
2025-26 would substantially be utilized. 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
1.13.6 The Committee recommends that the Ministry of Health and Family Welfare, in 
collaboration with state governments, establish a standardized and expedited process for land 
acquisition and obtaining statutory clearances for AIIMS projects. This should include 
formulating clear guidelines and timelines for state governments regarding the provision of 
providing encumbrance -free land; creating a fast -track mechanism for obtaining 
environmental and other necessary clearances; and establishing a dedic ated coordination cell 
to address site -related hindrances. Delays in land acquisition and statutory clearances are 
significant bottlenecks in the timely completion of AIIMS projects. As evident by the 
situations in Darbhanga, Madurai, and Rewari, these delays lead to cost escalations and hinder 
the delivery of essential healthcare services. 
 
(Para 3.6.9 of the Report) 
Action Taken 
 
1.13.7 For setting up of new AIIMS, a MoU with the respective Sate Government is signed 
by the Ministry wherein State Governmen t commits to provide encumbrance free site with 
four lane road connectivity, water and electricity supply adequate to cater to requirement of 
fully functional AIIMS.  Through such MoU, the State Government also commits to make 
adequate provision for disposal of STP, ETP waste and storm water drain beyond the limits of 
AIIMS campus. In this context, it is further to state that to set up an AIIMS, a very large land 
parcel (approx. 200 acres) is required. Given the scale of the land requirement invariably both 
natural hindrances, like tree cover, water body, undulating rock outcrops as well as manmade 
hindrances like nallah, pipelines, high tension lines, religious structures etc. are encountered. 
Availability of encumbrance free land and securing all statutory clearances is critical before 
start of any project of this scale. The Ministry, therefore, actively coordinates with the 
concerned State government authorities for timely resolution of a land related issue and early 
grant of statutory clearances.  Project r eview meetings are held by senior officers of the 
Ministry with the State Governments, Executing Agencies, etc. for addressing the site -related 
hindrances and expediting statutory clearances. 
 
1.13.8 The issues affecting progress of the projects are also t aken up with the State 
Government through letters from Secretary (HFW) to the Chief Secretary of the concerned 
State Government.  Such issues are also taken up the State Government through Project 
Monitoring Group (PMG) being chaired by Secretary (Coordinat ion) in the Cabinet 
Secretariat. Thus, all efforts at every level are taken for early resolution of land related issues 
and statutory clearances for timely implementation of the projects. 
 
 
 

24 
 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
1.13.9 The Committee takes into account various challenges confronting the Department that 
result into delay in implementation of projects and strategy measures chalked out to combat 
those challenges for effective utilization of funds earmarked for various institutions under 
PMSSY and ti mely completion of projects though the Committee understands that certain 
challenges are really a Himalayan barrier, the resolution of which is of course herculean task. 
Despite that the Committee believes that the Pro -Active Governance and Timely 
Implementation (PRAGATI) Portal, reviewed by the Prime Minister for expeditious 
resolution of various issues like land acquisition, utility shifting, contains the strength of 
mount-melting capacity with quick and effective decision -making for speedy resolution of all 
vexing issues/challenges. The Committee feels that the two components of PMSSY i.e. 
setting up of new AIIMS and up -gradation of Government Medical College/Institutions 
equipped with basic infrastructural facilities and equipment‘s contain capacity meas ures for 
correcting regional imbalance in the availability of affordable/reliable tertiary healthcare 
services and to augment facilities for quality medical education in the country. The 
Committee, therefore, recommends the Government for identifying more GMCs (beyond 
already identified 75 GMCs) and setting up of more AIIMS -like institutions as requests 
received by the Department from Karnataka, Kerala, Goa, Tripura, Sikkim, Mizoram, 
Manipur, Uttar Pradesh, Bihar (in Kosi region) and Maharashtra in the next  phase of PMSSY 
keeping in view the density of population, distance from existing AIIMS and regions from 
where patients inflow to AIIMS, New Delhi for treatment, increasing its workload and 
pushing up the waiting time even for critical patients. 
 
(Para 3.6.16 of the Report) 
Action Taken 
 
1.13.10  The present phase of PMSSY scheme is up to 2025 -26. The recommendations of the 
Committee would be duly considered during the process of extension of the scheme beyond 
2025-26. 
 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
1.13.11  The Committee recommends that the Ministry further strengthen its monitoring and 
coordination mechanisms for PMSSY projects, including both AIIMS and GMC upgrades. 
This should involve enhancing the effectiveness of Project Review Committees and ut ilizing 
the PRAGATI portal for timely issue resolution; conducting regular site visits by senior 
ministry officials to monitor progress and address challenges; improving coordination with 
state governments and executing agencies to ensure seamless implementation. The Committee 
considers that effective monitoring and coordination are essential for ensuring the timely 
completion of PMSSY projects and the efficient utilization of funds. Strengthening these 
mechanisms will help mitigate delays and improve overall project outcomes. 
(Para 3.6.18 of the Report) 
 

25 
 
Action Taken 
 
1.13.12  In order to ensure regular review of the projects and their timely completion and 
operationalization the following mechanisms have been put in place by the Ministry: - 
(i) In accordance with Rule 141 of General Financial rules, 2017, Project Review 
Committees under the chairpersonship of Joint Secretary (PMSSY) has been set for 
each ongoing project of setting up of AIIMS. 
(ii) Further, progress of projects is reviewed regularly in the Ministry  by Secretary HFW 
with the Executing Agencies and Procurement Support Agency to ensure timely 
completion of projects. 
(iii) The Project Monitoring Group (PMG), in the Cabinet Secretariat is another institutional 
mechanism for milestone -based monitoring of projec ts and expending issue resolution 
and regulatory bottlenecks in projects with investments upward of Rs. 500 crore. It 
ensures proactive monitoring of projects and in time resolution of issues with the State 
Government and other stakeholders to prevent dela ys and cost overruns. PMG has been 
helpful in fast -tracking of approvals, sectoral policy issues and removal of bottlenecks 
for expediting project commissioning.  
(iv) Pro-Active Governance and Timely Implementation (PRAGATI) is a multi -purpose 
and multi -modal platform for simultaneously monitoring and reviewing important 
programmes and projects of the Central Government as well as projects flagged by 
State Governments. Through PRAGATI the progress of AIIMS has been reviewed 
resulting in quick and effective deci sion making and swift resolution of issues with the 
State Governments. 
(v) Further for each project Third Party Quality Assurance (TPQA) Agencies including 
RITES/IIT Roorkee etc. have been appointed for independent periodical check on 
quality of construction, technical audit of the records, bills raised, testing of 
construction materials from approved independent Laboratories etc. If any deviations 
are found they are highlighted for rectification and the payment of consultancy to the 
Executing Agencies is made after duly considering the observations and compliance of 
the TQPA. 
1.13.13  In addition to the aforesaid institutional arrangements, the issues affecting progress 
of the projects are also taken up with the State Governments by the Secretary (HFW) with the  
chief Secretary of the concerned State Government. Need based directions are also given to 
Institutes/Executive Agencies/PSA for expeditious resolution of the issues between them.  
Further, site visits by senior officers of the Ministry are also undertaken  to comprehensively 
monitor all aspects of project execution, including civil construction, procurement & 
installation of equipment, accreditation, recruitment etc. 
 
 
 
 
 
 

26 
 
1.14 National AIDS and STD Control Programme (NACP) 
 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
1.14.1 The NACP program highlights the importance of integrated health campaigns among 
high-risk and bridge populations for early HIV identification. The Committee recommends 
the Department to increase the frequency and scope of integrated health campaigns  in select 
high-risk districts, focusing on early HIV detection and STI/RTI management; the 
strengthening of link worker schemes by providing enhanced training and resources, ensuring 
they can effectively reach and engage with high -risk populations; increase in the usage of the 
SOCH MIS system to better track the beneficiaries and services offered to them; and to 
complete and utilize the data from the round 2 of programmatic mapping and population size 
estimation to refine targeted interventions. 
(Para 3.7.7 of the Report) 
 
Action Taken 
 
1.14.2 The recommendation of the Committee has been noted. Under the NACP, Integrated 
Health Campaigns have been made a significant part of Annual Action Plan 2025 -26 for all 
SACS to go for targeted campaign. Training of staff of SACS is being imparted periodically 
for improving usage of SOCH portal and beneficiary tracking. A dedicated IT Helpdesk is 
operational to resolve queries in SOCH usage. The national training of trainers for 
implementation of round 2 of programmatic mapping and population size estimation has been 
completed. 
 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
1.14.3 The Committee highlights that NACP emphasizes index testing campaigns for 
partners and biological children of HIV -positive persons and Lost -to-follow-up tr acking 
campaigns are crucial to ensure continuity of treatment. The Committee, therefore, 
recommends the Department for institutionalizing and expanding index testing campaigns, 
providing incentives and support to HIV -positive individuals to encourage part ner 
notification. A robust, technology -driven lost-to-follow-up tracking system, utilizing mobile 
technology and community health workers to reconnect individuals with treatment services 
should be implemented to achieve the objective of the Programme.   
 
(Para 3.7.8 of the Report) 
Action Taken 
 
1.14.4 The recommendation of the Committee has been noted. NACP Programme continues 
to focus on index testing of sexual partners, biological children and spouses of HIV -positive 
persons through effective outreach, counselling and follow up.  Web -based mobile apps  are 
created on SOCH to track and monitor the same. Care and support Centres (CSC) have been 
established to provide peer -counselling and outreach services to PLHIV and create and 

27 
 
enabling environment for their long -term retention in care. For seamless real -time tracking of 
lost-to-follow-up cases, a Mobile Application has been developed in SOCH portal. 
 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
1.14.5 The Committee takes into consideration the Intensify and Tailor IEC Campaigns and 
Community Engagement to Reduce Stigma and Discrimination and Increase Service Uptake. 
NACP recognizes the importance of 360 -degree multimedia campaigns and community 
outreach with reducing stigma and discrimination as a key objective. The Committee, 
therefore, recommends developing cult urally sensitive IEC campaigns that address local 
stigma and misconceptions about HIV/AIDS; expanding community engagement through 
partnerships with local NGOs, community leaders, and faith -based organizations; and 
providing targeted training and sensitiza tion programs for key stakeholders, including Self -
Help Groups, Anganwadi workers, ASHAs, etc. to promote understanding and reduce stigma. 
 
(Para 3.7.9 of the Report) 
 
Action Taken 
 
1.14.6 The recommendation of the Committee has been noted. The targeted IEC plan has 
been sought from all SACS on the basis of their cultural and social need. NACO has adopted 
a 360 -degree approach to communication, implementing multimedia campaigns across 
television, radio, digital platforms, print and outdoor media. These ca mpaigns are designed to 
be inclusive, culturally sensitive and evidence -based, aiming to normalize discussions around 
HIV and encourage testing, treatment adherence and social acceptance of People Living with 
HIV (PLHIV). 
 
1.15 Global Fund Grant Under COvid-19 Response Mechanism 2021 
 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
1.15.1 Given that the Rs. 355.07 crore has been allocated in BE 2025 -26 under the Global 
Fund Covid-19 Response Mechanism is time -limited, expiring in December 2025, and aimed 
at strengthening health systems for future pandemic preparedness and TB elimination, the 
Committee recommends expedited and efficient utilization of these funds with a focus on 
sustainable capacity building and infrastructure development. The Committee is of the view 
that immediate procurement of handheld X -rays for TB screening and strengthening of 
oxygen infrastructure should be prioritised. Simultaneously, integrated public health 
laboratory enhancements and staff training should also be accelerated. The Department should 
also create a plan for how these systems will be maintained and funded after the global fund 
grant expires. 
(Para 3.8.3 of the Report) 
 

28 
 
Action Taken 
1.15.2 The Ministry is committed to utilize the Global Fund grants in a time bound manner.  
Procurement of  hand-held X -rays for TB screening,  the tendering process has already been 
initiated by CMSS and is expected to be concluded before December 2025.  Regarding 
strengthening of oxygen infrastructure, HITES has been selected as the procurement agency 
for procurement of booster pumps for oxygen cylinder refilling. The requirements from the 
States are being collected and the technical specifications have been finalised for initiating the 
process of procurement.  Procurement of equipment for the Integrated Public  Health 
Laboratories is in the tendering process through CMSS.  National level training workshop on 
oxygen management and administrative capacity building is under process through AIIMS & 
NIHFW. Post Global Fund grant, the maintenance & operationalization of these 
equipment/systems will be done by the State/ UTs through the State Budget and 
supplemented by the National Health Mission, wherever required. 
 
1.16 Family Welfare Schemes 
 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
1.16.1 The Committee has been informed that the  main reason for projecting less amount 
under BE 2025 -26 over BE 2024 -25  is less allocation of funds under WHO supported 
National Polio Surveillance Project (NPS) due to non -finalization of continuation of this 
component by Expenditure Finance Committee ( EFC) at time of finalization of BE 2025 -26. 
Now, this programme has now been approved in SFC, the additional funds shall be sought at 
the time of finalization of RE 2025 -26. The fluctuation of the WHO -NPS project budget due 
to the EFC process, shows the ne ed for better long term financial planning. The Committee is 
of the view that the Government should prefer to implement a long -term financial planning 
system, that reduces the impact of delayed approval processes on program funding. 
(Para 3.9.3 of the Report) 
Action Taken 
1.16.2 Observations of Parliamentary Standing Committee have been noted. Process to be 
followed for securing payment till financial year 2026 -27 have been effectively completed. 
The amount for each year has been crystalized for payment. Al l steps will be taken for 
necessary follow up in a timely manner. 
Recommendations/Observations 
1.16.3 The Committee observes that, for the FY 2024 -25, BE was Rs. 694.72 crore which 
was revised to Rs. 619.37 crore at RE stage and the total amount released till 31.01.2025 is 
Rs.   360.56 crore which is 58.21 % of RE. The budget utilization trends reveal disc repancies 
between approved budgets and actual spending, indicating potential inefficiencies or shortfall 
in fund allocation. The Committee recommends the Department to strengthen data -driven 
program management facilitating real -time monitoring of key perfo rmance indicators, 
enabling timely identification of program gaps and areas requiring intervention. The 

29 
 
Department should utilize this data to conduct regular, evidence -based reviews of program 
effectiveness and resource allocation, ensuring that funding i s aligned with program priorities 
and achieving desired outcomes. Further, the department should create a system that tracks 
the percentage of program goals achieved, versus the percentage of budget spent ensuring 
effective and optimal/rational utilization of funds. 
(Para 3.9.4 of the Report) 
Action Taken 
1.16.4 The budget allocation under Family Welfare Scheme, a Central Sector Scheme during 
the financial year 2023-24 and 2024-25 is given below: -
