1.16.5 It is evident from the above table the Department has utilized 96.39% and 99.94% 
budget allocation in respect of RE provisions of this scheme during the financial year 2023-24 
and 2024 -25 respectively.   This target has been achieved by optimal utilization of funds 
through regular review and monitoring each sub -scheme.  However, the suggestions given by 
the Committee has been noted. 
 
 
Recommendations/Observations 
1.16.6 The Committee notes that the success of the Family Welfare Scheme depends on the 
synergistic interaction between its various components (SNA, Contraceptive Distribution, 
PNDT Act Awareness, etc.). Further,  the general public awareness of the programs is vital to 
the programs‘ success. The Committee recommends the Department to develop a 
comprehensive communication strategy that integrates messaging across all components of 
the Family Welfare Scheme. The Dep artment should leverage a variety of communication 
channels, including digital media and community outreach programs to reach diverse 
populations focusing on raising public awareness about the availability of services, promoting 
healthy behaviors, and addr essing social and cultural barriers to accessing care. The 
Committee believes that the co -ordination and collaboration among various programs should 
be further enhanced to increase their effectiveness due to co -location of activities and caution 
the Department that the programs do not progress in silos. 
(Para 3.9.5 of the Report) 

31 
 
Action Taken 
1.16.7 The suggestions of the Committee have been noted. Swastha Nagarik Abhiyan (SNA) 
is an ongoing Central Sector Scheme to carry out Information, Education and Communication 
(IEC) activities across the country from the central/headquarter level. Such IEC activ ities are 
planned in consultation with the respective Programme Division as per their programme 
priorities and accoridingly the conceptualization, formation and creative finalization of the 
communication strategy is done. The target audience is also chosen  carefully and media 
strategy formulated accordingly. There is a collaboration mechanism for analyzing the 
feedback received which is used to fine tune and revive the communication channels 
including digital media and community outreach programmes. The inp uts of the 
Parliamentary Standing Committee on a comprehensive communication strategy have been 
duly noted.  
1.16.8 The co-ordination and collaboration among various programs is essential to build up 
synergy between various components of RMNCHA+ and enhanc e awareness, promote 
healthy behaviours, and improve service uptake. The continuum of care approach under 
RMNCHA+ strategy reinforces Government‘s commitment to safeguard the health and well -
being of the girl child—from pre conception to the antenatal period, through infancy and early 
childhood, adolescence and entire reproductive span to ensure a nurturing environment for 
improved health outcomes and gender equity. The PNDT Division, in collaboration with the 
Swastha Nagrik Abhiyan (SNA) Division, is formu lating a focused advocacy and IEC 
campaign to promote a positive behavioural shift in societal attitudes towards the girl child.  
The campaign includes targeted audio -visual content such as radio jingles and short video 
messages, accompanied by strategic dissemination plan to ensure wide outreach and sustained 
impact across diverse population groups. 
1.16.9 In this context, the Family Planning Division is also making focused efforts to develop 
a comprehensive Information, Education, and Communication (IEC) plan under the National 
Family Planning Programme. It aims to promote informed decision -making on contraceptive 
choices and encourages healthy timing and spacing of pregnancies (HTSP), which has a direct 
bearing on the health and well -being of mother and c hild. The IEC activities under the FP 
Division prioritize thematic areas such as spacing, limiting and permanent methods of 
contraception, post -pregnancy contraception, and male engagement to address social and 
cultural barriers and support informed reproductive choices. Annual initiatives such as ‗World 
Population Day & Fortnight‘ and ‗Vasectomy Fortnight‘ further strengthen awareness and 
service uptake. 
1.17 Establishment and Strengthening of NCDC Branches and Health Initiatives, 
Inter Sectoral Co -ordination for Preparation and Control of Zoonotic Diseases and 
Other Neglected Tropical Diseases, Surveillance of Viral Hepatitis, Anti Microbial 
Resistance 
Recommendations/Observations 
1.17.1 The Committee recommends a sustained and expanded focus on capacity building 
across all programs, with a particular emphasis on AMR containment and zoonotic disease 

32 
 
management. Leveraging the existing network of laboratories, training sites, and regional 
coordinators, the program should prioritize consistent, high -quality training delivery to 
strengthen expertise and ensure standardized practices. For AMR containment, the Committee 
recommends the expansion of laboratory networks must be accompanied by rigor ous, 
standardized training in bacteriology and HAI surveillance, ensuring quality -assured testing 
and evidence -based treatment. Similarly, for zoonotic disease management, the successful 
approach of multi-stakeholder consultations and hands-on training should be scaled up, with a 
strong focus on developing and implementing standardized training modules for medical 
officers and frontline workers. Additionally, the expansion of Regional Venom Centers and 
the establishment of Poison Information Centers for sna kebite management should be 
supported through comprehensive training programs. The Committee recommends for wider 
dissemination of IEC material for snakebite prevention and control, and protocols on initial 
management of snakebite especially in remote/rura l areas. The Committee also recommends 
for launching snakebite Helpline Project and training for medical officers on snakebite in all 
States/UTs. The Committee is of the opinion that a continuous investment in human resource 
development will be the key to ensure long-term success and sustainability of these critical 
public health initiatives. 
(Para 3.10.5 of the Report) 
Action Taken 
1.17.2 The recommendations of the committee are noted.  National Programme on AMR 
Containment was launched by MoHFW during the 12th five -year plan (2013 -14) and is 
coordinated by NCDC. Under the programme, National AMR Surveillance Network (NARS -
Net) involving State Medical College labs have been established an d are being expanded in 
phased manner. The programme has been expanded from 8 labs in 7 States/UTs in 2014 to 60 
Medical Colleges in 33 States/UTs till March 2025.   
1.17.3 For Standardized, consistent high-quality trainings, the following steps are taken: - 
(i) Standard Operating Procedures (SOPs) have been developed for laboratory testing 
including internal quality controls. These SoPs are regularly updated and translated to 
training modules and used for trainings. These are also made available on NCDC 
website for countrywide dissemination. 
(ii) SoPs have also been developed for AMR data management and trainings are provided 
across the country at National and State level. 
(iii) National infection prevention and control guidelines for healthcare facilities including 
surveillance of Healthcare Associated Infections (HAIs) have been developed and 
translated to training modules. These have been used to conduct National training of 
trainers for all States and UTs. Trainings are being cascaded by States using the 
standardized modules.  
 
1.17.4 For AMR data surveillance, the following steps are taken: - 
(i) To ensure robust data surveillance protocols Standard Operating Procedures (SOP) 
have been developed and are used by all sites submitting data under the programme  

33 
 
(ii) To ensure data quality all labs submitting AMR data are participating in EQAS and are 
maintaining good scores and all AMR alert pathogens identified at programme sites are 
confirmed at AMR NRL established at NCDC.  
(iii) Annual AMR surveillance data is collated to develop annual National AMR 
Surveillance reports which are available on NCDC website and the data is also 
submitted to the Global AMR Surveillance System (GLASS) 
1.17.5 For Expansion of laboratory networks and standardized trainings in bacteriology and 
HAI, the following steps are taken: - 
(i) Capacity building on AMR containment is being expanded to additional Medical 
Colleges in States through State level AMR surveillance networks.  
(ii) Standardized HAI surveillance trainings for sites under National Programme  on AMR 
containment are being conducted in collaboration with AIIMS, New Delhi. 
1.17.6 National One Health Programme for Prevention & Control of Zoonoses(NOHP -PCZ) 
is being implemented since 12th FYP. Under the programme, the strengthened Regional 
Coordinators are mandated to conduct Multi -stakeholder sensitization trainings and hands -on 
training on Zoonotic Diseases.  In addition, the Programme Division (PD) routinely conducts 
consultation workshops and capacity-building sessions with relevant stakeholders to enhance 
Inter-sectoral collaboration and field -level preparedness.  Learning resource package (e -
learning module) for Medical Officers and frontline workers on zoonoses have been 
developed. Under snake bite prevention and control activities, trainings of  Medical Officers 
and health workers are being undertaken through National Health Mission. To enhance 
surveillance and reporting of snakebite cases and associated mortalities, the Ministry has 
communicated with all States/UTs to declare snakebite as a Noti fiable Disease under the 
State Public Health Act or other applicable legislation (Ref. D.O. No. 
ISCP/57155/06/DZDP/NCDC dated 27th November 2024) (Annexure -6). In line with the 
Committee‘s recommendations to raise community awareness and support health wor kers in 
rural and remote areas, the Ministry has developed a comprehensive suite of Information, 
Education, and Communication (IEC) materials and protocol on management of snakebite 
focused on prevention, first aid, and timely referral and the same has bee n shared with the 
States/UTs and made available on the NCDC website for wider dissemination. Snakebite 
Helpline number has been launched for five States/UTs (Andhra Pradesh, Puducherry, Delhi, 
Madhya Pradesh, and Assam). 
 
Recommendations/Observations 
1.17.7 The Committee recommends strengthening surveillance systems and data integration 
to enhance proactive disease management. Building on the AMR surveillance network and 
NOHP-PCZ integration into IHIP, programs should implement robust surveillance prot ocols 
and data management systems. For AMR, data collection and reporting to Global 
Antimicrobial Resistance and Use Surveillance System (GLASS) should be complemented by 
facility-based antibiograms to support evidence -based antibiotic treatment. For zoono tic 
diseases, implementing the Augmented Zoonotic Disease Surveillance (AZDS) proposal is 

34 
 
critical for strengthening pathogen surveillance in hotspot areas. Additionally, the Rabies 
Helpline expansion and snakebite case notifications should be integrated i nto a unified 
national surveillance platform for timely data analysis and informed decision -making. The 
Committee is of the view that establishing a Programme Management Unit (PMU) at NCDC 
will ensure coordinated surveillance, data quality, and rapid information dissemination. 
(Para 3.10.6 of the Report) 
Action Taken 
1.17.8 The recommendations of the committee are well noted. Under the national programme 
on AMR containment, the network Medical Colleges are being trained to utilize the AMR 
data locally for development of antibiograms to guide evidence based antibiotic treatment.  
NOHP-PCZ portal is onboarded on IHIP portal. Preliminary process of scanning data 
collection points related to One Health from different Ministries is undertaken by NCDC.  
Augmented Zoonotic Disease Surveillance (AZDS) is critical for identifying early warning 
signals for zoonotic diseases originating from wildlife and coordination with relevant 
institutions for the same has been initiated. 
Recommendations/Observations 
1.17.9 To ensure  the sustained effectiveness and responsiveness of the Integrated Disease 
Surveillance Programme (IDSP), the Committee recommends a targeted initiative to enhance 
data collection and analysis to arrive at a decision in specific time -frame in the Integrated  
Health Information Platform (IHIP). The Committee believes that it can be achieved through: 
(i) intensified training and capacity building for healthcare workers at all levels on IHIP data 
entry & analytics and utilization; (ii) streamlined data entry pro cesses and user interface 
improvements to minimize reporting burdens; (iii) regular monitoring and feedback 
mechanisms to identify and rectify reporting challenges; and (iv) leveraging the recently 
launched community reporting tool to supplement and valida te data from health facilities, 
thereby fostering a more comprehensive and robust surveillance system. 
(Para 3.10.12 of the Report) 
Action Taken 
1.17.10  The recommendation of the committee is well -taken for incorporating in the current 
& future training plans of IDSP and NCDC.  Before the transition of IDSP reporting, an 
intensive training exercise was undertaken at State, district, and sub -district levels to ensure 
that all the healthcare workers viz. ANMs, Medical Officers, Lab Personnel, and Private 
Practitioners are well -versed in reporting IDSP data on IHIP.  The routine trainings under 
IDSP are also geared towards enhancing the understanding of the trainees of all levels 
towards use of IHIP.  The trainings of other divisions of NCDC such as FETP, Sector  
Connect, and PHEDM are also aimed at capacitating the district surveillance units of IDSP in 
better understanding & utilization of surveillance data on IHIP.  Applications have been 
developed for all levels of users (Syndromic [S], Presumptive [P], and Lab oratory [L] form 
users) to report the data through hand -held devices such as mobile phones and tablets.  
Integrating IDSP -IHIP with Ayushman Bharat Digital Mission [ABDM] is in an advanced 
stage and once completed will enable data entry through the use of A BHA ID. IDSP-IHIP is 

35 
 
compliant with e -Governance standards of Government of India facilitating seamless 
exchange of data with other platforms through APIs.  The IDSP-IHIP is designed to generate 
auto-analysed reports to all levels of users serving as an eff ective feedback mechanism. 
Dashboards to indicate reporting performance, disease trends, outbreak investigation scores, 
and non -performing units are in -built in IDSP -IHIP. A daily SMS based feedback on 
reporting is sent to District, State, and Central Surv eillance Unit [CSU] level users. A weekly 
feedback bulletin identifying the better performing & lagging units is shared with the senior 
officials of the State health department.  A monthly feedback report is also shared with State 
Surveillance Units of IDSP  detailing their performance, identifying key challenges, and 
recommendations for improvement.  The community reporting tool was launched in January‘ 
2024 and efforts are on to increase the community awareness towards the use of this tool. 
 
Recommendations/Observations 
1.17.11 The Committee recommends building on existing collaborations with NACP and 
NFHS, it should implement a standardized framework for data collection and scientific 
analysis of data, integrating seroprevalence data across multiple disease areas to ensure 
continuity and clarity between surveillance of data and programmatic action. This expansion 
will optimize laboratory infrastructure, generate a comprehensive dataset essential for 
informed policymaking, targeted interventions, and effective disease control. 
(Para 3.10.14 of the Report) 
Action Taken 
1.17.12 The program is already integrating with the population level surveys/programs for 
carrying out surveillance of chronic viral hepatitis as also recommended by the Committee . 
The program is also implementing the same through standardized framework for data 
collection and scientific analysis of data. This is to ensure optimal utilization for laboratory 
infrastructure and generate comprehensive data set essential for program pl anning and 
prioritizing interventions: - 
(i) National Family Health Survey - integrated with round 4, 5 & 6 for estimation of 
seroprevalence of hepatitis B and hepatitis C. Factsheet on seroprevalence of hepatitis B 
and hepatitis C based on NFHS -4 was developed . First ever nationally representative 
data for seroprevalence of both hepatitis B and hepatitis C obtained. Seroprevalence of 
hepatitis B and hepatitis C was found to be 0.95% and 0.32% respectively. The testing of 
specimens obtained during NFHS-5 is ongoing for hepatitis B and hepatitis C. 
(ii) HIV Sentinel Surveillance (HSS) of National AIDS Control Program (NACP) for 
inclusion of bio-markers of hepatitis B and C in HSS utilising existing machinery of HSS 
with minimum financial implications. Report on estimation of seroprevalence of 
hepatitis B and hepatitis C was developed based on the data of round 17 of HSS held in 
2021. Seroprevalence of hepatitis B and hepatitis C was found to be 0.85% and 0.29% 
respectively. The biomarkers of hepatitis B and hepat itis C have also been included in 
the 18th & 19th round of HSS. 

36 
 
 
1.18 Development of Nursing Services 
Recommendations/Observations 
1.18.1 The Committee believes that, to achieve sustained improvements in nursing services 
and rectify geographical disparitie s, the Government need to implement a comprehensive, 
data-driven strategy for the targeted expansion and enhancement of nursing education and 
training with a particular focus on underserved regions. The Committee urges for integrated 
implementation of the existing Central Sector Scheme for the Development of Nursing 
Services, the establishment of 157 new nursing colleges in co -location with existing medical 
colleges, and the Continuing Nursing Education (CNE) framework. The Committee further 
recommends that  the scheme must emphasize the training of nurses in the field of 
specialization, frequent/periodic in -service training, infrastructure development by way of 
strengthening of Schools and upgrading of Schools of Nursing to Colleges of Nursing. 
(Para 3.12.6 of the Report) 
Action Taken 
1.18.2 To bring reforms in nursing education with a vision to provide high quality, affordable 
and equitable nursing education in India speciallyto underserved districts and States/UTs, one 
hundred and fifty-seven (157) new nur sing colleges are being established in co -location with 
the existing 157 medical colleges established since 2014 under the Centrally Sponsored 
Scheme. The Empowered Committee headed by the Union Health Secretary in the Centre and 
Principal Secretary, Healt h/Medical Education in the States monitors the progress of work.  
Under the Central Sector Schemes of Development of Nursing Services (DNS), funds to the 
tune of Rs. 2,28,500/ - per course of 7 days duration for 30 participants is provided for 
conducting sho rt term training courses for in -service nursing personnel and a onetime 
financial assistance of Rs. 7.00 crore per institution is given for upgrading School of Nursing 
attached to a Medical College into College of Nursing.  
1.18.3 Digital Monitoring of Nur sing Education by INC - as part of regulation and standards 
of Nursing Education, INC examines the suitability of all the Nursing Colleges/Institutes 
(both Government & Private) for all the nursing courses in the country. The complete process 
of issuing of suitability letters to the Nursing Institutions is automated through 100% 
digitization by INC. As part of this, institutions apply/upload all the data online including 
online payment. After receiving the application online from the institutions INC examine s all 
the applications as per set guidelines and as per its office workflow and the letters can be 
downloaded by the institutions from the above URLs. INC Website 
www.indiannursingcouncil.org is dynamic. IN C website has been re -designed in the year 
2021. National Reference Simulation Centre (NRSC) has been established in collaboration 
with Jhpiego, Laderal and SGT University Gurugram by INC to enhance nurses competencies 
and up skilling of nurses. National Reference Simulation Centre (NRSC) has been established 
to improve the quality of Nursing Education. 982 faculties have been trained in Training of 
Trainer (ToT) for 6 days training and 731 faculties have been trained for 5 days training for 
Midwifery faculty in Simulation.  

37 
 
1.18.4 Nurse Practitioner Programmes: INC has initiated Nurse Practitioner Programme post 
graduate level programme in residency mode in last one -decade Nurse Practitioner Critical 
Care (NPCC) already implemented in various States since 2 017 and till date 68 institutions 
and 985 seats is running the programme successfully. Under the Chintan Shivir mandate, 
twelve new Nurse Practitioner Programmes have been prepared and placed on the INC 
website and details are as follow: - 
 Nurse Practitioner in Critical Care (NPCC) 
 Nurse Practitioner in Midwifery Programme (NPM) 
 Nurse Practitioner in Emergency & Trauma Care (NPE&T) 
 Nurse Practitioner in Geriatric Nursing (NPGN) 
 Nurse Practitioner in Organ Transplant Nursing (NPTN) 
 Nurse Practitioner in Mental Health/Psychiatric Nursing      
 (NPMN) 
 Nurse Practitioner in Nephrology Nursing (NPNEPN) 
 Nurse Practitioner in Family Medicine Nursing (NPFM) 
 Nurse Practitioner in Anesthesia Nursing (NPA) 
 Nurse Practitioner in pediatrics Nursing (NPPN) 
 Nurse Practitioner in Neonatal Nursing (NPNEON) 
Recommendations/Observations 
1.18.5 The Committee is of the opinion that Government should actively enforce CNE 
regulations with rigorous monitoring of training outcomes to ensure that all nurses maintain 
their competency through mandatory training. The approval and funding processes for state 
government proposals should be streamlined, expediting infrastructure development and 
training initiatives. These measures will directly address the shortage of qualified n ursing 
personnel and enhance healthcare delivery across India. Additionally, the Committee gives 
emphasis to the efficient utilization of allocated funds and the strategic distribution of 
resources based on state-wise data on nursing institutions and admission capacities. 
(Para 3.12.7 of the Report) 
Action Taken 
1.18.6  Continuing Nursing Education (CNE) is a critical component for ensuring the ongoing 
professional development and competency of nursing personnel. The INCs Gazette 
notification dated 06.11.2020 mandates requiring all Registered Nurses and Midwives (RN & 
RM), Axillary Nurse Midwives (RANM), and Lady Health Visitors (RLHV) to complete 150 
hours or 30 credits of CNE every five years as a perquisite for renewal of registration. The 
enforcement of this requirement, with active participation by the State Nurses Registration 
Councils (SNRCs), is imperative to maintain and enhance the quality of nursing services 
across the country. INC has been working with SNRCs to strengthen the use of the Nurses 
Registration and Tracking System (NRTS) to ensure timely and accurate tracking of CNE 
participation and credit accumulation.  

38 
 
1.18.7 Recognizing the urgent need to address the shortage of qualified nursing personnel, 
the Ministry agrees with the Committees re commendation to streamline the approval and 
funding processes for state proposals related to infrastructure development and training 
initiatives.  The Ministry has been working in close coordination with State Health 
Departments to fast -track the establish ment and enhancement of nursing institutions.  INC is 
also developing accessible e -learning modules and conducting simulation -based training 
programmes with financial support provided for participant. 
1.19 Health Sector Disaster Preparedness and Response and Human Resource 
Development for Emergency Medical Services 
Recommendations/Observations 
1.19.1 For efficient and optimal utilization of funds under the scheme, the Committee 
believes that the Government should consider the following: (i) pre -identified and vetted site 
alternatives to mitigate delays due to site changes; (ii) mandatory quarterly fund utilization 
reports from grantee medical colleges for NELS Skill Centers, with clear timelines for 
disbursement and penalties for non -compliance; and (iii) a  proactive, needs-based assessment 
system for disaster and public health emergency response logistics, rather than solely relying 
on state/UT requests, to ensure allocated funds are strategically utilized. This systematic 
approach will address the recurrent issues of delayed project execution and underutilization of 
funds. 
(Para 3.13.7 of the Report) 
 
Action Taken 
1.19.2 The observations/recommendation of the Hon‘ble Committee are duly noted.  (i) and 
(ii) As suggested by Hon‘ble Committee submission of quarterly fund utilization report is 
already a part of MoUs signed with grantee institutions. States/UTs are encouraged to identify 
multiple suitable Government Medical Colleges where establishment of NELS Skill centres 
may be considered.  (iii) Disaster Ma nagement and Health are State subjects. This Ministry 
offers Medical logistics and other assistance as per the needs of the State. This necessitates 
maintaining buffer funds for meeting such eventualities.  With regards to feasibility of 
implementing a proa ctive, needs -based assessment system for disaster and public health 
emergency response logistics, States/UTs are proactively requested through this Ministry‘s 
Regional Officers of Health and Family Welfare to convey any requirement of medical 
logistics to support State Health Department‘s initiative to provide medical care relief to 
disaster affected communities. 
1.20 National Organ Transplant Program (NOTP) 
Recommendations/Observations 
1.20.1 The Committee notes that the funds utilization trend under the N ational Organ 
Transplant Program (NOTP) show significant discrepancies between budgeted and actual 
expenditures, indicating poor fund utilization. The Committee, therefore, recommends the 

39 
 
Government to establish a robust mechanism incorporating accountabil ity and ensuring 
enhanced and effective fund utilization by ROTTOs and SOTTOs. The Department needs to 
address the root causes of underutilization, including administrative bottlenecks and lack of 
state-level capacity and overcome the reasons for under -utilization and also persuading the 
non-performing States for optimal utilization of allocated fund.   
(Para 3.14.5 of the Report) 
 
1.20.2 The Committee, on examination of the submissions made by the Department of Health 
and Family Welfare, observes that many  ROTTOs and SOTTOs are in their infancy stage, 
lacking infrastructure and operational efficiency. The Committee, therefore, recommends the 
strengthening of ROTTOs and SOTTOs by provide comprehensive capacity -building 
programs for ROTTOs and SOTTOs, focusin g on website development, data management, 
budget utilization, and training program organization. 
(Para 3.14.6 of the Report) 
Action Taken 
1.20.3 The observations/recommendation of the Hon‘ble Committee are duly noted. It is 
important to highlight that despite the underutilization of funds, the program has reliably 
achieved its annual targets for deceased donations, organ donation rates, and the number of 
organ transplants ahead of time. Since 2013 -14, these metrics have demonstrated a consistent 
upward trend. Notably, the total number of transplants increased from 4,990 in 2011 to 18,711 
in 2024, and the count of deceased donor transplants rose from 837 in 2013 to 3,236 in 2024. 
 
1.21 National Digital Health Mission (NDHM)/ Ayushman Bharat Digital Missio n 
(ABDM) 
Recommendations/Observations 
1.21.1 The Committee recommends to fully leveraging the potential of AI and ML in 
healthcare by accelerating the integration and scaling of AI-driven tools within the Ayushman 
Bharat Digital Mission (ABDM). Specifically, the successful collaborations, such as the MoU 
with IIT Kanpur and the AI-based Clinical Decision Support System (CDSS) in e -Sanjeevani, 
should be rapidly replicated. This includes prioritizing the development and deployment of 
unified learni ng platforms, quality -preserving databases, and open benchmarking systems, 
ensuring that these technologies are accessible and beneficial across all healthcare facilities, 
especially in rural and underserved areas. This will enhance diagnostic accuracy, st reamline 
patient care, and improve overall health outcomes by making data -driven decisions more 
efficient. 
(Para 3.16.10 of the Report) 
 
 

40 
 
Action Taken 
1.21.2 The Suggestion of the Hon‘ble Committee are duly noted.  Several Artificial 
Intelligence (AI) research and decision -making systems are in progress. The status of AI 
systems/ applications deployed or at an advanced s tage of readiness is as given. The Clinical 
Decision Support System (CDSS) AI solution has been integrated with the national 
telemedicine platform, e -Sanjeevani. This has helped to enhance consultation quality by 
streamlining symptoms/patient complaints entry and providing AI -based 
diagnosis/differential recommendations.  Amongst the various AI solutions under 
development ‗Diabetic Retinop athy‘ AI solution is at an advanced stage and best efforts are 
being made for its early integration. Other AI applications are at different stages and would be 
suitably integrated in due course.  
1.21.3 Government of India has launched Ayushman Bharat Digital Mission (ABDM) which 
aims to create a platform enabling interoperability of health data within the health ecosystem 
so as to create longitudinal Electronic Health Record (EHR) of every citizen. Under ABDM, 
multiple registries are made to ensure that data silos can be broken and longitudinal electronic 
health record (EHR) of citizen is created. Further it will integrate cutting edge technologies 
such as Artificial Intelligence, IoTs, block chain etc. with existing health IT applications as 
per need for improving the performance of the health services. NITI Aayog has published two 
approach documents on AI for India, ―Responsible AI‖ approach documents in February 2021 
and ―Operationalizing Principles for ResponsibleAI‖ in August 2021.Ministry of Health has  
also recently designated AIIMS Delhi, PGIMER 
ChandigarhandAIIMSRishikeshasCentreofExcellenceforArtificialIntelligencewith an aim to 
promote creation and use of AI based solutions in Health. 
 
1.21.4 MoU with the All -India Institute of Medical Sciences (AIIMS),Delhi. The National 
Health Authority (NHA) has signed a MoU with AIIMS, Delhi to support healthcare 
providers in providing evidence -based care to patients. Towards this end, AIIMS has 
provided Clinical Decision Support System (CDSS) under ABDM for ABDM compliant 
digital solution companies (DSCs). CDSS has been developed to help manage the Non -
Communicable Disease (NCD) burden in India and provides evidence -based care tailored to 
individual patient clinical profiles i.e. generating personalized custo mized management plans 
for patients and suggesting optimal drugs, their dosage and warning contraindications, etc. 
This has been designed to act as an informative and advisory toolto support healthcare 
providers in making informed decisions.  
1.21.5 Current Status of Work Under the MoU. As a part of ongoing integration eﬀorts of the 
AIIMS CDSS, the following progress has been made 
 10 DSCs(including public and private)havesuccessfullycompletedintegration. 
 3DSCsarecurrentlyintegrating 
 As on date, a total of 1,74,474 successful transactions have been recorded across all 
integrated platforms. 

41 
 
 Comprehensive training material has been developed and is being shared with all 
integrators to support usage of CDSS by healthcare professionals. 
1.21.6 MoU with IIT Kanpu r. Furthermore, a Memorandum of Understanding (MoU) 
between the National Health Authority (NHA) and IIT Kanpur has also been entered into 
which aims to develop digital public goods for AI in healthcare under the Ayushman Bharat 
Digital Mission (ABDM).This collaboration is designed to create a public benchmarking 
platform for AI models, enabling researchers and developers to validate and enhance their AI 
tools using ABDM data. 
 
1.21.7 Objectives of the MoU 
(i) Federated Learning Platform: Establishing a machine learning model pipeline that 
allows AI models to be trained across decentralized datasets while preserving privacy. 
(ii) Quality-Preserving Database (QPD): Developing a data management layer that 
ensures statistical integrity while allowing AI models to be test ed on real-world health 
data.  
(iii) Open Benchmarking Platform (OPD): Creating a public benchmark for AI models to 
compare and validate their performance against standardized datasets. 
(iv) Consent Management System (HIECM): Implementing a secure framework for 
patients to provide consent for their health data to be used in AI research. 
1.21.8 Current Status of Work Under the MoU 
 
(i) TheAIDPGsarefullydevelopedandreadyforintegrationintoNHA‘sITsystems. 
(ii) Live instances have been deployed on AWS, accessible for review and training. 
Training will be provided to NHA teams to assess the hosted instances and provide 
feedback on integration feasibility. 
(iii) Document has been shared by IIT -K which provides detailed steps for system 
interaction, including Federated Learning workflows.  The Fed Client and Fed Server 
instances are operational, supporting bone age prediction models using CNN 
architectures. 
The QPD dataset mechanism is functional which also ensures secure data. 
 
Recommendations/Observations 
1.21.9 The Committee believes that the ABDM should effectively reach rural and tribal 
populations for gains in real terms. The Committee, in this connection, emphasizes the need 
for enhanced digital literacy and connectivity initiatives alongside the mission's te chnical 
advancements. The NHA should intensify efforts to implement and promote the assisted 
mode for ABHA creation in areas with poor internet connectivity and limited digital literacy. 
Additionally, targeted awareness campaigns and training programs shou ld be conducted in 
local languages to educate healthcare providers and citizens about the benefits and usage of 

42 
 
ABDM services like Scan and Share and Scan and Pay. Collaborations with local community 
leaders and NGOs can further facilitate the adoption of ABDM, ensuring that even the most 
remote populations have equitable access to digital health services while maintaining robust 
privacy and security measures. 
(Para 3.16.11 of the Report) 
Action Taken 
1.21.10 The Ayushman Bharat Digital Mission (ABDM) envis ions the creation of a national 
digital health ecosystem that enables universal health coverage in an efficient, accessible,  
inclusive, aﬀordable, timely, and secure manner. To address some issues like poor internet 
connectivity, limited access to digital devices user-friendly and multilingual applications such 
as the ABHA app and Arogya Setu app have been developed to make digital health services. 
To ensure widespread adoption of ABDM in rur al and tribal areas, NHA has prioritized some 
measures. ABDM has provisions for assisted mode in places where internet connectivity 
might be poor. For instance, offline mode for creation of ABHA has been enabled wherever 
internet connectivity might be poor  or there would be unavailability of hardware or both.  
User-centric design in the ABHA application. As a new feature NHA is working on ensuring 
that a multi-lingual application is available for the citizens in multiple Indian languages.  High 
quality IEC ma terial for various initiatives such as Scan & Pay and Scan & Share has been 
prepared by NHA. Editable version of these materials has been shared with states to be 
prepared in local language. 
 
1.21.11 NHA has adopted a cascade model for conducting training programs. A total of 
approx. 1061 participants covered during 17 in -person trainings/workshops out of which 136 
were master trainers. Apart from this, 57 virtual sessions were conducted to cover around 
9073 participants for various stakeholders including S tate Mission Directors, PMUs, 136 
master trainers to increase awareness of ABDM and its benefits amongst healthcare providers 
and citizens. Currently, 121 Microsites are operational across 20 States/UTs, including remote 
and hilly regions like Mizoram, Manipur, Nagaland, Assam, Tripura, and Uttarakhand. Under 
the 100 Microsite project, NHA has earmarked the fund for IEC & CB activities which the 
states have utilized in various awareness campaigns.  NHA has provided the IEC material 
which the states have cust omized in their regional languages, and field teams have been 
trained accordingly. NHA is working on use cases for non -smart phone users as well, which 
will allow for consent capture, record sharing and ABHA creation for non -smart phone users. 
This makes some of the functionalities inclusive of citizens in rural areas and ground 
conditions. Awareness materials and how to sessions for health facilities about availing 
supporting government schemes such as PM Vaani (public WiFi) and using DHIS incentives 
for Network Boosters or procurement of digital devices to address the lack of internet 
connectivity in rural and tribal areas.  Dedicated teams working for increasing ABDM 
adoption in the rural areas who understand the unique on ground requirements. NHA gets 
inputs through the microsite and state teams on the same. This enables NHA to roll out more 
context specific actionable and functionalities. 

43 
 
 
1.22 Statutory and Regulatory Bodies 
Recommendations/Observations 
1.22.1 Given the importance of FSSAI's activities,  particularly in supporting States/UTs, the 
Committee is of the opinion that a phased funding release tied to performance and capacity 
building should be implemented. Instead of a single annual allocation, funds could be released 
in tranches, contingent up on the timely submission of UCs and demonstrable progress in 
enhancing food safety infrastructure and surveillance. In addition, a portion of the funds 
should be dedicated to capacity -building initiatives, such as training programs for food safety 
officers, procurement of essential equipment, and the establishment of robust laboratory 
facilities at the state and local levels. This approach will incentivize States/UTs to prioritize 
food safety initiatives, improve their administrative efficiency, and build s ustainable food 
safety systems. 
(Para 3.17.7 of the Report) 
 
Action Taken 
1.22.2 Funds released to the States/UTs are utilized for capacity building and training along 
with activities related to enforcement and strengthening of lab infrastructures. Approximately 
50 percent of amount released is utilized towards this.  The funds are released to States/UTs in 
two tranches subject to utilization of previous funds. In the first tranche, 50% of the Revenue 
and 25% of Capital funds are released to the State s/UTs.  The second tranche is released only 
after the State/UT submits 60% utilization of the fund released under 1st tranche. Funds in FY 
2024-25 have been released only to those states who have submitted UC up to 2022 -23. 
Dedicated funds are being earmar ked for training of Food Safety Officers (FSO) /Designated 
Officers (DO) and for enhancing food testing infrastructure.  
1.22.3 Funds released/spent to State/UTs in the FY 2024 -25 for Capacity Building and   
enhancement of food testing infrastructure are as follows:  
Rs.10.53 Crore has been released to various State/UTs FSSAI for the FY 2024 -25 for training 
of Food Businesses in food safety and hygiene and training of Food Safety officers. In 2024 -
25, 6.32 lakhs food handlers have been trained in 11900 trai nings which include 53660 Street 
Food Handlers and 2500 Food Safety Officers have been trained in 27 trainings. 
 
Recommendations/Observations 
1.22.4 The Committee notes that FSSAI is actively deploying Mobile Food Testing 
Laboratories (MFTLs) to enhance on-the-spot food testing and awareness, and while progress 
is made in this connection, achieving the target of one MFTL/FSW per district by 2026 is 
contingent on state readiness. The Committee recommends FSSAI to implement a structured 
program to actively assist states in achieving readiness/willingness to deploy MFTLs/FSWs. 

44 
 
The Committee feels that through dedicated State liaisons, FSSAI personnel shoul d work 
directly with state governments to identify and address specific barriers to FSW deployment. 
The Committee desires that targeted training programs and expedited funding mechanisms 
should be established showing proactive approach to prevent delays an d ensure consistent 
food safety monitoring across all districts. 
(Para 3.17.13 of the Report) 
Action Taken 
1.22.5 FSSAI provides technical and financial support to States/UTs for procurement, 
deployment and to bear the operational costs of MFTLs.  Presently, 519 MFTLs have been 
sanctioned which covers all the districts in surveillance activities.  A dedicated team at the 
FSSAI HQs plays a pivotal role for effective coordination and management of MFTLs via 
real time monitoring of MFTL/FSW through ―ded icated online portal‖ 
(http://fsw.fssai.gov.in/) and ―live GPS tracking system‖. 
 
Recommendations/Observations 
1.22.6 Furthermore, the Committee notes that not all States have separate food safety 
departments, which could lead to inconsistencies in implementation, however, most of the 
States have Food Safety Appellate Tribunals.  The Committee, therefore, holds that separate 
Food Safety Departments should be promoted. The Committee strongly emphasizes the 
Department t o encourage the remaining states/UTs to establish separate Food Safety 
Departments. Providing guidance and examples of the benefits of separate departments, as 
well as possible assistance during the transition, will help to increase the number of states 
with separate departments. 
(Para 3.17.15 of the Report) 
Action Taken 
1.22.7 All State/UTs have Food Safety Commissioners headed by Senior bureaucrats. 
However, 10 States/UTs have a separate Food Safety Department, while the others have a 
combined department with drugs. Details are provided in Annexure -8. The Food 
Commissioner is the head of Food Safety of the State and UT. Under Section 30 of FSS Act 
2006, the Food Commissioner is appointed by the State Government for efficient 
implementation of food safety and standards and other requirements laid down under the Act.  
The recommendation for having an independent Food Safety Department will be forwarded to 
the Chief Secretary of the States for further necessary action at their end.

(ii) Environmental clearance (EC) - The process was started on 5th March 2024 and matter

Section8(b) of the EIA Notification, 2006. Accordingly, fresh application is under 
submission to MoEF & CC.

with Forest Department due to stay on felling of trees without p ermission of Hon‘ble 
Delhi High Court Order in CM Application No.45986/2024. Hon‘ble High Court of 
Delhi is hearing the matter regarding the said permission for AIIMS, New Delhi for 
Critical Care Block Project vide CM Application No.62212/2024. The last he aring was 
held on 8th April 2025 and the judgement is still awaited.

Recommendations/Observations 
1.23.7 The Committee notes the AIIMS, New Delhi's efforts to address patient overcrowding 
and improve services which include providing temporary shelter ("Ashray"), enhancing 
patient convenience through various facilities, streamlining appointments via online sys tems, 
expanding infrastructure with new specialty blocks, and offering accommodation through 
"Vishram Sadans." Additionally, the Committee desires the AIIMS, Delhi to expand the 
"Ashray" initiative by providing more permanent, weather -protected waiting are as with 

47 
 
adequate seating, clean washrooms, and access to affordable food options. This will 
significantly reduce the hardship faced by patients and their attendants‘ awaiting 
appointments. However, significant challenges remain, particularly with long wait  times for 
walk-in OPD appointments and substantial staff vacancies across all cadres. The Committee, 
therefore, recommends that the Institute implements a robust, real -time digital queue 
management system for walk-in OPD appointments. This system should provide patients with 
estimated wait times via SMS or a mobile app, allowing them to wait in designated, 
comfortable areas rather than overnight outside. Keeping in view the fact of long waiting time 
for urgent treatment of even critical patients, the Commi ttee desires that Department should 
explore the possibilities of construction of another AIIMS in NCR region close to New Delhi. 
(Para 3.18.9 of the Report) 
Action Taken 
1.23.8 On the Committee‘s observations and recommendations regarding long wait times, 
the ―Ashray‖ initiative and the need for an improved queue management system, the ongoing 
efforts are as under: - 
 
(i) The ―Ashray‖ initiative, which began in late December 2024, has significantly 
increased its capacity to address the needs of patients and the ir attendants. Originally 
designed to accommodate 80 patients, the initiative now serves approximately  225 
patients daily. This corresponds to around 500 individuals, including  patients, 
attendants and relatives, who are provided with a weather- protected waiting area, free 
food distribution and access to washroom facilities. 
 
(ii) The Institute is actively working on expanding the ―Ashray‖ initiative further. Plans 
are underway to establish additional weather -protected waiting are as in key locations 
such as near Gate No. 2, Masjid Moth Campus, the Mother  & Child Block, and other 
areas of AIIMS, New Delhi. These new areas will provide adequate seating, clean 
washrooms, and aﬀordable food options, helping to reduce the hardship faced by 
patients and their attendants. 
(iii) The Institute has expanded sheltered waiting areas especially in close proximity to the 
Masjid Moth Campus for patients and their attendants and developed covered waiting 
halls for patients and their attendants. 
(iv) To assist patients and their attendants, AIIMS has Vishram Sadans at various 
locations within the premises of the Institute with provision of over 1150 beds 
(including at Jhajjar campus), facilities for clean drinking water, mobile charging 
stations, cafeteria and restroom etc. 
(v) AIIMS, New Delhi has implemented a digital queue management system for 
scheduled appointments, which has significantly improved the flow of patients. 
AIIMS has also implemented online appointments through the ORS portal and a token 
system based on a first -come, first-served basis to manage patient flow for walk -ins. 
50% slots for online appointments, 30% slots for walk-ins and 20% slots for cross-
referrals. 
 

48 
 
(vi) AIIMS is exploring various options for better communicating real-time estimated wait 
times for walk-in appointments, particularly through digital platforms such as SMS or 
mobile apps. This will allow patients to wait in designated, comfortable areas rather 
than outside overnight, thus improving their experience. 
(vii) New specialty blocks like Burns & Plastic Sur gery Block, Surgical Block, New RAK  
OPD Block, Mother & Child Block, National Centre for Ageing and vertical  
expansion of JPNATC have been commissioned to alleviate overcrowding and 
increase OPD and IPD capacity.  As such, in order to cater to increasing pa tient load, 
AllMS Delhi has added 1000 beds to its inpatient  bed strength, and plans to add 
another 150 beds of the Critical Care Centre. In  order to decongest the OPD, the New 
RAK OPD has been opened. Similarly,  to decongest the main AIIMS campus by 
relocating all OPD services to the Masjid Moth Campus and to augment the OPD 
capacity for  CN Centre, Dr. BRAIRCH and Dr. RP Centre, new Super Specialty 
Block is under process for appraisal. 
(viii) The Institute has improved accessibility for disabled patients by optimized use of 
ramps and escalators and adhering to Universal Design Features at newly 
commissioned blocks of New RAK OPD, installed multilingual signage across the 
campus and commissioned accessible e -shuttle services with multiple pick -up/drop 
off points around the campus periphery. 
(ix) The Institute has deployed trained staﬀ (Patient Care Coordinators) and sensitized 
guards in assisting the patients in navigating the campus eﬀectively. An intra -hospital 
navigation system is also being implemented to ensure  easy access to various patient 
care areas. 
(x) The Institute has incorporated telemedicine facilities for preliminary and remote 
consultations. 
(xi) For manpower, additional posts for doctors and staﬀ have been created to handle 
increased patient loads efficiently . The process for filling up vacant Faculty posts has 
already been started and advertisement for vacant faculty posts is currently live.The 
NORCET (Nursing Officers Recruitment Common Entrance Test) is being conducted 
twice a year to fill up vacancies in the nursing cadre. 
(xii) In order to get laboratory tests done and to make reports available to patients in time 
the core lab with a capacity of>2,00,000/day has been established and digitized 
through NIC to provide test reports through the ORS software in the mo bile of the 
patient as a text message. Moreover, charges of all investigations and interventions 
less than Rs.300 have been abolished. Due to the high load of patients requiring MRI, 
the service timings of the existing MRIs have been increased to 24*7 and another 
machine is being replaced and a new one installed in the National Centre for Ageing. 
(xiii) Information desks manned by Patient Care Coordinators at multiple points have been 
set up. 
(xiv) The Institute has strengthened the availability of generic drugs through in - house 

49 
 
pharmacies dispensed through easily accessible AMRIT Pharmacy counters as well as 
Jan Aushadhi Counter. 6 outlets of HLL and 1 of Jan Aushadhi are available at 
different places in AllMS. 
(xv) Implementedeffectivetriagingofpatientsarrivingattheemergencyarea. 
(xvi) Established the JPNATC (dedicated center) which is strategically located at the Ring -
Road for immediate care of trauma and road accident patients. Dynamic monitoring 
and optimization of deployment of staﬀ to managespikes in demand in emergency and 
other critical care areas. 
(xvii) Developed multi-level basement parking facilities across newly constructed blocks at 
Masjid Moth Campus to accommodate more vehicles seamlessly. 
(xviii) Encouraged t he use of public transportation and e -shuttle services by improving 
connectivity to nearby transit hubs (Metro stations and Bus stops). 
(xix) Continuous endeavour towards improved cleanliness across the campus, including 
washrooms and other public areas. 
(xx) Implemented regular audits and feedback mechanisms using simple SOPs to maintain 
hygiene standards.Increased transparency by commissioning a live/regularly updated

1.26 National Institute of Mental Health and Neuro Sciences (NIMHANS), Bengaluru 
Recommendations/Observations 
1.26.1 The Committee observes that NIMHANS demonstrates strong fund utilization, 
spending the full allocated amount in FY 2023 -24. It has been submitted that the budgetary 
fluctuations occurred in FY 2024 -25 due to the Model Code of Conduct (MCC) imposed 
during election which highlights potential planning issues. The Committee, in this connection, 
would like to highlight that the imposition of MCC during election is only for a certain period 
in the whole duration of a fiscal year. The Institute must have been aware of the imposition of 
MCC in advance and should plan the execution of projects and utilization of funds 
accordingly. The Committee believes that the Department should promote enhanced 
Budgetary Planning and Mitigation Strategies among the Institutions under its purview. The 
Committee, therefore, recommends that NIMHANS implement a more robust budgetary 
planning process that accounts for foreseeable disruptions, such as the Model Code of 
Conduct. Specifically, NIMHANS should develop contingency plans for procurement and 
project execution to minimize the impact of such events. This includes creating a detailed 
timeline for major projects, prioritizing essential procurements, and establishing cl ear 
communication channels with relevant authorities to anticipate and address potential delays. 
By proactive planning, NIMHANS can ensure consistent and efficient utilization of allocated 
funds, maintaining the continuity of vital projects and services. 
(Para 3.21.3 of the Report) 

52 
 
Action Taken 
1.26.2 The observations/recommendation of the Committee are duly noted.  NIMHANS is 
committed to implement a more robust budgetary planning process to account for foreseeable 
disruptions.

in both FY 2023 -24 and FY 2024 -25, indicating potential issues with financial plannin g and 
execution; and the actual expenditure consistently falling below the revised estimates, raising 
concerns about the institute's ability to utilize allocated funds effectively. The Committee, 
therefore, recommends that NEIGRIHMS should formulate a robu st financial planning, 
execute that with allocated fund within stipulated time under a monitoring system to minimize 
discrepancies between budgeted and revised estimates by regular reviews of financial 
performance, and ultimately, undertake proactive measures to address any potential shortfalls. 
The Committee is of the firm view that once the institute develops a clear strategy for optimal 
utilization of allocated funds within the stipulated timeframe by detailed project plans, 
timelines, and regular progress reports, the desirable outcome will automatically flow.   
(Para 3.22.2 of the Report) 
Action Taken

1.30 Lokpriya Gopinath Bordoloi Regional Institute of Mental Health (LGBRIMH), 
Tezpur 
Recommendations/Observations 
1.30.1 The Committee observes with concern that the core issue relating to the institute is 
reduced budget allocation of Rs. 60 crore (further reduced to Rs. 50 crore at RE stage) in FY 
2024-25 in comparison to RE 2023 -24 of Rs. 72.53 crore and allocation of insufficient funds 
to the tune of Rs. 65 crore in FY 2025 -26. Needless to say that this reduced budget a llocation 
threatens the institute's ability to cover essential operational expenses. The Committee, 
therefore, recommends an immediate and thorough review of the Institute's budget allocation 
for FY 2025 -26. Given the institute's documented essential finan cial requirements, including 
salaries, pensions, and critical operational expenses, the proposed allocation of Rs. 65 crore is 
deemed insufficient as stated in submission to the Committee. The Committee, therefore, 
strongly advises the Ministry of Finance to enhance the budget allocation to adequately meet 
the institute's financial requirements. All such financial estimation and enhancement should 
be based on a detailed analysis of the institute's expenditure patterns and projected operational 
costs, ensuring uninterrupted service delivery and compliance with financial obligations. This 
action is critical to prevent operational disruptions and maintain the institute's ability to 
provide essential healthcare services. 
 
(Para 3.25.4 of the Report) 
 
Action Taken 
1.30.2 It is stated that the matter of additional funds will be taken up with the Ministry of 
Finance in the Revised Estimate for the financial year 2025 -26 as per the demand. The 
institute has been instructed to increase the pace of expenditure so a s to justify the need to 
MoF of increasing Budget at the Revised Estimates stage.  
Recommendations/Observations 
1.30.3 The Committee further observes the significant number of vacant positions, 
particularly in Group A category. Moreover, there exist substantial vacancies of the post of 
Senior Residents (Contractual position). The Committee has also been apprised of the 
institute‘s struggle to handle a large patient load due to staffing shortages. The Committee 
recommends the expedited approval and implementation of the institute's proposals for pay 
scale upgrades and the creation/revival of essential posts. Addressing the c ritical vacancies, 
particularly in Group A positions and Senior Resident roles, is paramount. The Committee, 
therefore, recommends that the Department should prioritize the approval of the proposed 19 
new super-specialty posts and the revival of the 7 exis ting posts. Additionally, the committee 
recommends that the institute, in conjunction with the Department, create a focused 
recruitment strategy that highlights the advantages of working at the institute, along with the 
approved pay scale improvements, to attract qualified candidates. A specific recruitment drive 
should be initiated to fill up the senior resident positions as quickly as possible. This 

56 
 
combined approach of improved pay and targeted recruitment will help to fill up the critical 
staffing gaps, thereby enhancing the institute's capacity to deliver quality patient care. 
(Para 3.25.5 of the Report) 
Action Taken 
1.30.4 The Comments of the Hon‘ble Committee are noted.  The proposal for the creation of 
19 essential category posts and revival of 7 existing posts has been taken up with DoE. The 
proposal for upgradation for 5 posts and recruitment of Group A and Senior Residents 
initiated.  
National Health Mission 
1.31 Budgetary Allocation 
Recommendations/Observations 
1.31.1 The Committee recommends the Department that NHM should prioritize increased 
funding and resources for these high -risk areas. Interventions should include strengthening 
facility-based newborn care, improv ing access to skilled birth attendants, enhancing prenatal 
and postnatal care, and addressing nutritional deficiencies in women and children. A feedback 
loop must be put in place to measure the effectiveness of the interventions and allow for quick 
adjustments. 
(Para 4.2.5 of the Report) 
Action Taken 
1.31.2 The child births attended by Skilled Health Personnel i.e. Skilled Birth Attendants 
(SBA) has improved from 81.4% in NFHS -4 (2015 -16) to 89.4% in NFHS -5 (2019 -21). 
Further, the births attended by the skilled health personnel are 98.4% as per the HMIS in 
2024-25. Skilled Birth Attendants (SBAs), which include midwives and community health 
workers, receive essential skill upgradation, knowledge enhancement, and technical training 
to ensure they provide high -quality maternal care services. This comprehensive t raining 
encompasses various programs such as Skilled Birth Attendant (SBA) trainings, Skill labs 
training, DAKSH training, DAKSHTA training, and Basic Emergency Obstetric Care 
Training (BEmONC), all designed to equip them with the necessary expertise for t heir vital 
role.The SBA trained ANM posted at Sub -Centre/Ayushman Arogya Mandir (AAM) 
designated as Delivery Points (DPs) are entitled to receive Performance Based Incentives 
(PBIs) in the 184 High Priority Districts (HPDs). The Antenatal Care (ANC) covera ge has 
improved from 83.5% in NFHS -4 (2015 -16) to 93.9% in NFHS -5 (2019 -21).  The ANC 
coverage is 98.1% as per the HMIS in 2024-25. Similarly, the mothers who received postnatal 
care from health personnel within 2 days of delivery has also improved from 62 .4% in NFHS-
4 (2015-16) to 81.7% in NFHS-5 (2019-21). The MoHFW assesses the implementation of the 
interventions including antenatal and post -natal care under the National Health Mission 
(NHM) by undertaking periodic Regional/ State level review meetings, field visits in various 
States/Union Territories (UTs), PIP Appraisals, Mid Term Review (MTR) and Common 

57 
 
Review Missions (CRMs). The Ministry of Health and Family Welfare implements 
Reproductive, Maternal, Newborn, Child, Adolescent Health and Nutrition (R MNCAH+N) 
strategy in a life cycle approach under National Health Mission (NHM), which includes 
interventions to address nutrition concerns among women and children across the country, 
including high risk areas based on the Annual Program Implementation Pla n (APIP) 
submitted by States / UTs, as placed below: - 
 Nutrition Rehabilitation Centres (NRCs) are set up at public health facilities to provide in-
patient medical and nutritional care to children under 5 years suffering from Severe Acute 
Malnourishment (SAM) with medical complications. In addition to curative care, special 
focus is given on correction of micronutrient deficiencies through supplementation of 
potassium, magnesium, zinc, iron and vitamin supplements and on improving the skills 
for mother and caregivers on complete age-appropriate caring and feeding practices. 
 Anaemia Mukt Bharat (AMB) Programme is implemented to reduce anaemia among six 
beneficiaries age group - children (6-59 months), children (5-9 years), adolescents (10-19 
years), pregnan t and lactating women and in women of reproductive age group (15 -49 
years) in life cycle approach through implementation of six interventions - Prophylactic 
Iron and Folic Acid Supplementation, deworming among children 1 -19 years and 
pregnant women, Intensi fied Behaviour Change Communication (BCC) Campaign, 
Provision of IFA fortified food in government funded public health program and 
addressing non -nutritional causes of anaemia in endemic pockets with special focus on 
malaria, haemoglobinopathies and fluorosis, via robust institutional mechanism.  
 Mothers‘ Absolute Affection (MAA) Programme is implemented to improve 
breastfeeding coverage in children which includes early initiation of breastfeeding and 
exclusive breastfeeding for first six months followed by  counselling on age -appropriate 
complementary feeding practices. 
 Lactation Management Centres: Lactation Management Units (LMU) are facilities 
established to provide lactation support to mothers to facilitate expression of mother‘s 
own milk and Comprehensi ve Lactation Management Centres (CLMC) are established to 
ensure availability of safe, pasteurized Donor Human Milk for feeding of sick, preterm 
and low birth weight babies admitted in Neonatal Intensive Care Units and Special 
Newborn Care Units. 
 Under National Deworming Day (NDD) albendazole tablets are administered in a single 
fixed day approach via schools and Anganwadi centres in two rounds (February and 
August) to reduce the soil transmitted helminth (STH) infestation among all children and 
adolescents (1-19 years). 
 Under Bi-annual Vitamin A supplementation Programme, all children age 6 –59 months 
are administered Vitamin A dose every 6 months in two rounds during Village Health 
Sanitation and Nutrition Days or in campaign approach.  
 Village Health Sanitation and Nutrition Days (VHSNDs) are observed for provision of 
maternal and child health services and creating awareness on maternal and child care 
including nutrition in convergence with Ministry of Women and Child Development. 
 
Recommendations/Observations 
1.31.3 The Committee having micro-analysis of the identified challenges (infrastructure gaps, 
human resource shortages, drug and diagnostic deficiencies, service delivery issues, urban -
rural divide, and fragmented digital portals) and the governme nt's current strategies (NQAS, 
PM-ABHIM, HR incentives, free drug and diagnostic initiatives, tele -consultation, and 

58 
 
ABDM), strongly recommends the urgent and prioritized establishment of a unified, real -
time, and interoperable national health data platfor m. This platform should integrate all 
existing health portals, including HMIS, NFHS, ABDM, and others, to provide a 
comprehensive, longitudinal view of health infrastructure, human resources, drug and 
diagnostic availability, service delivery metrics, and patient outcomes across all levels (rural 
and urban). This unified system will enable data -driven decision-making, facilitate efficient 
resource allocation, streamline service delivery, enhance monitoring of program 
implementation, and ultimately bridge the urban-rural divide by providing equitable access to 
healthcare information and services. The Committee believes that the implementation of these 
steps would maximize the impact of ongoing programs and enhance the credibility of public 
hospitals. 
(Para 4.2.9 of the Report) 
Action Taken 
1.31.4 Ministry of Health & Family Welfare is developing a National Health Aggregator 
Application. This application aims to provide a unified platform for healthcare providers, 
allowing for streamlined data capture, secure  data exchange, and real -time synchronization, 
all while adhering to national health data standards.  
1.31.5 Key features of this new application includes: - 
Centralized Data Management the need for entering data multiple times into different 
programme applications. 
Unified User Interface: Offers a single interface for managing program -related data and 
searching patient information across various programmes. 
Comprehensive Patient View: Provides a complete historical view of patients‘ data, including 
disease, medication, and treatment timelines, for all integrated programmes.  
Data Reuse: Common data parameters can auto-populate across different health programmes. 
Integration with existing system: Users secure API integration for smooth data exchange with 
current health portals and databases. 
Offline Data Capture: Community Health Offices (CHOs) can collect data offline, which 
automatically synchronizes once connectivity is restored.  
The goal is for this open API -based platform to become a major national-level integration 
platform for the Government of India.
