1.32 Strengthening of State Drug Regulatory System 
Recommendations/Observations 
1.32.1 The Committee recommends the urgent implementation of a proactive, structured 
engagement strategy with all States and Union Territories well in advance of each fiscal year's 
budget cycle to enhance the demand for the Strengthening of State Drug Regulatory System 
(SSDRS) scheme. This strategy  should focus on providing clear guidelines, technical 
assistance, and regular communication to ensure that States understand the SSDRS scheme's 
requirements, and to assist States in developing comprehensive, eligible proposals. This 
process should also in volve regular monitoring of fund utilization by states, providing early 
feedback and support to address any bottlenecks hindering the 75% utilization requirement. 
Given the scheme's demand-driven nature and the observed pattern of reduced allocations due 
to insufficient or non -compliant state proposals, actively facilitating state participation will 
mitigate the risk of underutilization and ensure the crucial strengthening of drug regulatory 
systems, particularly as the scheme's extension is being considere d. Specifically, the 
department should establish a dedicated team to conduct workshops, provide templates for 
proposals, and track state progress, thereby ensuring that the allocated funds are effectively 
utilized to improve drug quality and accessibility across the nation. 
(Para 4.3.3 of the Report) 
Action Taken 
1.32.2 The recommendations of the Hon‘ble Committee are noted.To monitor utilization, 
physical progress and resolving outstanding issues, regular state visits are conducted. Periodic 
review meetings are also held with State Drug Controllers/State Health Administration for 
monitoring the progress and resolution of issues. States are also being motivated to utilize the 
funds to upgrade their infrastructure. To further improve capacity of the St ates, CDSCO is 
regularly conducting training programs. 
Recommendations/Observations 
1.32.3 To ensure a consistently high standard of drug regulation across India, mirroring the 
success achieved in vaccine regulation, the Committee recommends the immediate 
implementation of a comprehensive, nationwide, and phased "Medicines Regulatory Maturity 
Enhancement Program." This program should be modelled after the WHO NRA re -
benchmarking process used for vaccines, involving a detailed scientific review of state and 
union territory drug regulatory systems against the WHO Global Benchmarking Tool. It 
should include: i) mandatory, challenging -mode state audits conducted by a central team of 

61 
 
international and national experts, focusing on the quality, safety, and efficac y of medicines, 
APIs, and formulations; ii) targeted regulatory capacity building and infrastructure upgrades 
in states/UTs, informed by the audit findings and stakeholder consultations; iii) the 
establishment of clear, time -bound milestones for achieving and maintaining higher maturity 
levels, with progressive implementation based on the turnover of the manufacturers as done 
with GMP revisions; iv) continuous professional development and training for state drug 
regulatory officials, leveraging existing CDS CO training programs and expanding to 
incorporate international best practices; and v) the integration of the Online National Drug 
Licensing System (ONDLS) with the benchmarking process to track and monitor progress, 
thereby ensuring uniformity and efficie ncy in drug regulation across the country. This 
systematic approach, coupled with stringent enforcement measures like risk -based inspections 
and amended Drugs Rules, will elevate the entire drug regulatory framework to international 
standards, safeguarding  public health and reinforcing India's position as a global 
pharmaceutical leader. 
(Para 4.3.5 of the Report) 
1.32.4 The Committee is of the view that India being pharmaceutical hub of the world, the 
same level of benchmarking/ capacity building of States/UTs in the field of medicines/drugs 
is the need of the hour. This requires regulatory capacity building up-gradation of the States in 
medicines/drugs/API /formulations. The Committee welcomes the launching of Online 
National Drug Licensing System (ONDLS) portal for submission of applications and issuance 
of licenses by the States through this portal. 
(Para 4.3.6 of the Report) 
Action Taken 
1.32.5 WHO assess the National Regulatory Authority (NRA) against various benchmarking 
tools Global Benchmarking Tools (GBT) viz. National Regulatory System (RS), Registration 
and Marketing Authorization (MA), Vigilance (VI), Market Surveillance and Control (MC), 
Licensing Establishments (LI), Regulatory Inspection (RI), Laboratory Testing (LT), Clinical 
Trials Over sight (CT) and NRA Lot Release (LR). WHO assess the National Regulatory 
Authority (NRA) against the regulatory system including state regulators to achieve the 
benchmarks.   
1.32.6 In order to meet the global requirements and best regulatory practices, Ministry of 
Health and Family Welfare and Central Drugs Standard Control Organization (CDSCO) has 
taken the following measures: - 
 Central Government has amended the Drugs Rules 1945 vide G.S.R. 922 (E) dated 
28.12.2023 to revise the schedule M to the said rules related to Good Manufacturing 
Practices and requirements of premises, plant and equipment for pharmaceutical 
products. Revis ed Schedule M has become effective for the drug manufacturers with 
turnover > Rs. 250 crores from 29.06.2024. However, for manufacturers having turnover 
of less than Rs. 250 Cr, extension of the timeline for implementation have been granted 
till 31st Decem ber, 2025 vide G.S.R. 127(E) dated 11.02.2025 provided the units 

62 
 
registers themselves by submitting an application in Form 'A' through the Online 
National Drugs Licensing System (ONDLS) portal by 10th May 2025. 
 Online National Drugs Licensing System (ONDLS ) portal has been developed by 
CDAC in coordination with States for processing of application and online licensing of 
Sale/Manufacturing license/ Blood centre applications on the ONDLS Portal. ONDLS 
portal is a single window platform for online processing of various applications 
submitted by the applicants for issuance of manufacturing and sales licenses including 
Blood Banks, and other certificates like COPP, GMP, WHO -GMP, Market Standing 
certificate etc., and post approval changes. At present, total of 34  states and UTs are live 
on the ONDLS platform. Out of 33 states, 15 states are using multiple types of 
applications (Blood Centre, Manufacturing, and also for sales). ONDLS has helped in 
establishment of uniformity w.r.t the requirement of submission of d ocuments for 
different type of applications as well as issuance of licenses/ permissions throughout 
India and in uniform administration of the provisions of the Act and Rules by utilizing 
the latest technologies tools including e -governance for ensuring ma jor progress in 
regulatory system strengthening activities in States/UTs. 
 In order to assess the regulatory compliance of drug manufacturing premises in the 
country, as per Drugs & Cosmetics Act, 1940 and Rules thereunder especially to the 
requirements of Good Manufacturing Practices (GMP), CDSCO, in collaboration with 
state regulators, initiated risk-based inspections (RBI) of drug manufacturing and testing 
firms in December 2022. As of now, 905 units have been inspected, resulting in 694 
actions being tak en. These actions include Stop Production Orders (SPO), Stop Testing 
Orders (STO), license suspensions/cancellations, warning letters, and showcase notices, 
depending on the severity of non -compliance. This initiative has provided valuable 
insights into th e ground reality of manufacturing practices and has led to relevant 
corrective actions, resulting in noticeable improvements in the regulatory framework. 
 In this regard, Checklist benchmarks have been provided to the joint inspection teams 
for uniformity i n inspections and reporting. Accordingly, the inspection team conducts 
the inspection and submit their report along with findings and recommendations.  
 In order to ensure the effective regulation of medical products including drugs, medical 
devices and cosmetics, CDSCO is developing a State Drug Regulatory Index to improve 
the standards of drug regulation across the country and enhance its global standing. The 
primary objective of the Index is to enhance the effectiveness and uniformity of drug 
regulatory m echanisms across the country by strengthening drug safety surveillance, 
promoting accountability, fostering collaboration, enhancing resource utilization. 
 MoHFW is implementing a Centrally Sponsored Scheme to strengthen and upgrade the 
States Drug Regulatory System and laboratory infrastructure in the country at an outlay 
of Rs. 850 crore. So far, funds amounting to Rs. 756.56 crore have been released as 
Central Share to 34 States/UTs leading to construction of 17 New Drug Testing Labs 
and up-gradation of 24 existing labs in various States/UTs. 

63 
 
 Central Drugs Testing Lab (CDTL) Indore was inaugurated on 07.01.2024 and CDTL 
Bhubaneswar Lab was inaugurated on 29.10.2024. As on date now CDSCO has 09 
central drugs testing lab for testing of drugs, cosmetics & Med ical Devices (Mumbai, 
Chennai, Hyderabad, Kasauli, Kolkata, Chandigarh, Guwahati, Indore, Bhubaneshwar). 
The development of new labs has reduced the sample burden on exiting labs and has 
facilitated the pharmaceutical industry.  
As part of further strengthening of existing Labs the new additions are: Microbiology lab 
at CDTL, Hyderabad and new annexe building for Guwahati are under process of 
establishment. 
 CDSCO has made 21 agreements with regulators of other countries on co -operation in 
the field of regul ation of pharmaceutical products including sharing of regulatory 
information. CDSCO has started a training academy for enhancing skills of the central 
and State regulators on emerging topics and updates on Drug regulations. Experts from 
the CDSCO, State and the Industries are invited to deliver talks to the participants. 
 Central government is providing regular residential, regional training and workshops to 
officials of CDSCO and State Drug Regulatory Authorities on Good Manufacturing 
Practices. In the Fina ncial Year 2023 -24 and 2024 -25 CDSCO has trained 22854 and 
20551 persons respectively. 
 Drug Inspectors from the CDSCO and the State Drug Control Department accompany 
inspections or audits carried out by international regulatory agency teams in the capacity  
of observers. 
 CDSCO has become affiliate member of International Medical Device Regulatory 
Forum (IMDRF). IMDRF is a global group of medical device regulators working 
together to accelerate harmonization and convergence of international medical device 
regulations.  
 CDSCO has been developed and implemented SUGAM lab portal which has provisions 
Online Tracking of samples and updating the status of prosecution cases etc. Sugam Lab 
platform automates critical processes and manages the NSQ database, thereby 
empowering regulatory authorities to enforce the Drugs & Cosmetics Act of 1940 
effectively. It has also significantly enhanced post -marketing surveillance, bolstering 
India's reputation in health regulation. 
 On 17.11.2022, the Drugs Rules, 1945 were amended v ide G.S.R. 823(E) which has 
come into force from 1st of August, 2023 providing that the manufacturers of top 300 
brands of drug formulation products, as specified in Schedule H2, shall print or affix Bar 
Code or Quick Response Code on its primary packaging  label or, in case of inadequate 
space in primary package label, on the secondary package label that store data or 
information legible with software application to facilitate authentication. 
 On 18.01.2022, the Drugs Rules, 1945 were amended vide G.S.R. 20 (E) providing that 
every Active Pharmaceutical Ingredient (bulk drug) manufactured or imported in India 

64 
 
shall bear Quick Response Code on its label at each level of packaging that store data or 
information readable with software application to facilitate t racking and tracing. The 
stored data or information shall include the minimum particulars including unique 
product identification code, Batch Number, Manufacturing date, Expiry Date etc. 
 On 11.02.2020, the Drugs Rules, 1945 were amended vide G.S.R. 101 (E) , providing 
that with effect from 01.03.2021, any marketer who sells or distributes any drug shall be 
responsible for quality of that drug as well as other regulatory compliances along with 
the manufacturer under these Rules. 
 The Drugs and Cosmetics Act, 1 940 was amended under Drugs & Cosmetics 
(Amendment) Act, 2008 to provide stringent penalties for manufacture of spurious and 
adulterated drugs. Certain offences have also been made cognizable and non-bailable. 
 States/ UTs have set up special Courts for tri al of offences under the Drugs and 
Cosmetics Act for speedy disposal.  
 To ensure efficacy of drugs, the Drugs and Cosmetics Rules, 1945 have been amended 
providing that applicant shall submit the result of bioequivalence study along with the 
application for grant of manufacturing license of oral dosage form of some drugs.  
 The Drugs and Cosmetics Rules, 1945 have been amended, making it mandatory that the 
applicants shall submit evidence of stability, safety of excipients etc. to the State 
Licensing Authority before grant of manufacturing license by the Authority.  
 The number of sanctioned posts in Central Drugs Standard Control Organization 
(CDSCO) has been significantly increased in last 10 years. 
1.32.7 Central regulator coordinates activities of State Drug Control Organizations and 
provides expert advice through the Drugs Consultative Committee (DCC) meetings held with 
State Drugs Controllers for uniformity in administration of the Drugs and Cosmetics Act. 
 
1.33 Functioning of Central Drugs Standard Control Organization (CDSCO) 
Recommendations/Observations 
1.33.1 The Committee is having the concerns that centralization of authority at any level may 
result in creating a situation for arbitrary/ discretionary decision making. This may have an 
adverse impression that India is keen on over regulation since last two years to create a 
business of licensing rather than making it a manufacturing powerhouse for medical devices. 
The Department should ensure faster documentation processes including reduction of  human 
interface to facilitate the growth of medical devices industry in the country. The Committee is 
of the view that the Department should promote start -ups in medical devices by streamlining 
and overhauling the licensing system and preventing the delib erate delays by following a 
transparent process with minimum room for discretion/human inference. The Committee 
believes that these steps would result into India becoming a hub of the medical devices‘ 
manufacturing and alone generating an over one trillion-dollar industry in medical devices. 
(Para 4.4.8 of the Report) 

65 
 
Action Taken 
1.33.2 The Medical Devices Rules, 2017 have been comprehensively revised the medical 
device regulations with emphasis on ease of doing business while ensuring the safety and 
efficacious quality of medical devices. CDSCO launched a portal named MedTech Mitra in 
coordination with ICMR, CDSCO and NITI AYOG in December 2023 to give handhold 
support to the innovator, Manufacturer, Start -up on regulatory clearance, development of 
clinical study plan etc. in Medical Device Domain. More than 344 innovators have been 
provided supports so far in development and approval of the Medical Devices.  There is a 
delegation of authority for issues of licenses for medical devices. Licenses for Class A and B 
medical devices are issued by State Licensing authority concerned and Class C and D by 
CDSCO.  
1.33.3 Various measures have been taken to streamline processing of applications as under: - 
(i) CDSCO has implemented MD Online which is an online portal for submission of 
the various applications related to manufacturing and import of medical devices and 
their clinical investigation. MD Online provides paperless  and completely faceless 
grant of various clearances in a time-bound manner. 
 
(ii) In accordance with MDR 2017, phase -wise regulation of all medical devices has 
been implemented to ensure the safety, efficacy and quality of medical devices.  
 
(iii) Initially all Class A medical devices were under the licensing regime. Chapter III B 
was introduced in the Rules for registration instead of licensing of class A non -
sterile and non -measuring medical devices vide GSR 777 (E) dated 14 October 
2022. This has eliminated th e licensing requirements for a large part of Class A 
devices. A license is not needed for this category. 
 
(iv) To address such issues relating to manpower and capacity of handling medical 
device applications, a Medical Device Vertical has been created. Recruitm ent Rules 
have been published vide GSR 689 (E) dated 06.11.2024 for the post of Drugs 
Inspector (Medical Devices), Assistant Drugs Controller (I), Deputy Drugs 
Controller (I), Joint Drugs Controller (I), and Additional Drugs Controller (I). The 
Recruitment process has been initiated to fill such positions. 
 
(v) The Rules also provide for the registration of Medical Devices Testing Laboratories 
which are responsible for testing medical devices on behalf of manufacturers. 
Currently, 73 laboratories are registered  with CDSCO for this purpose. Also, 
CDSCO has registered 14 notified bodies to perform their duties and functions 
concerning Class A and Class B medical devices. 

66 
 
 
(vi) Various guidance documents, Frequently Asked Questions (FAQ), specimen 
proforma for submission of applications, and checklists for various applications have 
been published for transparency and ease of document submission by the applicants. 
 
(vii) Trainings are conducted on the regulatory process and stakeholder meetings are held 
periodically. In the Financial Year 2023-24 and 2024-25 CDSCO has trained 22854 
and 20551 persons respectively. Further, CDSCO is also deputing officers for 
international training program/workshop with aim to upgrade knowledge on the best 
regulatory practices being followed by  stringent regulatory authorities across the 
Globe and to facilitate sharing of information with Indian medical devices regulation 
for harmonization. 
 
(viii) Dedicated public relations offices are established in CDSCO HQ and zonal offices 
with a mandate to act as  an interface between CDSCO and its stakeholders, guide 
stakeholders on regulatory pathway including startup/new entrepreneurs, for 
disposal of grievances of stakeholders by addressing issues including at the level of 
Drugs Controller General of India. 
 
(ix) Keeping in view the focus of Government on ease of doing business in the country, 
Drug Controller General of India (DCGI) holds open walk -in meetings with 
stakeholders twice a week. These meetings are held twice a week. During these 
sessions, the DCGI addres ses the problems, complaints, grievances and suggestions 
of the stakeholders, aiming for swift resolution. More than 238 stakeholders have 
taken benefit of the process in last 14 months, since it started. 
 
(x) The CDSCO is also conducting interactive meetings with the industry association 
from time to time to discuss issue and challenges of the industry for its remedial 
measure. Six such interactive meetings were conducted by DCG(I) during the last 
two years. Further, over 180 meetings with State Drugs Controll er and inter -
ministerial meetings were conducted by CDSCO on various technical matters related 
to Medical Devices sector. 
1.33.4 CDSCO has also organized various awareness workshops prior to and post 
notification on Revision of Schedule M during the year 2 023-2024 and 2024 -2025 across 
India for Pharma manufacturers through offline as well as online mode. A total of 21720 and 
12522 industry representatives respectively participated in the training programs. India has 
been accepted as an affiliate member of I MDRF in 2024 which is a collaborative group of 
global medical device regulators dedicated to accelerating the harmonization and convergence 

67 
 
of international medical device regulations, based on a detailed study of process of the Indian 
NRA. This will give an impetus to the effort to make India a manufacturing power house for 
medical devices. 
1.34 Tertiary Care Programs (TCPs) 
Recommendations/Observations 
1.34.1 The Committee is of the opinion that the BE for 2025 -26, amounting to Rs. 483.54 
crore, shows a substantial increase, indicating a commitment to these programs, however, this 
increased allocation will be wasted if the fund utilization issues are not addressed. The 
Committee recommends a detailed, program -specific review of the factors contributing to  the 
significant underutilization of funds. This review should include analyzing the specific 
bottlenecks in fund disbursement and program implementation at the state level; conducting 
on-site assessments of program activities and infrastructure; implement ing a real -time 
monitoring system with clear performance indicators to track fund utilization and program 
progress; providing targeted technical assistance and capacity -building support to states with 
low utilization rates; and specifically for the NMHP, t he Department should investigate why 
the money allocated for the Centers of Excellence, and PG programs are not being used. This 
proactive approach will ensure that the increased budget allocation for 2025 -26 translates into 
tangible improvements in service delivery. 
(Para 4.5.5 of the Report) 
Action Taken 
1.34.2 Regular handholding is done for the States/UTs to address the issues with regards to 
fulfilment of DoE conditions. Capacity Building exercises are also undertaken to encourage 
utilization of funds.  Detailed action schemes-wise to ensure budget allocation is at Para 4.5.4. 
Regular meetings are held with the CoEs to find out the reasons for non utilization of funds. 
Some of the major factors are pertaining to the conditions of DoE and SNA. UC is pend ing in 
many states, interest was not deposited in Consolidated fund of India, Corresponding State 
share has not been released, etc. 
Recommendations/Observations 
1.34.3 Given the importance of Tertiary care programs the Committee is of view that there 
has been considerable rise in mental health issues in the country. The Committee, 
accordingly, recommends the Department to take effective measures to increase the outreach 
of mental healthcare services and integrate psychological support services into primary health 
care. Further, considering the prevalence of non -communicable diseases, the Committee feels 
that concerted efforts must be taken to tackle lifestyle diseases. Th e budgetary allocation 
under NPCDCS must be increased considerably, the Department, however, must improve the 
absorption capacity under various Schemes. The Committee in its 139th Report had assessed 
the status of NPCDCS Scheme and noted that the screening  of common Cancer under 
NPCDCS program must be done nationwide as seriously as it should be to prevent the killer 
disease and unstable the fiscal foundation of Household management. The Committee had 
recommended that cancer must be dealt separately, with s pecific programmes and intended 

68 
 
budgetary allocation and enhanced and forensic inputs of early detection and prevention of 
cancer. and must not be grouped under other lifestyle diseases. The Committee reiterates its 
recommendation that the Department must devise a targeted plan for cancer control. 
(Para 4.5.7 of the Report) 
Action Taken 
1.34.4 The Government to India is implementing the National Mental health Programme 
(NMHP) in the country. The District Mental Health Programme (DMHP) component of the 
NMHP has been sanctioned for implementation in 767 districts for which support is provided 
to States/UTs through the National Health Mission (NHM). Facilities made available under 
DMHP at the Community Health Centre (CHC) and Primary Health Centre (PHCP) levels  for 
all including elderly, inter -alia, include outpatient services, assessment, counselling/psycho -
social interventions, continuing care and support to persons with severe mental disorder, 
drugs, outreach services, ambulance services etc. In addition to a bove services there is a 
provision of 10 bedded in -patient facility at the district level. Around 1,32,99,505 patients 
were catered in Mental Health OPDs in the FY 2024 -25. The Government is also taking steps 
to strengthen mental healthcare services at pri mary healthcare level. The Government has 
upgraded more than 1.75 lakh Sub Health Centres (SHCs) and Primary Health Centre (PHCs) 
to Ayushman Arogya Mandirs. Mental health services have been added in the packages of 
services under Comprehensive Primary Hea lth Care provided at these Ayushman Arogya 
Mandirs. Operational guidelines and training manuals for various cadre on Mental, 
Neurological, and substance use disorders (MNS) at Ayushman Arogya Mandirs have been 
released under the ambit of Ayushman Bharat. 
1.34.5 The Government has launched a ―National Tele Mental Health Programme‖ on 10th 
October, 2022, for further improve access to quality mental health counselling and care 
services in the country. As on 23.06.2025, 36 States/UTs have set up 53 Tele MANAS C ells 
and have started tele mental health services. More than 22,80,000 calls have been handled on 
the helpline number. The Government has also launched Tele MANAS Mobile Application 
on the occasion of World Mental Health Day -October 10, 2024. Tele -MANAS Mo bile 
Application has also been developed to provide support for mental health issues ranging from 
well-being to mental disorders. Under NPCDCS the programme focuses on strengthening 
infrastructure, developing human resources, promoting health awareness, an d conducting 
population-based screening for individuals aged 30 years and above for common NCDs like 
diabetes, hypertension, and three most common cancers along with ensuring early diagnosis 
and effective management. States are continually encouraged to ut ilize their allocated budgets 
under the NHM NCD component and are supported in requesting additional funds, as needed. 
Screening activities are being systematically strengthened to ensure greater coverage of the 
eligible population. Additionally, financial  support for NCD -related ailments is extended 
through schemes such as Pradhan Mantri Jan Arogya Yojana (PMJAY). 
1.34.6 To strengthen cancer care at the tertiary level, the Central Government is 
implementing the Strengthening of Tertiary Cancer Care Centres  Facilities scheme. Under 
this initiative, financial assistance is provided as a one -time grant of up to Rs.120 crore for 

69 
 
State Cancer Institutes (SCIs) and up to Rs.45 crore for Tertiary Cancer Care Centres 
(TCCCs), including the State share. As of now, 1 9 SCIs and 20 TCCCs have been approved. 
These institutions serve as critical hubs for advanced cancer care, diagnosis, research, and 
capacity building and are equipped with specialized infrastructure and expert manpower.  At 
the national level, for effectiv e coordination and comprehensive disease prevention, cancer 
has been integrated within the NP -NCD framework. This integration facilitates streamlined 
policy-making, optimized resource allocation, and consolidated data collection. However, the 
actual implem entation of cancer services relies significantly on specialized institutions. 
TCCCs and SCIs play a pivotal role in delivering high -quality care and strengthening the 
public health infrastructure. Integrating cancer care within NP -NCD also promotes early 
detection and preventive efforts, while robust referral linkages to these specialized centers 
ensure timely access to advanced treatment modalities. 
 
1.35 Pradhan Mantri Ayushman Bharat Health Infrastructure Mission (PM-ABHIM) 
Recommendations/Observations 
1.35.1 The Committee notes the progress made under the PM -ABHIM scheme. Although 
there is advancement in work initiation and completion across various components 
[Secondary Health Care -Ayushman Arogya Mandir (SHC -AAM), U - Ayushman Arogya 
Mandir, Block Publi c Health Unit (BPHU), Integrated Public Health Laboratories (IPHL)], 
however, the Critical Care Blocks (CCBs) are significantly lagging the pace of execution, 
with only 3% work completion. The Department has taken steps to accelerate progress, 
including preponing activities and conducting regular reviews. However, land acquisition for 
CCBs remains a major bottleneck. The scheme's focus on strengthening health infrastructure 
across all levels is well taken, but the timelines for completion of project need cl oser 
monitoring. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Department prioritize and 
implement a focused strategy to address the land acquisition and construction challenges 
facing the Critical Care Blocks (CCBs). This strategy should include (a) estab lishing a 
dedicated task force at the state and district levels to expedite land identification and 
allocation for CCBs; (b) providing technical assistance and streamlined approval processes to 
States/UTs to overcome bureaucratic hurdles in construction; ( c) exploring alternative 
construction models, such as modular construction, to reduce lead times; and (d) 
implementing a strict monitoring system with milestone based review of each CCB project to 
ensure timely completion of the project. Given the critical  role of CCBs in pandemic 
preparedness and critical care, immediate and decisive action is crucial to ensure the timely 
completion of the project. 
(Para 4.7.10 of the Report) 
Action Taken 
1.35.2 PM-ABHIM is an infrastructure initiative which intends to help improve health 
infrastructure up to the block level. Being a capital grant having infrastructure components 
involving lead time/gestation period, accordingly, pace of expenditure is slow. Howev er, 

70 
 
MoHFW is regularly monitoring and evaluating the progress of all States/UTs through review 
meetings, video conferences & field visits by senior officials. Further, Common Review 
Missions (CRM) are conducted periodically to assess and monitor the progre ss and 
implementation status of scheme. However, scheme being in the last year of the scheme 
period, expenditure is expected to increase on the completion of projects. 
 Under Centrally Sponsored Scheme (CSS) Components of PM – Ayushman Bharat 
Health Infrastructure Mission (PM -ABHIM), provision is made for establishment of 602 
Critical Care Blocks (CCBs) during the scheme period (FY 2021 -22 to FY 2025-26). Out 
of which approval has been accorded for 621 units. 
 During the FY 2021 -22 to 2024 -25, approval s have been accorded for 401 CCB units 
against which work has been started in 317 units which is more than 75%. Work has been 
completed for more than 30 CCBs. Further, many units are near completion. 
 The establishment of CCB is a novel addition to the health system, designed for managing 
infectious diseases which is extremely technical subject. Finalization of Hospital design 
specification required planning and appropriate land availability adjoining to existing 
hospitals. Therefore, delay occurs on account  of land availability issues, preparation of 
DPR and final grounding of the work. 
 To ensure the timely implementation of the scheme, the deliverables envisaged in the 
scheme for FY 2024 -25 and 2025 -26 were preponed in FY 2023 -24. This proactive 
measure aims to facilitate an earlier commencement of activities. 
 Also, to facilitate the timely completion of the projects approved under the scheme, 
States/UTs have been supported for establishment of PMU to provide technical 
assistance. 
 Flexibility has been provid ed to the States to utilize funds released without 
earmarking/linking it against year wise approvals. 
 Based on the recent photographs of the construction, the reviews are undertaken for 
CCBs, these ensures the milestones of construction are appropriately m onitored. Further, 
meetings are also held with the executing agencies and procurement agencies of the 
Projects to understand the bottlenecks and take actions. 
 Continuous monitoring through regular reviews by Hon‘ble Union Minister of Health & 
Family Welfare & by Union Health Secretary and other senior officials of the Ministry is 
also being done time to time to monitor the progress of scheme. 
 Periodic unit wise CCB is being reviewed by senior official of this ministry to monitor the 
progress and pace of construction. 
The Chief Secretaries, along with other departments of the states, undertake regular meetings 
to facilitate land availability for the projects. 
1.36 Ayushman Bharat-Pradhan Mantri Jan Arogya Yojana (AB-PMJAY) 
Recommendations/Observations 
1.36.1 The Committee is of the opinion that the Ayushman Bharat -Pradhan Mantri Jan 
Arogya Yojana (AB -PMJAY) is a pivotal and visionary health initiative aiming to provide 
financial protection and healthcare access to a large number of India's vulnerable po pulation. 

71 
 
While the scheme has seen substantial growth in terms of state participation, card creation, 
and hospital admissions, several key areas require attention like Budget Utilization and State 
Participation. The Committee observes a persistent trend o f underutilization of allocated 
funds, particularly in the previous year as well as in the current financial year. The Committee 
observes that, in the FY 2023 -24, an amount of Rs. 7200.00 crore was allocated as BE 2023 -
24 which was reduced to Rs. 6800.00 c rore at RE stage, however, the actual expenditure was 
Rs. 6670.47 crore.  In the FY 2024 -25, Rs. 7300.00 crores was made in BE 2024 -25 which 
was revised to Rs. 7605.54 crores at RE 2024 -25, however, the actual expenditure was to the 
tune of Rs. 5034.03 Cro res till 09.01.2025. In FY 2025 -26, the enhanced amount of Rs. 9406 
crore has been allocated as BE 2025 -26.  This indicates potential inefficiencies in fund 
disbursement or implementation bottlenecks. The Committee, therefore, recommends a 
thorough review of the fund release mechanisms to states/UTs. This review should focus on 
identifying and eliminating bottlenecks that contribute to underutilization. Specifically, the 
Department should implement a system of proactive monitoring of fund utilization by sta tes, 
coupled with targeted support to address any implementation challenges. This support could 
include technical assistance, capacity building, and simplified reporting procedures. 
Furthermore, the committee suggests that the release of funds be linked to  the performance of 
states in terms of card creation, hospital admissions, and beneficiary feedback, ensuring that 
funds are directed to areas of greatest need and impact. The Department should also create 
and publish a clear and concise guideline for stat es, explaining the process of fund requests, 
and the expected timelines. To ensure that the allocation of fund to the tune of Rs. 9406.00 
crore in BE 2025 -26 is utilized, a quarterly review system may be set up to avoid any delays 
in release of fund to States/UTs. 
(Para 4.8.7 of the Report) 
Action Taken 
1.36.2 There has been a consistent upward trend in expenditure under the PM -JAY scheme 
over the past few years, reflecting the scheme‘s expanding implementation and increasing 
utilization across the country. In alignment with this growth, the total funds released to States 
and Union Territories have also shown a steady increase. In the financial year 2024 -25, Rs. 
6,881.65 crore representing 90% of the allocated budget was utilized, reflecting a slight 
increase from the Rs. 6,554.15 crore spent in financial year 2023 –24. Notably, in addition to 
the regular Implementation and administration funds, separate funds are now also being 
released for ASHA, AWW, and AWH workers, as well as under the Vay Vandana Scheme for 
the treatment of senior citizens aged 70 years and above. These allocations represent 
additional support measures aimed at enhancing outreach and ensuring inclusive healthcare 
access.  Further, a comprehensive review of the fund release mechanisms is be ing initiated to 
identify and address bottlenecks at both central and state levels. 
Recommendations/Observations 
1.36.3 The Committee is of the view that besides the large number of empaneled hospitals 
and issuance of cards, the crucial factor for successf ul implementation of the Yojana in public 
awareness about the Yojana is public awareness about the Yojana. The Committee, therefore, 

72 
 
recommends a focused campaign, high -level engagement with the respective state 
governments, addressing their specific conce rns and highlighting the benefits of the scheme 
for their populations. The Department should intensify its efforts to enhance beneficiary 
awareness, particularly in rural and underserved areas. This can be achieved through targeted 
Information, Education, and Communication (IEC) campaigns, utilizing local languages and 
community-based approaches. Leveraging ASHA and Anganwadi workers, as well as digital 
platforms, to disseminate information and facilitate card creation is crucial. The Committee 
also recommends that the Department conduct regular beneficiary feedback surveys to assess 
the effectiveness of awareness campaigns and identify areas for improvement. A dedicated 
helpline and easily accessible grievance redressal mechanism should also be established to 
address beneficiary concerns promptly. Further, public displays of information regarding 
hospital empanelment and available procedures should be made mandatory at all public health 
centers. 
(Para 4.8.8 of the Report) 
Action Taken 
1.36.4 In response to the Committee's emphasis on enhancing public awareness as a crucial 
factor for the effective implementation of AB -PMJAY, it may be noted that towards 
enhancing public awareness, National Health Authority (NHA) has undertaken a 
comprehensive Information, Ed ucation and Communication (IEC) strategy to increase 
visibility and understanding of the scheme, especially in rural and underserved regions. 
Key initiatives include: 
1. Targeted IEC Campaigns: 
 NHA has developed robust IEC campaigns utilizing a mix of mass me dia and grassroots 
outreach. These include radio jingles, TV advertisements, outdoor hoardings, branding of 
trains and auto-rickshaws, and announcements at railway and bus stations. 
 These campaigns are conducted in local languages and customized to the cultural 
contexts of different regions to ensure deeper penetration and impact. 
2. Field-Level Mobilization: 
 ASHAs, Anganwadi Workers, and Panchayati Raj functionaries have been actively 
engaged as frontline mobilizers. They are incentivized to conduct household visits, assist 
in Ayushman Card creation, and educate the community about scheme benefits. 
 
3. Campaign-Based Outreach: 
 
 Dedicated initiatives like ―Aapke Dwar Ayushman‖ (phases 1.0, 2.0, and 3.0), 
Ayushman Bhava, and Viksit Bharat Sankalp Yatra hav e significantly accelerated card 
creation and awareness in marginalized areas. More than 11.4 crore Ayushman Cards 

73 
 
were created in FY 2023-24 alone, and an additional 3 crore were generated in FY 2024 -
25. 
4. Beneficiary Feedback Mechanisms: 
 
 NHA has establish ed a multi -tier grievance redressal system at district, state, and 
national levels to promptly address beneficiary concerns. In addition, feedback is 
gathered through various channels including call centers (14555), emails, and physical 
grievance submission points to continually improve service delivery. 
5. DedicatedHelpline: 
 
 A national toll -free helpline (14555) is operational to support beneficiaries with 
information, assistance in card creation, and grievance resolution. 
NHA remains committed to strengthen ing engagement with State Governments to address 
state-specific challenges, and to further intensify awareness efforts to ensure that no eligible 
beneficiary is left behind. 
Recommendations/Observations 
1.36.5 The Committee also recommends the Department to launch public awareness 
campaign about AB-PMJAY so that intended beneficiaries of the Yojana are not left out from 
coverage. 
(Para 4.8.10 of the Report) 
Action Taken 
1.36.6 A range of structured and sustained public awareness initiatives have been 
implemented to ensure that the intended beneficiaries of the Ayushman Bharat Pradhan 
Mantri Jan Arogya Yojana (AB -PMJAY) are fully informed about the scheme and do not 
miss out on coverage. These efforts are aimed at improving understanding, mobilizing eligible 
individuals, and promoting Ayushman Card creation, particularly among rural and 
underserved populations. 
Key public awareness activities and campaigns include: - 
(i) IEC Campaigns (Mass Media and Outdoor): 
 Radio jingles, television spots, newspaper advertisem ents, and outdoor hoardings have 
been deployed extensively. 
 Branding of public transportation systems such as trains, buses, and auto -rickshaws, 
along with announcements at railway stations and bus depots, have reinforced visibility 
across high-footfall public spaces. 
(ii) Digital and Mobile Platforms: 

74 
 
 SMS alerts, online videos, and beneficiary testimonial campaigns are regularly shared 
through digital platforms to educate and motivate potential beneficiaries.  
 
(iii) Campaign-Based Outreach Initiatives: 
 
 Aapke Dwar Ayushman Campaigns (Phases 1.0, 2.0, and 3.0): Launched in 2021 and 
scaled up in subsequent years, these door-to-door campaigns mobilized frontline workers 
such as ASHAs and Anganwadi Workers to facilitate Ayushman Card creation and 
inform families about s cheme entitlements. Aapke Dwar Ayushman 3.0 also introduced 
the self-registration feature to increase coverage. 
 Ayushman Bhava Campaign: Focused on strengthening awareness through Jan Arogya 
Abhiyan and Jan Samvad platforms at the village and community levels. 
 Viksit Bharat Sankalp Yatra (VBSY): Leveraged to organize Ayushman Card creation 
drives and IEC activities, especially in aspirational districts and remote areas. 
 PM-JANMAN (Pradhan Mantri Janjati Adivasi Nyaya Maha Abhiyan): Targeted card 
creation an d information dissemination among Particularly Vulnerable Tribal Groups 
(PVTGs). 
 PM-JUGA (Pradhan Mantri Janjatiya Unnat Gram Abhiyan) and Dharti Aaba Abhiyan: 
Conducted health and awareness camps in tribal -dominated regions to promote 
Ayushman card distribution and scheme adoption. 
(iv) Field-Level Worker Mobilization: 
 ASHAs, Anganwadi Workers, and Anganwadi Helpers are incentivized to support card 
creation and beneficiary awareness at the grassroots level. Their role in mobilizing 
households has been pivotal in remote and tribal areas. 
(v) Beneficiary Card Creation Outcomes: 
 Over 40.59 crore Ayushman Cards have been generated as of April 2025, including 11.4 
crore cards created in FY 2023-24 and over 7 crore in FY 2024-25 through these targeted 
campaigns. 
(vi) Helpline and Grievance Redressal: 
 A toll-free national helpline (14555) operates for beneficiary support, guiding users on 
eligibility, card generation, and grievance redressal. 
 Beneficiaries may also raise concerns through the web -based Centralized Grievance 
Redressal Management System (CGRMS), emails, call centers, or direct communication 
with State Health Agencies. 
 
These initiatives have significantly enhanced the scheme‘s reach and visibility, ensuring that 
beneficiaries across India, including those in rural, tribal, and underserved communities, are 
aware of their entitlements and empowered to access healthcare under AB-PMJAY. 

75 
 
Recommendations/Observations 
1.36.7 The Committee observes that several high -end interventions/ procedures, and even 
high-end diagnostics are not included in AB -PMJAY. The Committee recommends that the 
number of packages/ procedures covered under the Yojana should be reviewed and the fresh 
packages/procedures related to treatment of critical illness with high cost of treatment an d 
expensive high -end investigations/ diagnostics like radiological diagnostics (CT, MRI, 
imaging including Nuclear Imaging) be incorporated in AB -PMJAY instead of leaving the 
same to be booked as Add-on packages. 
(Para 4.8.12 of the Report) 
Action Taken 
1.36.8 The Committee‘s recommendation to review and expand the number of 
packages/procedures under the Ayushman Bharat -Pradhan Mantri Jan Arogya Yojana (AB -
PMJAY) to include high -end interventions, procedures, and diagnostics, such as radiological 
diagnostics (CT, MRI, and nuclear imaging), as standard packages rather than add -on 
packages, is noted.  
AB-PMJAY currently provides a health cover of Rs. 5 lakh per family per year for secondary 
and tertiary care hospitalization, benefiting approximately 60 crore  individuals across 12.37 
crore families. The Health Benefit Package (HBP) 2022, adopted by 18 States/UTs, includes 
1,961 procedures across 27 specialties, covering critical treatments such as PTCA, CABG, 
neurosurgery, cancer care (radiation oncology, chem otherapy, and surgical oncology), and 
nephrology. The packages are designed to be comprehensive, encompassing pre -
hospitalization diagnostics, consultations, and medications (up to 3 days) and post -
hospitalization expenses, including medicines (up to 15 da ys). High-end investigations like 
CT, MRI, and nuclear imaging are currently available as add -on packages to medical 
procedures under the scheme, ensuring flexibility in addressing diagnostic needs. 
To address the Committee‘s concern, NHA has revised the HBP five times since the scheme‘s 
inception, with the latest HBP 2022 increasing rates for 350 packages and introducing new 
packages to cover emerging healthcare needs. NHA continuously evaluates the inclusion of 
additional high-end procedures and diagnosti cs based on disease burden, cost -effectiveness, 
and stakeholder feedback. States/UTs have the flexibility to customize HBP rates and 
packages to align with local requirements, such as provider expectations and resource 
availability, ensuring region -specific needs are met. As of March 31, 2025, 31,005 hospitals 
(17,108 public and 13,897 private) are empanelled, facilitating access to specialized 
diagnostics and treatments. 
Recommendations/Observations 
1.36.9 The Committee has come across the instances of del ay in settlement of claims of 
empaneled hospitals under AB -PMJAY and that the package rates have not been reasonably 
revised in many States resulting into inconvenience caused to the patients due to denial of 
treatment by many empaneled hospitals. The Comm ittee, therefore, considers that the 

76 
 
States/UTs which have not revised the package rates may be persuaded by the Department to 
revise the package rates on the similar lines of rates revised by some of the States in 2022. In 
addition to the above, the Commi ttee recommends that the Department review and streamline 
the process related to settlement of claims submitted by the empane lled hospitals within the 
specified time. 
(Para 4.8.13 of the Report) 
Action Taken 
1.36.10 The Committee‘s concern regarding delays in the settlement of claims by empanelled 
hospitals under the Ayushman Bharat -Pradhan Mantri Jan Arogya Yojana (AB -PMJAY) and 
the lack of revision of package rates in several States/UTs, leading to inconvenience and 
denial of treatment to beneficiaries, is duly noted. 
1.36.11 To address delays in claim settlements, NHA has implemented robust monitoring 
mechanisms, including the Transaction Management System (TMS 2.0), operational in 31 
States/UTs, which streamlines in -patient registration, pre -authorization, claims management, 
and payments. Claim settlement is monitored at the highest level to ensure adherence to 
defined turnaround times. NHA has also established a toll -free hospital support helpline 
(14413) and directed State Health Agencies (SHAs) to cond uct regular District 
Implementation Unit (DIU) visits to resolve on -ground issues promptly. In cases where 
beneficiaries face denial of treatment, a three -tier grievance redressal system at the district, 
state, and national levels is in place. Beneficiarie s can lodge complaints through the 
Centralized Grievance Redressal Management System (CGRMS), the national toll -free 
helpline (14555), email, or letters to SHAs, with appropriate actions taken, including support 
for availing treatment and punitive measures against errant hospitals, such as de-empanelment 
or penalties. 
1.36.12 Regarding package rates, NHA has revised the HBP five times since the scheme‘s 
inception, with HBP 2022 standardizing costs across city tiers for both public and private 
hospitals. Currently, 18 States/UTs have adopted HBP 2022, which includes enhanced rates 
for 350 packages and new additions. NHA has repeatedly urged States/UTs to adopt these 
updated rates and has provided flexibility to customize HBP rates based on local factors such 
as provider expectations, resource availability, and disease burden. NHA has sent multiple 
communications, including DO letters, to States/UTs that have not yet adopted HBP 2022, 
encouraging them to align with the revised rates, similar to those updated by  some States in 
2022.

improvements. In the discussion with the Commission, the focus was on enhancement  of 

77 
 
diagnostic capabilities, up -gradation of infrastructure to meet Indian Public Health Standards 
(IPHS) 2022, and addressing the key infrastructure gaps in healthcare facilities to align with

required to achieve the goals of enhancing diagnostic infras tructure, meeting IPHS 2022, and

1.38 Elderly Care 
Recommendations/Observations 
1.38.1 The Committee observes that the primary issue is the significant shortfall in funding 
for the National Centre for Ageing (NCA) at Banaras Hindu University (BHU). While a 
demand of Rs 95.38 crore was made for the 2025 -26, only Rs 50 crore has been allocated. 
Given that the NCA is under construction with a total cost of Rs 150 crore, and Rs 47.20 crore 
has already been sanctioned, the remaining funds are crucial for timely completion. To ensure 
continuity and prevent project dela ys, the Committee recommends that the Ministry prioritize 
the release of the remaining Rs 35 crore as initially planned. Furthermore, the Committee 
explicitly advocates for a proactive monitoring mechanism to assess the project's progress 
during the first half of 2025-26. This will allow for the timely identification of any additional 
funding needs. This approach ensures the NCA's timely completion, benefiting elderly 
citizens. 
(Para 5.2.2 of the Report) 
Action Taken 
1.38.2 It is informed that the approved budget for establishment of NCA at IMS, BHU, 
Varanasi is Rs. 147.39 crore, out of this an amount of Rs. 67.42 crore has already been 
released till 31.03.2025. Further, it is informed that for FY 2025 -26, the BE proposed was Rs. 
95.38 crore which includes R s. 80 crores for NCA, BHU, but the amount allocated in BE is 
Rs. 50 crore only under Grant -in-Aid (Grants for Creation of Capital Assets) for FY 2025 -26 
(out of which only Rs. 30 crore has been provided for NCA, BHU). As the target date for 
completion of t he NCA, BHU is end of March 2026, the balance amount required for NCA, 
BHU will be sought at RE 2025-26 stage. 

78 
 
1.39 Emission Gap Report 2020 UN Environment Programme 
Recommendations/Observations 
1.39.1 Keeping in view of the observation of Emission Gap Report 2020 UN Environment 
Programme that India has seen over 6% more Green House Gas Emission than previous year, 
the Committee feels that it is high time for India to establish an Environmental Health 
Regulatory Agency for Comprehensive and Cohesive Envi ronmental Governance to control 
detrimental health effects of exposure to air, water and soil pollutants to prevent wide range of 
non-communicable diseases. The Committee appreciates that the Ministry of Health & Family 
Welfare (MoHFW) has been working clo sely with other ministries, such as the Ministry of 
Environment, Forest and Climate Change (MoEF&CC) and NITI Aayog, to develop multi -
sectoral strategies for mitigating environmental health risks and National Programme on 
Climate Change and Human Health (N PCCHH) has developed an organizational structure at 
all levels-National, State and District level. The Committee recommends that the Department 
significantly enhance the regulatory authority and scope of the State Level Environment 
Health Cells within the NPCCHH framework, especially, these cells should be empowered 
with clear mandates for enforcement and monitoring of environmental health standards, 
including air, water, and soil pollution, and be given the resources to conduct independent 
assessments and issue binding directives. This expansion would leverage the established 
structure of the NPCCHH, particularly the state -level Multi-Sectoral Task Forces, to ensure a 
more robust and proactive approach to mitigating the detrimental health effects of 
environmental pollution, thereby strengthening the existing framework ensuring continuity 
and efficient utilization of existing resources while addressing the urgent need for stronger 
environmental health governance. 
(Para 5.3.3 of the Report) 
Action Taken 
1.39.2 The recommendations of the Committee are noted.  The National Programme on 
Climate Change & Human Health (NPCCHH) was approved in 2019 to provide State level 
technical and financial support for implementation of Health Mission on Climate Change 
under Prime Minister‘s Council on Climate Change. NPCCHH looks after the health issues 
which are due to the impact of Climate Change. It also addresses issues of air pollution 
related health impacts and water related health concerns in coordination with respective 
Ministries of MoEF&CC and MoJS.The recommendation of establishing Environmental 
Health Regulatory Agency for Comprehensive and Cohesive Environmental Governance 
made by the Hon‘ble DRPSC on Health & Family Welfare is very well acknowledged by the 
Ministry.―Environmental Protection‖ falls under the Concurrent List (List III) in the Seventh 
Schedule of the Indian Constitution (Article 246), hence both the Central and State 
governments have the power to legislate on environmental matters. Central level has 
overarching responsibility, especially for policy formulation, regulation, and international 
commitments.Ministry of Environment Forest & Climate Change, Ministry of Jal Shakti 
along with other statutory bodies like Central Pollution Control Board, National Bio diversity 
Authority, National Green Tribunal, Commission for Air Quality Management, etc. look after 

79 
 
implementation of policy matters related to regulation of standards for air, water and 
soil.Considering that there are already environmental regulatory age ncies with necessary 
legislative provision to address these concerns it may be suitable to let environmental 
regulation and governance be with these agencies. MoHFW would support concerned 
agencies in addressing environmental health issues. 
1.40 Opportunity for Global Health Leadership 
Recommendations/Observations 
1.40.1 Given the fact of US withdrawal from the WHO and India's existing strong 
engagement and digital health leadership, the Committee recommends a focused strategy to 
solidify India's global health leadership by leveraging its digital health expertise and 
educational capacity. The Committee recommends that India should proactively establish a 
dedicated "Global Digital Health Partnership Initiative" under the WHO framework that 
would certainly streamline the offering of India's Digital Public Infrastructure (DPI), like 
Ayushman Bharat Digital Health Mission and Co -Win, to developing nations through Global 
Initiative on Digital Health (GIDH), coupled with robust capacity -building programs and 
educational exchanges. By actively promoting its successful digital health models and 
expanding its medical education offerings, India can not only fill the leadership vacuum but 
also foster a more robust domestic biomedical ecosystem and become a premier hea lthcare 
training hub for Asian -African countries, thus maintaining continuity with its existing 
contributions and maximizing the opportunity presented by the changing global health 
landscape. 
(Para 5.5.2 of the Report) 
Action Taken 
1.40.2 The Ministry of Health and Family Welfare has established the Global Initiative on 
Digital Health (GIDH) under the WHO framework during India‘s G20 Presidency. The G20 
New Delhi Leaders‘ Declaration welcomes the establishment of the Global Initiative on 
Digital Health (GIDH) within a WHO -managed framework to build a comprehensive digital 
health ecosystem in compliance with respective data protection regulations.The Ministry of 
Health and Family Welfare, in coordination with Ministry of Electronics and Information 
Technology, Ministry of External Aﬀairs has actively presented India's Digital Public 
Infrastructures, including e-Sanjeevani, the Integrated Health Information Platform, Co -Win, 
and SAKSHAM, to other countries during various international engagements like bilatera l 
meetings, Joint Working Groups and multilateral forums. Following these engagements, the 
governments of Sri Lanka, Bhutan, and Nauru have expressed strong interest in learning from 
India's digital healthadvancements. They have approached the Government of  India seeking 
technical assistance, financial support, and exposure visits to  India to strengthen their own 
digital health infrastructure. 
 
 
 

80 
 
1.41 Review of National Health Policy 2017 
Recommendations/Observations 
1.41.1 The Committee notes that almost 8 years have been passed since the formulation of the 
National Health Policy 2017. The Committee, therefore, recommends establishing a formal, 
periodic review mechanism for the policy's implementation and impact assessment. This 
mechanism should mandate cons istent, transparent engagement and feedback collection from 
various stakeholders and the public. The review of the policy and publishing the findings can 
ensure that the policy remains responsive to evolving healthcare needs and maintains the 
initial broad  consensus achieved through its formulation. This will guarantee continuity 
between the policy's foundational principles and its ongoing adaptation of global practices in 
the field of healthcare. 
(Para 5.6.2 of the Report) 
Action Taken 
1.41.2 The National Health Policy (NHP) 2017 provides a comprehensive framework to 
strengthen the health system and improve access, quality and affordability of healthcare 
services. It seeks to attain the highest possible level of preventive and promotive approach, 
universal access, and financial protection. To operationalize the goals of NHP 2017, the 
National Health Mission (NHM) serves as the key vehicle for implementation. NHM focuses 
on strengthening primary healthcare through a continuum of care approach and integration of 
public health systems, with emphasis on decentralized planning, intersectoral convergence, 
and community participation.Under NHM, several mechanisms have been institutionalized to 
monitor and assess implementation of NHP objectives: - 
 Annual Programme I mplementation Plans (PIPs) are prepared by States/UTs and 
appraised at the central level, ensuring alignment with policy goals. 
 Common Review Missions (CRMs) and Joint Monitoring Missions (JMMS) are 
conducted regularly with multi -stakeholder participation to assess progress, identify 
bottlenecks, and recommend corrective actions. 
 Health Management Information System (HMIS) are Real -Time Data Platforms are used 
to monitor service delivery indicators and health outcomes. 
 Independent evaluations and surveys su ch as National Family Health Surveys (NFHS), 
Sample Registration system (SRS), and National Health Accounts (NHA) further support 
evidence-based policy review and impact assessment. 
1.41.3 These mechanisms enable periodic review and mid -course correction to ensure that 
implementation remains aligned with the border goals of the NHP, thus, fostering 
accountability and improved health outcomes. 
 
 
 

81 
 
1.42 Gig Workers under PMJAY Scheme 
Recommendations/Observations 
1.42.1 The Committee, to ensure the effective inclusion of 10 million gig workers under the 
PMJAY scheme, recommends the Department for immediate establishment of a dedicated, 
automated, and real -time data synchronization system between the E -Shram portal and the 
AB-PMJAY database. This system should be designed to facilitate seamless, continuous, and 
error-free data transfer, enabling instant verification and de -duplication exercise to identify 
and eliminate any overlap. This focused approach to data integration, alongside the planned 
awareness campaigns and platform collaborations will minimize enrolment delays and ensure 
that all eligible gig workers receive timely access to essential healthcare benefits, while 
preventing double counting of beneficiaries. 
(Para 5.10.2 of the Report) 
Action Taken 
1.42.2 The Committee‘s recommendation for a dedicated, automated, real -time data 
synchronization system between the E -Shram portal and the AB -PMJAY database to include 
10 million gig workers is noted. NHA has communicated with the Ministry of Labour and 
Employment (MoLE) to integrate the E -Shram portal with NHA‘s Beneficiary Identification 
System (BIS) portal for seamless sharing of beneficiary data. This integration will streamline 
the identification and verification of gig workers and their families, ensuring  accurate 
enrolment. It will also facilitate the exclusion of individuals who are no longer gig workers 
and are not registered on the E -Shram portal, preventing overlaps. NHA in collaboration with 
MoLE will also conduct awareness campaigns to encourage reg istration, ensuring timely 
access to AB-PMJAY benefits for eligible gig workers. 
1.43 Hub & Spoke Model for Cardiac and Stroke 
Recommendations/Observations 
1.43.1 The Committee endorses the views of Department for strengthening infrastructure, 
human resource development, health promotion & awareness generation for prevention, early 
diagnosis, management and referral to an appropriate level of healthcare facility for treatment 
of the Non-Communicable Diseases. The Committee desires that for effective managem ent of 
patients with ST Segment Elevated Myocardial Infarction (STEMI) and Stroke, Hub and 
Spoke model may be implemented. The Department may persuade the States have to identify 
the Tertiary care hospitals as ‗Hub‘ based on infrastructure, experts and lab oratory and 
intervention services and Spokes for early interventions.  
(Para 5.15.3 of the Report) 
Action Taken 
1.43.2 The Department acknowledges the Committee's endorsement of its efforts under the 
National Programme for Prevention and Control of Non -Communicable Diseases (NP-NCD). 
The Committee‘s recommendations align with the Department‘s strategic direction to: 

82 
 
strengthen infrastructure, develop human resources, enhance health promotion and awareness, 
and improve timely diagnosis, management, and referral for NCD patients. The Department 
outlines the following action points w.r.t STEMI & Stroke: - 
(i) Human Resource Development and Capacity Building. 
 Specialist Training: Targeted programs are being implemented to build the capacity of 
general physicians and specialists in cardiology, neurology, and emergency medicine for 
managing STEMI and Stroke. 
 Telemedicine Support: e Sanjeevani platforms are being integrated with NP -NCD to 
address human resource gaps in remote areas and provide specialist support from hubs to 
spokes. 
(ii) Implementing a Hub and Spoke Model for STEMI and Stroke: The Department agrees 
with the Committee on ad opting a Hub and Spoke model for timely and effective 
management of STEMI and Stroke, major contributors to premature mortality. 
Identifying Hubs: Tertiary care hospitals (including government medical colleges, apex 
institutions, and NABH -accredited private centers) are being mapped and designated as 
‗Hubs‘ based on: - 
 24/7 availability of cardiology and neurology services. 
 Advanced diagnostic and imaging facilities (CT, MRI, Cath lab). 
 Availability of interventional care (e.g., PCI, thrombolysis, neurosurgery). 
 Ambulance and emergency protocols. 
 1.43.3 Designating Spokes: District Hospitals, CHCs, and Health and Wellness Centres 
(HWCs) will act as ‗Spokes‘, equipped with: - 
 Point-of-care diagnostics. 
 Basic resuscitative equipment. 
 Protocols for early identification, stabilization, and referral. 
 Trained staff for initial thrombolysis or basic stroke protocols. 
1.43.4 Strengthening Emergency Referral Linkages: States are being guided to strengthen 
108/102 ambulance networks and integrates them with e -health platforms for rapid 
communication and patient transfer.  Supporting States in Implementation: The Department is 
actively working with State Health Departments to: - 
 Encourage them to identify appropriate facilities as Hubs and Spokes. 
 Establish formal referral protocols and Memorandums of Understanding (MoUs) between 
facilities. 

83 
 
 Provide financial and technical support through NHM flexi -pools and NP -NCD vertical 
grants. 
 Monitor implementation via the Health Management Information System (HMIS) and 
ABDM-integrated platforms.  
Recommendations/Observations 
1.43.5 The Committee understands that NCD screening are integral component of NPNCD 
programme, therefore, the population aged 30 years and above must be undertaken for 
screening of NCDs like Diabetes, Hypertension, Oral cancer, Breast cancer and cervical 
cancers under National campaign launched from 20th Feb 2025. 
(Para 5.15.4 of the Report) 
Action Taken 
1.43.6 The population -based screening for population above 30 years of age for common 
NCDs, i.e. Diabetes, hypertension, oral cancer, breast cancer, and cervical cancer are integral 
component of NP -NCD. Through > 17600 Ayushman Arogya Mandir this particular 
screening is being implemented. Accredited Social Health Activist (ASHA) & Auxiliary 
Nurse and Midwife (ANM), and Community Health Officers (CHOs) are trained to conduct 
initial risk assessment, basic screening, and referrals. They also maintain line -lists of eligible 
populations and ensure follow-up.The established screening protocols include Blood  Pressure 
(BP) and Blood Glucose Measurement for detecting Hypertension and Diabetes, ensuring 
timely diagnosis and management. For Oral Cancer, an Oral Visual Examination (OVE) is 
performed, allowing healthcare providers to identify suspicious lesions ear ly. In the case of 
Breast Cancer, Clinical Breast Examination (CBE) is utilized to detect abnormalities, helping 
in early identification of potential malignancies. For Cervical Cancer, Visual Inspection with 
Acetic Acid (VIA).Frontline health workers such as Accredited Social Health Activists 
(ASHAs), Auxiliary Nurse Midwives (ANMs), and Community Health Officers (CHOs) play 
a pivotal role in the implementation of these screening protocols. ASHAs are actively 
engaged in community mobilization, spreading awa reness about NCD prevention, and 
ensuring individuals undergo screening. ANMs provide support in conducting screenings, 
recording health data, and facilitating referrals. CHOs, stationed at Health and Wellness 
Centres, act as key coordinators, ensuring sea mless execution of screening programs and 
linking suspected cases to higher healthcare facilities for confirmation and treatment. 
1.44 High Malaria Cases in Kokrajhar, Assam 
Recommendations/Observations 
1.44.1 The Committee expresses concern that all 4 blocks of Kokrajhar districts have 
reportedly witnessed an increase in cases in 2024, with the majority of cases from the 
northern part of the district. The Committee, therefore, strongly recommends that the 
Department should persuade the State Government to undertake concrete steps for combating 
the menace of malaria in Kokrajhar district, at the earliest. The Committee further 
recommends that the Department must ensure strengthening of treatment follow -up and 

84 
 
capacity building mechanism of healthcare professionals to eliminate malaria from the 
country. 
(Para 5.17.3 of the Report) 
Action Taken 
1.44.2 From January to May 20, 2025, Kokrajhar district has reported 625 malaria cases, 
including 568 cases of Plasmodium vivax (520 treated, the rest under treatment) and 57 cases 
of Plasmodium falciparum. In response, the Government of Assam has launched a series of 
proactive interventions. Surveillance and monitoring are being continuously carried out by the 
Directorate of Health Services, NHM, and the State Health Department. Contact tracing, early 
detection, and prompt treatment protocols are in place, with telephonic compliance tracking 
for unreachable patients.Rapid Response Teams (RRTs) have been activated at the distr ict 
level, and ASHAs and health workers are regularly conducting fever screening.Vector control 
measures include the use of Insecticide -Treated Nets (ITNs), larvicidal spraying, distribution 
of larvivorous fish in mosquito breeding sites (in collaboration with Guwahati University), 
and case -based fogging. Indoor Residual Spraying (IRS) is scheduled for June 2025. All 
healthcare facilities have been equipped with diagnostic kits and anti -malarial drugs, and 
health worker sensitization sessions have been comp leted. A temporary Health & Wellness 
Centre is planned in remote areas with support from the Forest Department. Public awareness 
campaigns are ongoing through intensified IEC/BCC activities, and inter -sectoral 
coordination meetings are being held regularly . A virtual review chaired by the Additional 
Director of NCVBDC on 14 May 2025 emphasized using ITNs in areas without LLINs, 
initiating control measures even in low -case blocks like Lungchung (FV), ensuring follow -up 
of all malaria cases, and deputing a la b technician through ROHFW to strengthen microscopy 
and lab diagnostics. These collective efforts aim to curb the spread of malaria and strengthen 
preparedness across the district. 
1.45 Other Recommendations of the Committee 
Recommendations/Observations 
1.45.1 Private medical education should be made cheaper and the Capitation fees needs to be 
regulated to make the medical education more affordable and accessible to merit candidates 
with poor financial background; 
(Para 5.20 (ii) of the Report) 
Action Taken 
1.45.2 Fees for Government Medical Colleges are regulated by the respective State 
Governments. Further, fees in private unaided medical colleges are decided by a Commission 
set up by the respective Sate Government. Additionality, Section 10 (i) of the Nat ional 
Medical Commission (NMC) Act, 2019 empowers the NMC to frame guidelines for 
determination of fees and all other charges in respect of fifty percent of seats in private 
medical institutions and deemed to be universities which are governed under the pr ovisions of 
this Act. Accordingly, guidelines have been framed and issued by the NMC on 03.02.2022. 
These guidelines have been challenged before the Court and the matter is sub-judice. 

85 
 
Recommendations/Observations 
1.45.3 Strengthening healthcare facilities up to District Hospital level so that patient need not 
be referred to tertiary hospitals;  
(Para 5.20 (vi) of the Report) 
Action Taken 
1.45.4 Strengthening of healthcare facilities at the primary and secondary levels is being 
undertaken through a structured handholding approach to support facilities in achieving 
compliance with IPHS. As of 31st March 2025, 17% of the facilities have achieved IPHS 
compliance out of the 96% that have been assessed.According to the NHM Conditionality 
Framework, 50% of  public healthcare facilities are required to be IPHS compliant by 2026. 
Furthermore, as per the target set by the MOHFW, all public healthcare facilities are expected 
to achieve full IPHS compliance by 2029.In addition, the Govt. is actively focusing on t he 
strengthening of Community Health Centres (CHCs). Under this initiative, a proposal has 
been put forward to upgrade existing CHCs to function as First Referral Units (FRUs). The 
proposal emphasizes equipping these facilities with the necessary infrastru cture, human 
resources, equipment, and other critical inputs to enable them to deliver effective and 
comprehensive healthcare services maintaining the continuum of care at the secondary 
level.This strategic focus on strengthening institutions up to the sec ondary healthcare level, 
including District Hospitals, aims to enhance the quality of care at these levels and 
significantly reduce unnecessary referrals to tertiary care facilities. 
Recommendations/Observations 
1.45.5 Strengthening infrastructure including Advanced Life Support (ALS) ambulances to 
handle cardiac cases and making available essential medicines and para -medical staff at rural 
health centres as per the norms of the Indian Public Health Standards (IPHS) 2022 for 
strengthening/improving rural health infrastructure. Efforts should be made that all Health 
Schemes should be implemented at ground level especially in rural, semi -urban, tribal and 
hilly areas; 
(Para 5.20 (vii) of the Report) 
Action Taken 
1.45.6 The Government of India supports States each year in improving their health 
infrastructure through the PIP (Program Implementation Plan) process. States propose for the 
required number of ambulances (along with the type of ambulance) including the operational 
costs/full operational costs based on the respective State‘s requirement/ gap analysis/ existing 
condition of the old ambulances/geography. The PIP is then reviewed by the National 
Program Co-ordination Committee. Based on the committee's recommendations, the Ministry 
of Health and Family Welfare grants approval for the projects. As on 30.9.2024, the total BLS 
and ALS operational in India are as detailed below: -

Recommendations/Observations 
1.45.8 Keeping in view that the Department has formulated National Sickle Cell Anaemia 
Elimination Mission setting goal for screening of 7 crore population in the country. The 
Committee desires to know the number of populations covered so far towards this goal and 
the number of persons given sickle cell genetic status card, along with the strategy chalked 
out to achieve the goal during 2026. The Committee wishes to highlight the incidence of 
sickle cell anaemia in tribal areas of the country, including Goa, where the Committee ha d 
recently visited and came across the prevalence of sickle cell anaemia in 1.5 lakh tribal, but 
without any certain provision for elimination of the disease. The Committee has further come 
across that even if ‗PSA‘ Test Kit is available at the health cent re; it is not opened till 
sufficient number of patients visit for full utilisation of the kit. The Committee is of the view 
that the health centre may be directed to open the ‗PSA‘ Kit even if single patient visits the 
centre; 
(Para 5.20 (x) of the Report) 
Action Taken 
1.45.9 The National Sickle Cell Anaemia Elimination Mission (NSCAEM) aims to cover 7 
crore people with screening, counselling for prevention, and care for people with sickle cell 
disease (SCD) in three years i.e. from 2023 to 2026. As of 11th  April 2025, 5.3 crore 
individuals are screened for sickle cell disease and 2.3 crore sickle cell cards have been 
distributed. (Source: NSCAEM Portal)To achieve the target of eliminating Sickle Cell 
Disease from India by 2047, Strategic Pillars for NSCAEM include: - 
Primary prevention: Primary prevention focuses on awareness generation and pre -marital 
and preconception counselling to prevent the conception of child with homozygous 
genotype. Secondary Prevention: Screening early detection of SCD to achieve a  reduction 
in mortality and morbidity with improvement in quality of life of the affected; as well as 
screening for detection of sickle cell trait to reduce the birth of children affected with sickle 
cell disease. Holistic management and continuum of care: This includes:  
 Management of persons with sickle cell disease at primary, secondary, and tertiary 
healthcare levels  
 Advanced diagnostic and treatment modalities at tertiary healthcare facilities  
 Integration with AYUSH  
 Patient support system  

87 
 
 Community adoption 
 Rehabilitation 
1.45.10 The Committee has provided recommendation regarding a ‗PSA‘ kit which is 
used for screening of Prostate cancer and is not related to Sickle Cell Disease (SCD). 
However, for the screening of SCD there are several rapid diagn ostic test kits available in the 
market. A total of 30 kits have been validated by the ICMR. Ministry of Health and Family 
Welfare (MoHFW) has approved the ICMR -validated kits. Centralised rate contract of Rs 28 
per POC has been conveyed to states to avail the procurement option by states.
