1.45.12 The Dentists Act of 1948 governs mattes related to all dental colleges. However, this 
Ministry is currently in the process of constituting the National Dental Commission, which 
shall repeal the Dentists Act, 1948 and replace the Dental Council of India. As per the 
provisions of the National Dental Commission Act, this Ministry has constituted a Search -
cum-Selection Committee for the selection of members of the Commission. Accordingly, the 
process for the constitution of the Commission is at an advanced stage and likely to be 
constituted soon. National Commission for Allied and Healthcare Professions (NCAHP) Act 
2021 was enacted on 28.03.2021. The National Commission for Allied and Healthcare 
Professions (NCAHP) was constituted vide notification dated 11.03.2024 to provide 
regulation and maintenance of standards of education and services by 57 allied types of allied 
and healthcare professionals, assessment of institutions, maintenance of a Central R egister 
and State Register and creation of a system to improve access, research and development and 
adoption of latest scientific advancement and for matters connected therewith or incidentals 
thereof.According to the mandate of the Commission, it is a com mon regulatory body to 
regulate the standards of education and services of the Allied & Healthcare Professions 
included in the Schedule of the NCAHP Act, 2021.Further, the Commission is in the process 
of finalization of Curricula for various professing, Cu rricula for 10 professions (including 
Physiotherapy) scheduled under the Act had already been framed (available in the website of 
the Commission). Process of framing curricula for remaining professions is going on. Work 
on framing Regulations for education , clinical practice and registration etc. is also underway 
for these professions. Following steps have been taken by Indian Nursing Council (INC) for 
simplification of procedures/norms, rules and regulations for establishing nursing colleges are 
as under: - 

88 
 
 INC established and monitors unfirms standard of nursing and assesses the suitability 
with regard to availability of teaching faculty, clinical and infrastructural facilities in 
conformity with regulations framed under the provisions of the INC Act, 1947  for all 
nurses courses in the country. All the guidelines of college of nursing have also been 
placed both in the INC website and available in print. 
 
 The eligible establishments/ organizations should obtain essential certificate/no objection 
certificate form the concerned State Government where the college of nursing is sought to 
be established. 
 
 After receipt of the essential certificate/no objection certificate, the eligible institutions 
shall get recognition from the concerned State Nurses Registration  Council (SNRC) for 
the programme for the particular academic year, which is mandatory requirement.  
 
 Digital monitoring of nursing education by INC - The complete process of issuing of 
suitability letters to the nursing institutions is automated through 10 0% digitization by 
INC. As part of this, institutions apply/upload all the data online including online 
payment. After receiving the application online from the institutions INC examines all the 
applications as per set guidelines and as per its office work flow and the letters can be 
downloaded by the institutions from the INCs website. INC website has been re -designed 
in the year 2021. 
 Some of the proactive measures taken to support establishment of nursing colleges and to 
support nursing education are as follows: - 
 The requirement of land to construct building for School/College of Nursing and Hostel 
has been relaxed. 
 The requirement of 100 bedded parent hospitals has been relaxed for hilly and tribal areas. 
 The student teacher ratio for M. Sc (N) programme has been relaxed from 1:5 to 1:10. 
 Student patient ratio for Nursing Institutions has been relaxed from 1:5 to 1:3. 
 Admission for Nursing has been allowed for married candidates. 
 Maximum number of 100 seats for Nursing College will be given to those havi ng parent 
hospitals with 300 beds without insisting Medical College. 
 Distance from school to hospital has been relaxed from 15km to 30 km. However, for 
holly and tribal areas the maximum distance is 50km. 
 Eligibility criteria to admission i.e. (Marks) for Diploma and Degree have been relaxed by 
5%. 
 Relaxation for opening M.Sc(N) programme, superspeciality hospital can start M.Sc (N) 
without having under graduate programme.  
 Establishment of 157 nursing colleges was announced in the budget speech 2023 -24 in 
co-location with medical colleges which have been approved under the Centrally 
Sponsored Scheme (SCC) for ―Establishment of new Medical College attached with the 
existing district/referral hospital‖. It will add roughly 15000 nursing seats in the 
government sector.

RECOMMENDATIONS/OBSERVATIONS ON WHICH THE COMMITTEE      
DOES NOT DESIRE TO PURSUE IN VIEW OF THE GOVERNMENT'S REPLIES 
 
2.1 Budgetary Projection vis-a-vis Allocation and Expenditure 
 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
2.1.1 The Committee notes that since FY 2022-23, the Revised Estimates have consistently 
been lower than the Budget Estimates. This trend undermines the credibility of the 
Department‘s demand for higher fund allocations. Moreover, the concern is further reinforced 
by the fact that, except for FY  2023-24, the actual expenditure has remained below the 
Revised Estimate, which itself is already lower than the Budget Estimate. This raises concerns 
about the effective utilization of allocated funds in real terms. 
(Para 2.2.4 of the report) 
 
Action Taken 
 
2.1.2 The Department has made all efforts for optimum utilization of allocated funds and 
achieved expenditure target more than 100% with reference to RE allocation during the 
financial year 2023-24 and 2024-25.  
 
2.2 All India Institute of Medical Sciences (AIIMS), New Delhi 
 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
2.2.1 The Committee's assessment reveals a concerning trend in AIIMS, New Delhi's 
utilization of budgetary allocation. While the utilization of budgetary funds was satisfactory 
in FY 2023 -24, a significant shortfall occurred in FY 2024 -25, with only 81.96% of the 
revised estimate being spent till the end of January 2025. This underutilization is further 
underscored by the slow progress of ongoing construction projects. The key issues include 
delayed Project implementation, weak project planning and execution, delay in tendering 
process and environmental and regulatory hurdles. 
 (Para 3.18.4 of the Report) 
Action Taken

year 2024-25 is acknowledged and the following clarification is provided as under: -

National Health Mission 
2.3 Budgetary Allocation 
Recommendations/Observations 
2.3.1 The Committee notes that budgetary allocations to NHM have generally seen increase in 
funds, but expenditure in FY 2024 -25, as of February 7, 2025, stands out to be only 81.28% 
of the allocated amount. The operational period of NHM is five years i.e. up to FY 2026, with 
a total outlay of Rs. 1,65,356.63 crore from FY 2021 to FY 2026. The budget for FY 2025 -26 
is proposed to be Rs. 37,226.92 crore, a 3.4% increase from FY 2024 -25. Recognizing the 
under-utilization of allocated funds in FY 2024 -25, the Committee strongly recommends 
implementing a more robust monitoring and evaluation system to track expenditure and 
program implementation. This system should provide real -time data on fund utilization and 
identify bottlenecks in the disbursement and expenditure processes. The NHM should 
streamline administrative procedures, provide capacity building for state and district -level 
health officials in financial management, and ensure timely release of funds. The  committee 
further recommends that the NHM provides reports quarterly regarding the budget utilization 
and provides the reasoning for any deficit in spending, along with plans to correct the deficit. 
This will allow for more efficient program implementation and ensure that allocated resources 
are effectively used to achieve the NHM's objectives. 
(Para 4.2.6 of the Report)

funds,  
 Submission of quarterly Financial Monitoring Reports (FMRs) and Statement of Fund

Review and monitoring of scheme components. 
2.4 Pradhan Mantri Ayushman Bharat Health Infrastructure Mission (PM-ABHIM) 
Recommendations/Observations 
2.4.1 The Committee recommends the establishment of a robust, real -time monitoring system 
to track not only the physical construction progress but also the timely operationalization of 
completed health facilities, particularly U -AAMs. This system should include regular audits 
to verify the functionality and service delivery of operationalized units, mechanisms to ensure 
adequate staffing and resource allocation for newly completed facilities, review of impact on 
community health outcomes, and creation of a public  dashboard that displays the current 
status of each project, to increase transparency and accountability. The department should 
create a system to track the time taken between civil work completion and full operational 
status to ensure that completed proje cts are made operational with delay. By focusing on 
operational effectiveness, the Department can ensure that the investments made in 

93 
 
infrastructure translate into tangible and timely improvements in healthcare access and 
quality. 
(Para 4.7.11 of the Report) 
 
Action Taken 
 
2.4.2 The physical construction progresses of NCDC Infrastructure under PM -ABHIM are 
being tracked through several indicators under Output -Outcome Monitoring Framework of 
NITI Aayog. Physical and financial progress is being tracked through NCDCs, NPMU, State 
Nodal Officers of NCDC, OICs of NCDC branches.  Further, progress Monitoring System 
(PMS) portal has been created for monitoring and update about the progress of activities 
under PMABHIM. States have been oriented on the use and a re directed to update the 
activities on the portal for monitoring at the State and the National level. 
 Milestone based monitoring of civil work is undertaken through real time data. 
 
 Regular meetings under senior officer‘s help in the expedition of the projects. 
2.4.3 Data for operationalized U AAMs is also gathered through PMS Portal and reviewed 
during the various meetings. During reviews especial focus is given for Operationalization of 
facilities constructed. These meetings are held at regular intervals  and procurement of 
equipments and deployment of manpower is also reviewed in such cases, thus helps in 
keeping tab on the time taken for Constructed units to become operational. 
 
2.5 Ayushman Bharat- Pradhan Mantri Jan Arogya Yojana (AB-PMJAY) 
Recommendations/Observations 
2.5.1 The Committee has been given to understand that the operational period of National 
Health Mission (NHM) is from FY 2021 to FY 2026, therefore, coming to an end on 
31.03.2026. The Committee, considering the pivotal signifi cance of the Mission, is of the 
considered view that the operational period of NHM must be extended beyond 31st March 
2026 and cover at least the duration of 16th Finance Commission to enable the Department to 
achieve the remaining goals and objectives of health indicators under the Mission. 
(Para 4.8.14 of the Report) 
 
Action Taken 
2.5.2 The Ministry agrees with the recommendations of the Committee and has initiated the 
proposal for extension of NHM scheme beyond 31.03.2026 for the 16th Finance Commission 
cycle. 
 
 

94 
 
Issues Raised 
2.6 Issuance of ABHA Cards 
Recommendations/Observations 
2.6.1 The Committee notes the expansion of coverage from different segments of population 
under AB -PMJAY scheme from time to time, however, the Committee recommends the 
Department for the immediate and consistent integration of updated demographic data into the 
ABHA card issuance and PMJAY beneficiary identification processes. Specifically, while the 
PMJAY scheme has made efforts to expand its coverage beyond the 2011 cen sus, the 
fundamental reliance on out -dated data for initial identification and ABHA card linkage 
creates potential exclusion and inaccuracy. This continuous updating will ensure that the 
schemes remain relevant and equitable, reflecting the true health needs of the population. 
(Para 5.8.2 of the Report) 
Action Taken 
2.6.2 Ayushman Bharat Health Account (ABHA) is a foundational digital identity under the 
Ayushman Bharat Digital Mission (ABDM), intended for every citizen of the country. It 
enables individuals to create and maintain a lifelong, secure, and consent -based digital health 
record. While ABHA is universal, it plays a pivotal role in facilitating service delivery under 
targeted schemes such as Ayushman Bharat Pradhan Mantri Jan Arogya Yojana (AB -
PMJAY). AB-PMJAY was initially rolled out based on the Socio -Economic Caste Census 
(SECC) 2011, which served as the primary dataset to identify the most vulnerable families. 
Recognizing the limitations of this outdated data, the NHA has provided States and Union 
Territories with the flexibility to use updated, Aadhaar -verified databases of poor and 
vulnerable populations drawn from other welfare schemes. This enabled the replacement of 
unidentified or ineligible SECC -listed beneficiaries with other eligible individuals from 
similar socio-economic backgrounds, thereby improving the inclusivity and accuracy of the 
scheme. 
2.6.3 As and when new beneficiaries are identified under AB -PMJAY using these updated 
datasets, their ABHA numbers are simultaneously generat ed and linked to their PM -JAY 
records through the Beneficiary Identification System (BIS 2.0). This process ensures real -
time digital on boarding of beneficiaries and seamless integration of their healthcare 
transactions within the ABDM ecosystem.  The dyna mic and progressive expansion of the 
beneficiary base has included specific groups such as: - 
● ASHAs, Anganwadi Workers, and Helpers (AWWs/AWHs) (included in March 
2024), 
● Senior citizens aged 70 years and above, covered under the Ayushman Vay Vandana 
initiative (October 2024), 
● Particularly Vulnerable Tribal Groups (PVTGs) through coordination with the 
Ministry of Tribal Affairs, and 
● Gig workers, as proposed in Budget 2025-26. 

95 
 
2.6.4 These measures have ensured that the AB -PMJAY scheme evolves with the changin g 
demographic landscape and continues to serve the intended beneficiaries equitably. The 
continuous integration of updated demographic data into both ABHA issuance and 
beneficiary identification processes ensures accurate targeting, reduced exclusion, and 
enhanced service delivery 
 
2.7 Ayushman Bharat- Tribal Areas and Migrant Population 
Recommendations/Observations 
2.7.1 The Committee is of the view that initiatives like "Aapke Dwar Ayushman" campaign 
should not be confined to creation of Ayushman Cards as  highlighting vast number of 
creations of card is not the only objective of the scheme. Rather the Department should make 
efforts to ensure establishing robust healthcare infrastructure and service delivery in tribal and 
rural areas. While the portability feature and card creation drives are valuable, the core issue 
remains the lack of essential medical facilities like blood storage in PHCs, and availability of 
specialists like gynaecologists, particularly in regions with migrating populations. Therefore, 
the Committee urges upon the co -ordination between different components of the Ayushman 
Bharat scheme. The National Health Authority (NHA) should integrate these critical 
healthcare components with the "Aapke Dwar Ayushman" framework. This would entail 
deploying mobile medical units equipped with basic ICU facilities, blood storage capabilities, 
and specialist services like gynaecology to remote areas during the campaign. Additionally, 
the campaign should facilitate the establishment of sustainable blood col lection and storage 
systems at PHCs and prioritize the recruitment and deployment of specialists like 
gynaecologists to these underserved regions, to bridge the gap between awareness and 
accessible, comprehensive healthcare delivery. 
(Para 5.9.2 of the Report) 
 
Action Taken 
2.7.2 The Committee‘s recommendation to strengthen the "Aapke Dwar Ayushman" 
campaign by incorporating robust healthcare infrastructure, such as mobile medical units with 
ICU facilities, blood storage systems, and specialist services like gynaecology in tribal and 
rural areas, is acknowledged. NHA clarifies that providing mobile units for ICU facilities, 
blood storage capabilities, and specialist services fall outside the mandate of AB -PMJAY, 
which focuses on providing health cover of Rs5 lakh per family per year for secondary and 
tertiary care hospitalization. The "Aapke Dwar Ayushman" campaign has successfully created 
40.59 crore Ayushman Cards, with initiatives like Aapke Dwar Ayushman 3.0, PM -
JANMAN, Ayushman Bhav, and Viksit Bharat  Sankalp Yatra enhancing outreach in tribal 
and rural areas. The scheme‘s portability feature further supports migrant populations by 
enabling access to 31,005 empanelled hospitals (17,108 public). 
 
 

96 
 
2.8 Other Recommendations of the Committee 
Recommendations/Observations 
2.8.1 Making availability of Doctors at PHC/CHC level and lucrative pay and perks for 
specialist doctors ensuring their retention in the Government Hospitals. Department may 
persuade the States/UTs to overcome the shortage of d octors at PHCs/CHCs through various 
measures including the creation of separate Medical Services Recruitment Boards, specialist 
cadres, and providing flexibilities under NHM for recruitment and retention; 
(Para 5.20 (iv) of the Report) 
 
Action Taken 
2.8.2 MoHFW has been providing regular guidance to the States on HR matters and 
multiple letters have also been sent. The States have been advised to create separate Medical 
Recruitment Boards similar to the State of Tamil Nadu to fast track the recruitments und er 
health. The States have also been advised to create a Public Health Management cadre with 
components of Specialist cadre, Public Health cad re, health management cadre and teaching 
cadre. This will improve the availability of clinical as well as public health specialists by 
enabling separate and direct entry for post graduate doctors so that the States can attract and 
have adequate number of specialists and health managers in health system at all levels. 
Proposal for a Nursing cadre is also under active consideration. Under NHM, following types 
of incentives and honorarium are provided for encouraging doctors to practice in rural and 
underserved areas of the country: - 
 Hard area allowance to specialist doctors for serving in rural and remote areas and for  
their residential quarters so that they find it attractive to serve in public health facilities 
in such areas.  
 Honorarium to Gynecologists/ Emergency Obstetric Care (EmoC) trained, Pediatricians 
& Anesthetist/ Life Saving Anaesthesia Skills (LSAS) trained doctors is also provided to 
increase availability of specialists for conducting Cesarean Sections in rural & remote 
area. 
 Incentives like special incentives for doctors, incentive for Auxiliary Nurse and Midwife 
(ANM) for ensuring timely Antenatal Check-up (ANC) check -up and recording, 
incentives for conducting Adolescent Reproductive and Sexual Health activities.  
 States are also allowed to offer negotiable salary to attract specialist including flexibility 
in strategies such as ―You Quote We Pay‖.  
 Non-Monetary incentives such as preferential admission in post graduate courses for 
staff serving in difficult areas and improving accommodation arrangement in rural areas 
have also been introduced under NHM. 
2.8.3 Multi-skilling of doctors is supported under NHM to overcome the shortage of 
specialists. Skill upgradation of existing HR is another major strategy under NRHM for 
achieving improvement in health outcomes. 
 

97 
 
Recommendations/Observations 
2.8.4 Appreciating the decision of establishment of the Day  Care Cancer Centres in all 
districts, particularly to provide the services to rural areas, in next three years, the Committee 
apprehends about the practical implementation of the provision on the ground level provided 
adequate budgetary allocation is made  for the purpose. Although the Committee is uncertain 
about the Corporate Hospitals coming forward to join the programme, however, in case some 
Trust Hospitals come forward to participate in the programme, the Department should 
welcome the move and extend assistance & facilitate all kind of collaboration, co -operation 
and co-ordination network; 
(Para 5.20 (ix) of the Report) 
 
Action Taken 
 
2.8.5 The Government of India has prioritized the expansion of cancer care services through 
the establishment of Day Care Cancer Centres (DCCCs) in all districts, ensuring accessibility 
for patients closer to their homes. In the Union Budget 2025 –26, the Government announced 
the saturation of all district hospitals with DCCCs within the next three years, with 200 
centres to be established in 2025 –26. These centres will offer chemotherapy services at the 
district level, while advanced care will be delivered through Tertiary Cancer Care Centres 
(TCCCs) and State Cancer Institutes (SCIs). Tertiary -level cancer care provided by corporate 
hospitals and Trust hospitals does not form part of the Day Care Chemotherapy Centre 
(DCCC) initiative, which is focused on strengthening public healthcare infrastructure through 
district hospital.

CHAPTER–III 
 
RECOMMENDATIONS/OBSERVATIONS IN RESPECT OF WHICH REPLIES OF 
THE GOVERNMENT HAVE NOT BEEN ACCEPTED BY THE COMMITTEE  
 
3.1 Staff Strength in the Department 
 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
3.1.1 The Committee observes that the vacancy under the different Divisions of the 
Department of Health and Family Welfare is 428 against the sanctioned strength of 1486 for 
the year 2024. The Committee is concerned to note that there has been negligible 
improvement in filling up the vacant posts from 2023 -24 when a tota l of 454 posts were 
reported to be vacant. The Committee notes that there is shortage of Officers/Staffs at various 
levels in the Department of Health and Family Welfare which may impact the effective 
administrative functioning of the Ministry. The Committ ee, therefore, reiterates its 
recommendation made in the 143 rd Report that the Ministry needs to take appropriate steps to 
fill up the vacant positions at various levels and implement better work management practices 
in the Department. The Committee recomm ends the Ministry to take up the matter of 
vacancies with the Cadre Controlling Authorities and recruiting agencies to expedite the 
recruitment of officers/Staffs at all levels in the Ministry. 
(Para 1.6.2 of the report) 
Action Taken 
 
3.1.2This Department has regularly pursued the issue related to filling up of vacant posts 
against sanctioned strength of staff with the respective Cadre Controlling Authority 
(Department of Personnel and Training (DoPT), Department of Economic Affairs (DEA) an d 
Ministry of Statistics and Programme Implementation (MoSPI) etc.). The Matter is being 
followed up with the concerned Cadre Controlling Authorities from time to time.  Further 
posting of 63 Assistant Statistical Officers (ASOs) and 60 Multi Task Services (MTS) 
personnel in the Ministry have been made. This has reduced the vacancy position by 123 (i.e. 
from 428 to 305). 
 
Further Recommendation 
 
3.1.3 The Committee appreciates the efforts made by the Ministry in pursuing the 
matter of vacancies with the Cadre Controlling Authorities concerned, thereby resulting 
into reduction of 123 vacant posts  due to posting of 63Assistant Statistical Officers and 
60 Multi Tasking Staff. The Committee,  however, observes that despite the  efforts so 
made, a substantial number of 305 posts still remains vacant, which may yet continue to 
hamper the administrative efficiency and timely execution of the Ministry’s 
programmes and schemes. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Ministry 
adopt a time-bound action plan, in coor dination with the Cadre Controlling Authorities 
and recruiting agencies, to fill up the remaining 305 vacant posts at the earliest so that 

99 
 
the functional proficiency of the Ministry is optimised and set objectives of various plans 
are achieved without any stumbling block. 
 
3.2 Budgetary Projection vis-a-vis Allocation and Expenditure 
 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
3.2.1 Over the past five years, it has been observed that the demand projected by the 
Department of Health and Family Welfare has been consistently low and has declined 
significantly compared to the 2021 -22 level. Furthermore, budgetary allocations have 
continuously fallen short of the Department‘s projected Demands for Grants, averaging a 
meager 0.3% of GDP at current prices. 
(Para 2.2.2 of the report) 
Action Taken 
 
3.2.2 The demand projected by the Department of Health and Family Welfare is based on 
the demands of the various States/Implementing Agencies of the State/UT Governments/ 
Institutes and overall envelope available under the schemes. 
 
 It is important to note that the overall allocation to the Department of Health and 
Family Welfare has increased year to year. 
 
 The budget allocation for DoHFW has increased by 174% from Rs. 35,063 crore in 
2013-14 (BE) to Rs. 95,957.87 crore in 2025-26 (BE). 
 
 As per National Health Account Estimates, the Government health expenditure has 
increased from Rs. 1.30 lakh crore (2013-14) to Rs. 4.34 lakh crore (2021-22) which is 
an increase of 234%.  
 
 The GHE as a percentage of General Government Expenditure (GGE) has increased 
from 3.78% (2013-14) to 6.12% (2021-22). 
 
Further Recommendation 
 
3.2.3 The Committee is of the opinion that health is a multiplier sector of the economy, 
determining productive capital formation and capacity building.  Therefore, the 
Government needs to invest maximally to build other significant superstructures of the 
economy. The Committee is not appreciative of the plea of the Department of 
highlighting budgetary allocation of too wide base years that is BE 2013 -14 an d BE 
2025-26.The Committee observes that despite the increase in the absolute budgetary 
figures i.e., from Rs. 35,063 crore in BE 2013 -14  to Rs. 95,957. 87 crore  in BE 2025-26, 
the health expenditure continues to remain low as a proportion of GDP i.e. 0.3 % at 
current price. The Committee, therefore,  emphasizes that sustained under -projection of 

100 
 
demand may constrain the human capacity building of the nation to address emerging 
challenges and achieve national health targets. The Committee,  in this regard, 
recommends that the Ministry adopt need-driven approach while projecting its financial 
requirement, ensuring realistic estimation of financial requirement under continuing 
health plans and Schemes/ programmes/ projects as well as futuristic plans and projects. 
The Committee further urges the Ministry to engage with the Ministry of Finance to 
progressively enhance the projected demand of health sector with a view to achieving 
the national goal of increasing public health expenditure to at least 2.5% of GDP in line 
with the National Health Policy,2017. 
 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
3.2.4 For the fiscal year 2025 -26, the projected demand has increased to Rs1,00,702.79 
crore. However, given the inflation rate of 5.2% in December 2024, the population growth of 
the country and the National Health Policy‘s goal of raising government health expenditure to 
2.5% of GDP by 2025, the Committee believes that the Department of Health and Family 
Welfare must reassess its policy framework. The Committee, therefore, recomme nds the 
Department to request the Ministry of Finance for higher allocations for significantly 
enhancing healthcare infrastructure and healthcare services nationwide. This effort must be 
undertaken with urgency, particularly in light of the challenges face d during the COVID -19 
pandemic, to ensure that the country is better prepared for the post -COVID era and for any 
future pandemics. 
 
(Para 2.2.3 of the report) 
Action Taken 
3.2.5 The Department of Health and Family Welfare   has taken up with States to prio ritize 
allocation to health sector and enhance their health budgets at least to 8% of total State 
budget. The budget allocation for DoHFW has increased by 174% from Rs. 35,063 crore in 
2013-14 (BE) to Rs. 95,957.87 crore in 2025 -26 (BE).  Further, the 15 th Finance Commission 
for the grant period from 2020 -21 to 2025-26 has provided Rs. 70,051 crore Grants for health 
through the local Government. 
 The Department has agreed that higher budgetary allocations are essential to bolster 
healthcare infrastructure and service delivery mechanisms across the country.  
 Gaps for addressing gaps in the Healthcare systems have been observed as part of the 
assessment exercise for 16th Finance Commission. 
 With the long -term goal to better prepare our country against public health emergencies, 
Pradhan Mantri - Ayushman Bharat Health Infrastructure Mission (PM-ABHIM) has been 
launched with the intent to enhance the capacity of primary, secondary and tertiary health 
care facilities/systems and institutes for identifying and man aging any new and emerging 
diseases. PM -ABHIM is a Centrally Sponsored Scheme with some Central Sector 
components. The total outlay of the scheme is Rs 64,180 crore from 2021-22 to 2025-26. 
 
 

101 
 
Further Recommendation 
3.2.6 The Committee applauds the initiati ves taken by the Ministry, including its 
engagement with States to enhance their health budgets to 8% of total State budget, the 
provision of health grants through the 15th Finance Commission, and the 
implementation of the Pradhan Mantri –Ayushman Bharat He alth Infrastructure 
Mission (PM-ABHIM) to strengthen healthcare infrastructure and preparedness. While 
these steps are commendable, the Committee observes that the overall public health 
expenditure continues to remain below the National Health Policy, 2017  target of 2.5% 
of GDP. The Committee, therefore, reiterates the need for the Ministry to pursue the 
matter vigorously with the Ministry of Finance to secure higher allocations in 
forthcoming budget cycles, particularly in light of rising inflation, popula tion growth, 
and post -pandemic healthcare needs.  The Committee believes that the Department 
would work upon further to eliminate gaps by addressing lacuna in the healthcare 
system and seek requisite fund from the 16 th Finance Commission. The Committee 
further recommends the Ministry to establish measurable outcome indicators to monitor 
the effective utilization of funds under PM -ABHIM and ensure that both Central and 
State governments align their health budget priorities with the broader goal of achieving 
equitable, accessible, and resilient healthcare infrastructure across the country. 
 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
3.2.7 The Committee notes that the primary determinant for revenue and capital outlay in 
healthcare bud geting is the annual, recurring demands from program divisions within the 
Department of Health & Family Welfare, coupled with the progress of ongoing projects. 
While the social service nature of the Department inherently limits capital expenditure, a 
recent push for infrastructure upgrades is evident, with "GIA -Capital Creation" (grants-in-aid 
for capital creation) playing a significant role in boosting effective capital spending, despite 
being categorized under revenue. Analysisofthe five -year trend reveals to the Committee that 
revenue expenditure consistently dominates, averaging over 90% of the total budget. 
However, the effective capital expenditure, including GIA -Capital, shows a fluctuating 
pattern in the period from FY 2021 -22 to FY 2025 -26, decreasing consistently since the FY 
2023-24, from 27.22 % in 2022 -23 to 15.60% in 2025 -26 of the total allocation. The 
Committee, therefore, recommends establishing a formalized, multi -year strategic capital 
expenditure plan, distinct from annual recurring demand s, that clearly outlines infrastructure 
development priorities and targets, including specific allocations for GIA -Capital, to ensure 
sustained and predictable growth in healthcare capital assets, thereby creating a more 
balanced approach between revenue and capital spending. 
(Para 2.2.8 of the report) 
Action Taken 
 
3.2.8 Fluctuating demand under Capital/GIA Capital is a normal phenomenon as it depends 
on the demand of funds required as per the pace and stage of the projects.  The budgetary 
allocation of the Revenue and Capital part of overall Budget of the Department is formulated 
considering the overall envelop of the Schemes/Non -Schemes based on recurring demands 

102 
 
raised by the Programme Divisions on year -to-year basis and sta tus of on -going projects 
progress at different stages. The nature of this Department is ‗Social Service Sector‘ hence 
there is a limited scope of Capital expenditure. The effective capital expenditure involves 
Capital Expenditure and Grant -in-Aid for Capit al Creation.  The Grant -in-Aid for Capital 
creation is revenue expenditure for the Department with the purpose to provide funds to 
autonomous bodies and State/UT Governments for creating the capital assets.  The 
requirement for funds for capital expenditure  depends on   pace and completion of various 
stages of construction work, completion of long drawn process of procurement of machinery 
and equipment and settlement of bills by Implementing and executing agencies etc.  However, 
the suggestions given by the Committee are noted. 
 
Further Recommendation 
3.2.9 The Committee understands that  capital expenditure inherently fluctuates based 
on the pace and stage of projects , and that the social service nature of the Department 
limits its scope, however, the Committee emphasizes the importance of forward-looking 
planning for raising healthcare infrastructure in far flung rural, semi rural and tribal 
areas and strengthening the primary, Secondary and tertiary healthcare hospitals where 
centrally sponsored s chemes are required to be launched and implemented with due  
impetus. The Committee, therefore, feels that the Ministry should develop a 'pipeline' 
document that, while accounting for the fluctuating nature of capital demands, maps out 
the planned trajectory of major infrastructure and capital creation projects  (including 
GIA-Capital) over a three to five -year period. This strategic document should clearly 
articulate the projected annual requirement and justification  for capital funds, thereby 
enabling more predictable and sustained year -on-year growth  in capital allocations 
(even if actual expenditure fluctuates) and  thereby ensuring that the budget process is 
driven by pre-determined development goals  besides considering the recurring annual 
demands and project status. 
 
3.3 Government Health Expenditure in India 
 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
3.3.1 The Committee acknowledges that various Government schemes have progressively 
expanded the scope of social security and broadened the list of beneficiaries. This sustained 
intervention has contributed to a notable decline in Out -of-Pocket Expenditure (OOPE ) on 
healthcare, which has reduced from 48.8% in 2017 -18 to 39.4% in 2021 -22. The Committee 
is quite optimistic that the current figures for OOPE might be lower than 39.4. However, the 
Committee apprehends that 39.4% OOPE out of THE continues to be a signi ficant barrier to 
accessing quality healthcare. As per the Global Multidimensional Poverty Index 2024, India is 
home to approximately 234 million poor people, the highest in the world. Also, in the five 
countries with the largest number of people living in  poverty, India is the only country with 
medium Human Development Index (HDI) and the remaining four are low HDI. Given this 
large population deprived of basic amenities, increased Government health spending is crucial 
to ensuring affordable healthcare access. 
(Para 2.3.10 of the report) 

103 
 
Action Taken 
 
3.3.2 As per the National Health Accounts, there has been a steady increase in Government 
Health Expenditure, from 1.15% in 2013 -14 to 1.84% 2021-22 as a share of GDP. During the 
same period, Government Health  Expenditure (GHE) as a share of Total Health Expenditure 
(THE) has significantly increased, which was 29.0% in 2014 -15 and 48.0% in 2021 -22. 
Further, the Central Government has taken several initiatives for supplementing the efforts of 
the States for prov iding quality and affordable healthcare services to the people. Under 
National Health Mission, the Government has taken many steps towards universal health 
coverage, by supporting the State Governments in providing accessible & affordable 
healthcare to peo ple. The National Health Mission provides financial and technical support 
for improvement in health infrastructure, availability of adequate human resources to man 
health facilities, to improve availability and accessibility to quality health care especial ly for 
the underserved and marginalized groups in rural areas. National Free Drugs Service Initiative 
and National Free Diagnostic Service has been rolled out to ensure the availability of essential 
drugs and diagnostic facilities and reduce the OOPE of th e patients visiting the public health 
facilities. To provide quality health care services especially for the underserved and 
marginalized groups in rural and undeserved areas, various initiatives carried out under NHM 
by the  Government in the country are operationalization of Ayushman Arogya Mandirs, 
support for National Ambulance Services, Mobile Medical Units, ASHAs, 24 x 7 Services 
and First Referral Facilities, Prime Minister's National Dialysis Programme, various activities 
under Reproductive & Child Health and Universal Immunization programme.  
 
3.3.3 Moreover, the Government has launched four mission mode projects, namely PM -
Ayushman Bharat Health Infrastructure Mission (PM -ABHIM), Ayushman Arogya Mandir 
(AAMs), Pradhan Mantri Jan Arogya Yojana (PMJA Y) and Ayushman Bharat Digital 
Mission (ABDM). Other notable initiatives are setting up of new AIIMS, upgrading of 
Government medical colleges under the Pradhan Mantri Swasthya Suraksha Yojana 
(PMSSY), support to States/UTs for setting up of new medical co lleges as well as for 
increasing UG and PG medical seats, provision of free drugs and free diagnostic facilities at 
Primary Health Centres (PHC)/ Community Health Centres (CHC) and district hospital level, 
substantial increase in availability of government ambulances etc. The Jan Aushadhi Kendra‘s 
(JAKs) under Pradhan Mantri Bhartiya JanaushadhiPariyojana (PMBJP) provides affordable 
generic medicines, including anti-cancer medicines. 
 
Further Recommendation 
3.3.4 The Committee appreciates the sustained effo rts of the Department of Health 
and Family to expand healthcare access and affordability through flagship initiatives 
such as NHM, PMJAY, PM -ABHIM, ABDM, and PMBJP, which have collectively 
contributed to the reduction in Out-of-Pocket Expenditure (OOPE) and enhancement of 
healthcare infrastructure. The Committee also acknowledges the steady rise in 
Government Health Expenditure as a share of GDP and Total Health Expenditure. 
However, the Committee observes that OOPE at 39.4%in 2021 -22 still remains 
considerably high, particularly given India’s large population living in poverty and 
vulnerable socio-economic conditions. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the 

104 
 
Ministry intensify its focus on strengthening primary healthcare delivery through 
Ayushman Arogya Mandirs, ensuring wider coverage of free drugs and diagnostics, and 
improving the efficiency and reach of Jan Aushadhi  Kendras in rural and remote areas. 
Further, the Committee urges the Ministry to establish a clear roadmap with 
measurable milestones f or reducing OOPE below 20%withinthe next two years by 
deepening financial protection under PMJAY, enhancing State -level health spending, 
and improving public awareness of available government health schemes and services. 
 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
3.3.5 The Committee observes that GHE as % of GDP of India (1.84%) is very low when 
compared to the developed countries like USA, UK, Japan and Germany. Hence, there is 
ample inspiration for the Government to make sustained efforts to increase the GHE which 
would have the declining effects on OOPE. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the 
Government intensify efforts to increase GHE and further reduce OOPE. The Committee 
urges the Government to adopt best practices from High -Income Countries (HICs), wher e 
nearly 70% of healthcare expenditure is publicly funded, and ensuring greater financial 
protection for citizens. 
(Para 2.3.11 of the report) 
Action Taken 
 
3.3.6 Following National Health Policy prescription, the GHE as a share of GDP has 
increased from 1 .15% in 2013 -14 to 1.84% as a share of GDP. In terms of share in Total 
Health Expenditure (THE), it has increased from 28.6% to 48.0% during this period. 
Concurrent to this increase in GHE, the share of OOPE in THE has declined from 64.2% to 
39.4% during the same period. It shows greater financial protection for citizens of India. 
The Central Government has taken several initiatives for supplementing the efforts of the 
States for providing quality and affordable healthcare services to the people. Under National 
Health Mission, the Government has taken many steps towards universal he alth coverage, by 
supporting the State Governments in providing accessible & affordable healthcare to people.  
Further, the Government has launched four mission mode projects, namely PM -Ayushman 
Bharat Health Infrastructure Mission (PM -ABHIM), Ayushman Aro gya Mandir (AAMs), 
Pradhan Mantri Jan Arogya Yojana (PMJAY) and Ayushman Bharat Digital Mission 
(ABDM). Other notable initiatives are setting up of new AIIMS, upgrading of Government 
medical colleges under the Pradhan Mantri Swasthya Suraksha Yojana (PMSSY ), support to 
States/UTs for setting up of new medical colleges as well as for increasing UG and PG 
medical seats, provision of free drugs and free diagnostic facilities at Primary Health Centres 
(PHC)/ Community Health Centres (CHC) and district hospital level, substantial increase in 
availability of government ambulances etc. 
 
Further Recommendation 
 
3.3.7 The Committee commends the steady increase in Government Health 
Expenditure (GHE) both as a share of GDP and of Total Health Expenditure (THE), as 

105 
 
well as the corresponding decline in Out -of-Pocket Expenditure (OOPE), reflecting the 
Government’s commitment to strengthening public health financing.  The Committee,  
however, observes that despite this progress, India’s GHE remains significantly below 
global benchmarks, particularly those of high -income countries where the higher 
volume of health expenditure is funded  by Governments of the countries. The 
Committee, therefore, reiterates that the Government should continue to enhance GHE 
in a phased and sustai ned manner to achieve the National Health Policy target of 2.5% 
of GDP from existing 1.84% as share of GDP  within projected time frame. The 
Committee further recommends that the Department  must undertake a comparative 
study of successful international heal th financing models to identify adaptable best 
practices for India’s  investment on healthcare sector. The Committee also  urges the 
Department to ensure effective convergence and coordination among key national health 
missions and schemes so that increased expenditure directly translates into improved 
service delivery, equitable access, and further reduction of OOPE for the whole  
population, especially among vulnerable and low-income groups. 
 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
3.3.8 The Committee observes that the data on General Health Expenditure (GHE), Total 
Health Expenditure (THE), Out-of-Pocket Expenditure (OOPE), and other relevant indicators 
is only available up to the year 2021 -22, with the National Health Accounts (NHA) Estimates 
for 2021-22 being released in September 2024. The Committee expresses concern over delay 
in updating data as old and obsolete data significantly hampers the assessment of the progress 
made by various government schemes and policies. The Committee, therefore, recommends 
the Government to update data pertaining to GHE, THE, OOPE, etc. without delay, sooner 
the better. 
(Para 2.3.15 of the report) 
Action Taken 
 
3.3.9 The National Health Accounts (NHA) Estimates are based on actual Expenditure by 
Central and State Govt. Entire actual  expenditures available at a lag of 18Months.   Currently, 
the preparation of NHA 2022-23 is underway. 
 
Further Recommendation 
3.3.10 The Committee takes into account the response of the Department which 
attributes that the data lag to the inherent process of compiling actual expenditure from 
Central and State Governments. The Committee notes with concern that the NHA 
Estimates are available at an 18-month lag , while currently preparing the NHA for 
2022-23, confirms the systematic nature of the delay but the  Department fails to outline 
any measure to mitigate its impact. The Committee does not agree with plea of the 
Department for the operational necessity for an 18 -month lag in utilizing confirmed 
actual expenditure data, the Committee, however, recommends t hat the Department 
must concurrently develop and publish Provisional Health Expenditure Estimates  for 
the most recent financial year. These provisional estimates, based on Revised Estimates 

106 
 
(RE) for the Central Government and the latest available Budget Es timates (BE)  or 
expenditure trend data for the States, should be released no later than six months  after 
the completion of specific financial year to provide policymakers and analysts with a 
timely, albeit interim, view of key indicators like GHE, THE, and  OOPE, thereby 
overcoming the issue of critical information deficit that currently hampers effective 
policy assessment. 
 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
3.3.11 In today's digital age, where Information Technology and Artificial Intelligence play a 
key role,  it is crucial to leverage these tools for the efficient collection and real -time 
processing of data. Therefore, the Committee recommends that the Government take 
immediate steps to integrate digital tools into the data collection process. Additionally, th e 
Government should ensure that data for a given financial year is made available by the last 
quarter of the following financial year. This approach will rationalize data analysis, enabling 
more effective evaluation and improvement of government schemes an d policies, ensuring 
they are aligned with the intended objectives and targeted effectively. 
(Para 2.3.16 of the report) 
Action Taken 
 
3.3.12 Best efforts have been made and continue to be made to integrate the data collection 
process into the digital tool s and be available in form of MIS or statistics. In alignment with 
the Committee‘s recommendations, MoHFW has already initiated the adoption of digital tools 
for data collection and processing of data. 
 
Further Recommendation 
 
3.3.13 The Committee assesses that Ministry's response, stating that "best efforts have 
been made" and that the adoption of digital tools has "already initiated," is vague and 
fails to provide any concrete plan, timeline, or measurable targets  for the complete 
digitization of the data collection process, particularly regarding the timely release of 
health expenditure data. The Committee understands that the Ministry has simply noted 
the intent of the Committee’s recommendation but without addressing the specific 
concern for a definitive annual data release deadline  (i.e., by the last quarter of the 
following financial year). The Committee, therefore, strongly urges the Department to 
establish and formally announce a clear, documented, and phased Digital Data 
Integration Roadmap . Such roadmap must include specific milestones  for the full 
deployment of MIS/digital tools across all major schemes, a guaranteed calendar date  
for the publication of provisional health expenditure data, and an assurance that the 
final, actual expenditure data  will be made available no later than the end of the third 
quarter (December) of the financial year immediately following the reporting period, 
thereby moving beyond mere initiation and ensuring accountability in data timeliness. 
 

107 
 
3.4 15th Finance Commission Health Grants 
 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
3.4.1 The Committee observes that, as of January 30, 2025, only 39.42% of the funds 
approved by the 15th Finance Commission for the States have been released, with only the 
funds for the fiscal year 2021 -22 being fully disbursed. The States have reported spending 
only 59.31% of the released funds. These funds were specifically allocated to address gaps in 
primary healthcare facilities across the country. However, the States' low utilizatio n of these 
funds undermines the purpose of this financial support. In light of these concerns, the 
Committee strongly recommends that the Government conduct a comprehensive study to 
determine the underlying causes of this under -utilization and implement me asures to resolve 
these issues. Furthermore, the Union Government should establish a dedicated 
communication channel with the States to facilitate continuous dialogue and ensure timely 
and effective resolution of challenges, thereby optimizing the utilization of these critical funds 
for primary healthcare. The Committee expresses serious concern that the Department must 
persuade the states for optimal utilization of funds released to the States. 
(Para 2.4.4 of the report) 
Action Taken 
3.4.2 To assess the ef fective implementation of the 15th FC Health Grant, the MOHFW, 
through NHSRC in collaboration with the National Institute of Public Finance and Policy 
(NIPFP), is conducting a study on implementation effectiveness of 15th Finance Commission. 
The study aims  to address systemic challenges faced by states, particularly in financial 
utilization and infrastructure development under the grant. Field visits have been completed in 
the states of Meghalaya, Telangana, Gujarat, Uttar Pradesh, Madhya Pradesh and Odisha . 
Regular review meetings with the States and the sessions on the State level progress updates 
including the fund utilization have been conducted in the regional workshops held last year. 
 
Further Recommendation 
 
3.4.3 The Committee accords the initiation made by the Department for a study on the 
implementation effectiveness of the 15thFinance Commission Health Grant through 
NHSRC and NIPFP, complete with initial field visits, which are steps toward 
understanding the underlying causes of under -utilization, however, the action taken is 
silent on establishing the dedicated communication channel for continuous dialogue and 
immediate resolution of challenges. Therefore, the Committee recommends that the 
Ministry must immediately operationalize the pro posed dedicated communication 
mechanism, specifying whether it will be a high -level Empowered Working Group or a 
Joint Steering Committee involving both Central and State Finance/Health Secretaries, 
which should meet quarterly. This body should be tasked n ot merely with reviewing 
fund utilization, but empowered specifically, for adjudicating and resolving financial, 
procedural, and procurement bottlenecks identified during the ongoing study and 
regular review meetings, thereby translating the diagnostic findings into proactive, time-
bound remedial action to accelerate the optimal utilization of the fund allocated to 
address gaps in crucial primary healthcare facilities across the country. 
 

108 
 
3.5 Central Government Health Scheme (CGHS) 
 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
3.5.1 The Committee takes into account the proposal to establish a State -of-the-Art Multi-
facility Centre in Mumbai. The Committee reiterates its recommendation, as made in its 155th 
Report, for setting up at least ten CGHS h ospitals with advanced facilities and research 
capabilities, particularly for critical illnesses such as cancer, cardiovascular diseases, and 
renal failure. The Committee further desires that at least one such advanced multi -facility 
centre be established in major cities across the country. 
 
(Para 3.1.7 of the report) 
Action Taken 
 
3.5.2 The CGHS is empaneling many multi-specialty Hospitals in all CGHS cities including 
Mumbai. MoU has already been signed with State of Art Cancer Hospital ―Tata Memorial 
Cancer Hospital and Research Centre‖ Mumbai, for providing cashless treatment to eligible 
CGHS beneficiaries. Large number of tertiary care Hospitals are already empaneled under 
CGHS in various cities for providing cashless treatment to eligible CGHS benef iciaries. 174 
HCOs are empaneled under CGHS Mumbai. MOU has been signed with AIIMS and other 
INIs in various CGHS cities to provide cashless treatment to eligible CGHS beneficiaries. 
 
Further Recommendation 
 
3.5.3 The Committee notes with appreciation the steps taken by the Department to 
enhance access to advanced medical care for CGHS beneficiaries through the 
empanelment of a large number of tertiary care and multi -specialty hospitals, including 
reputed institutions such as Tata Memorial Cancer Hospital a nd AIIMS. Nevertheless, 
the Committee observes that reliance solely on empaneled hospitals may not be a 
sustainable long -term solution to meet the growing healthcare demands of CGHS 
beneficiaries, particularly in major metropolitan areas where patient load  is already too 
high and also because of indifference of high profile profiteering healthcare 
metropolitan private hospitals towards CGHS beneficiaries due to low OPD and 
procedural rate of CGHS. The Committee, therefore, reiterates its earlier 
recommendation for establishing dedicated CGHS hospitals with state -of-the-art 
facilities and integrated research capabilities in major cities across the country. These 
hospitals should focus on critical and lifestyle -related diseases such as cancer, 
cardiovascular a ilments, renal disorders, and other non -communicable diseases. The 
Committee further recommends that the Ministry prepare a detailed roadmap with 
timelines, funding strategy, and inter-agency coordination plan to operationalize at least 
ten such advanced C GHS hospitals in a phased manner to ensure comprehensive, 
affordable, and high -quality healthcare services for government employees and 
pensioners. 
 

109 
 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
3.5.4 The Committee wishes to highlight with concerns over the quality of medicines 
dispensed at dispensary/pharmacy counters. At several time, apprehensions and complaints 
have been raised regarding the effectiveness of medicines procured through MSO and JAP, 
underscoring the critical importance of quality in healthcare service s. Ensuring compliance 
with the World Health Organization‘s (WHO) Good Manufacturing Practice (GMP) standards 
is essential, and any quality -related complaints must be addressed seriously and quickly to 
generate faith towards quality of medicines delivered at dispensary counters. 
(Para 3.1.9 of the report) 
Action Taken 
 
3.5.5 Revised schedule has already been notified by CDSCO. It is also clarified that Jan 
Aushadhi medicines fall under Pradhan Mantri Bhartiya Janaushadhi Yojana, a separate 
initiative under Department of Pharmaceutical operating independently from CGHS and these 
are not part of CGHS procurement system.   
 
Further Recommendation 
 
3.5.6 The Committee is aware of the distinction between medicines supplied under the 
Pradhan Mantri Bhartiya Janaushadhi Yojana (PMBJP) and those procured through 
the Central Government Health Scheme (CGHS) system notwithstanding the fact stated 
the Medicine procured under PMBJP are supplied at CGHS centres.  The Committee 
takes note of the notification of a revis ed schedule by the Central Drugs Standard 
Control Organisation (CDSCO) but with no detail. However, the Committee emphasizes 
that the assurance of drug quality within the CGHS framework remains of utmost 
importance to safeguard beneficiary health and trust  in the system. The Committee, 
therefore, recommends the Department to establish a robust and transparent 
mechanism for periodic quality testing of medicines supplied through the Medical Store 
Organisation (MSO) and other CGHS procurement channels, ensurin g full compliance 
with WHO-GMP and national quality standards. Further, the Committee advises that a 
centralized complaint redressal and pharmacovigilance system be developed to 
promptly address and track quality -related grievances, supported by random sam pling, 
third-party audits, and public disclosure of test results to enhance accountability and 
confidence in the medicines dispensed at CGHS dispensaries. 
 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
3.5.7 The Committee takes into account the acknowledgement of the Dep artment about the 
quality assurance of drugs, and the need for stronger quality assurance measures viz. 
implementing a systematic drug testing program at the Wellness Centre level. The Directorate 
is also considering empaneling the Quality Council of India  for enhanced monitoring. The 
Committee, therefore, recommends that the Government establish a rigorous quality control 
mechanism without delay, ensuring systematic drug testing at the Wellness Centre level. 

110 
 
Additionally, the Department should conduct surp rise audits by randomly testing medicine 
batches through an independent NABL-accredited or internationally reputed laboratory. 
 
 
(Para 3.1.10 of the report) 
Action Taken 
 
3.5.8 The recommendation of the Hon‘ble committee has been duly noted. It is submitted 
that drugs procured through MSO/ GMSD are already subjected to random sampling by 
medical officers and are tested at two NABL -accredited laboratories. Only after receiving 
satisfactory test reports are these drugs supplied to CGHS Wellness Centre‘s. 
 
Further Recommendation 
 
3.5.9 The Committee notes the Ministry’s assurance that drugs procured through 
MSO/GMSD are subjected to random sampling and tested at NABL -accredited 
laboratories before being supplied to CGHS Wellness Centres. While this is a  positive 
step toward maintaining quality assurance, the Committee emphasizes that a more 
comprehensive, continuous, and transparent quality control mechanism is essential to 
prohibit spurious drugs and maintaining high quality medicine for upholding the 
highest standards of patient safety and trust. The Committee, therefore, recommends 
that the Department must institutionalize a structured, periodic drug testing and audit 
framework at the Wellness Centre level, including unannounced and surprise 
inspections and random post -distribution sampling of medicines. Further, the 
Committee advises the Department to expedite the empanelment of the Quality Council 
of India or any other competent independent agency for enhanced oversight, introduce a 
digital tracking s ystem to record test results and quality compliance, and publicly 
disclose periodic quality audit summaries to ensure transparency and accountability in 
the drug supply chain under CGHS. 
 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
3.5.10 The Committee continues to rec ommend the Department to simplify referral 
procedures further as there is need to significantly reduce the burden on patients and their 
families by eliminating the requirement for repeated visits to dispensaries during illness. The 
Committee notes the prov ision made allowing CGHS pensioner beneficiaries aged 70 and 
above to directly consult specialists without requiring a CGHS referral, however, the 
Committee reiterates its recommendation made in the 155th Report to further review the 
guidelines and extend the benefit to all CGHS pensioners/beneficiaries aged 60 and above for 
continuing medical care to CGHS beneficiaries. Allowing direct specialist consultations at 
empaneled private hospitals without a CGHS referral would ensure consistency and 
continuity of care with the existing system, where pensioners/beneficiaries aged 60 and above 
already receive cashless treatment from these hospitals. 
(Para 3.1.12 of the report) 

111 
 
Action Taken 
 
3.5.11 The relaxation of consultation norms with respect to elderly CGHS beneficiaries was 
first permitted on 29 th May 2019 wherein all CGHS beneficiaries aged 75 years above were 
permitted to seek direct OPD consultation from specialists of private hospitals empaneled 
under CGHS. This benefit was further extended to beneficiar ies aged 70 years and above 
vides the departmental instructions dated 28 th June 2024. The reform was primarily aimed at 
improving the service delivery of health benefits under CGHS. Moreover, all the CGHS 
beneficiaries have the option to take telephonic co nsultations with doctors via video or audio 
calls, at ( https://esanjeevaniopd.in), by the CGHS login details. On e -Sanjeevani portal, the 
beneficiaries have the access to doctors across various specialists, including general medicine, 
dermatology, gynecology and more. Doctors can issue e -prescriptions and medical advice, 
which can be used for further treatment or medicine procurement. 
 
Further Recommendation 
 
3.5.12 The Committee appreciates the progressive relaxation  of consultation norms that 
now allow CGHS beneficiaries aged 70 years and above to directly consult specialists at 
empaneled hospitals, as well as the introduction of digital health services through the e -
Sanjeevani portal to facilitate remote consultatio ns. However, the Committee observes 
that a large proportion of CGHS pensioners and beneficiaries aged 60 years and above 
continue to face difficulties in accessing specialist care due to procedural requirements 
and physical limitations. The Committee, ther efore, reiterates its earlier 
recommendation that the age threshold for direct specialist consultation be further 
reduced to 60 years to ensure timely and convenient access to medical care for senior 
citizens. The Committee also recommends that the Ministr y strengthen the integration 
between e-Sanjeevani services and CGHS records to enable seamless continuity of care, 
including digital referrals, e -prescription management, and follow -up consultations. 
Further, awareness campaigns should be conducted to fami liarize elderly beneficiaries 
with the use of teleconsultation services, thereby reducing their dependency on in-person 
visits to Wellness Centres for routine medical needs. 
 
 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
3.5.13 The Committee observes significantly high number of vacancies in CGHS 
dispensaries, with 2,023 positions (28.81% of the sanctioned strength) unfilled in Allopathic 
Dispensaries and 111 positions (26.12%) in Ayush Dispensaries. Needless to say that the 
vacancies negatively impact the doctor -to-beneficiary ratio, increase patient waiting times, 
and reduce the quality of care. To address this, the Committee recommends that the 
Government should promptly fill up all vacant positions to ensure adequate staffing.  The 
Department should evaluate the required number of doctors in CGHS dispensaries nationwide 
and maintain a sufficient doctor -to-population ratio in all CGHS cities. A minimum doctor -
per-day consultation criterion should be established based on the numbe r of beneficiaries at 

112 
 
each Wellness Centre and the criterion must be strictly enforced. Ensuring adequate staffing is 
essential for the effective functioning of CGHS dispensaries and improving patient care. 
 
(Para 3.1.14 of the report) 
Action Taken 
 
3.5.14 Recruitment is a continuous process. Every year on the basis of vacancies projected by 
the MoHFW, UPSC conducts Combined Medical Services Examination for recruitment of 
Medical Officers of GDMO sub cadre of Central Health Services. Also, an Integrated 
Recruitment system is being implemented under which AD, CGHS Delhi has been designated 
as Nodal Officer for undertaking recruitment process of all Direct Recruitment Group -B 
(Non-Gazetted) and Group-C posts through Staff Selection Commission. 
 
Further Recommendation 
 
3.5.15 The Committee acknowledges the submission of the Department of Health and 
Family Welfare that recruitment for vacant positions in CGHS dispensaries is being 
carried out regularly through the Combined Medical Services Examination and that an  
Integrated Recruitment System has been introduced to streamline the process for Group 
B and C posts. While these steps are commendable, the Committee still remains 
concerned about the persistently high vacancy levels i.e. 2023 positions (28.81% of the 
sanctioned strength) in Allopathic dispensaries and 111 positions in Ayush dispensaries, 
which continue to affect the quality and accessibility of healthcare services for CGHS 
beneficiaries. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Ministry must adopt a 
time-bound recruitment plan with clearly defined annual targets to fill up all existing 
vacancies and formulate strategic plan to avoid accumulation of vacancies in future. 
Further, the Committee advises the Ministry to conduct a comprehensive manpower 
assessment to rationalize staffing patterns across CGHS cities based on beneficiary load 
and service demand. The Committee also recommends exploring the measures such as 
telemedicine support and task-sharing with AYUSH practitioners to bridge critical gaps 
until regular recruitment is completed, thereby ensuring uninterrupted and quality 
healthcare delivery to all CGHS beneficiaries. 
 
3.6 Safdurjung Hospital (SJH) & Vardhman Mahavir Medical College (VMMC) 
 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
3.6.1 The Committee not es that almost 32.6% of posts in Group A, 87.5% in Group B 
Gazetted, 16.5% in Group B Non -Gazetted and 35.3% in Group C are lying vacant in SJH 
and VMMC. Large vacancies in workforce will have an impact on the smooth functioning of 
the Institute. The Committee observes that the significant shortages in staff numbers across 
various groups in SJH and VMMC are impacting the institute‘s optimal functioning. To 
address this, the Committee recommends that the Department take immediate steps to 
expedite recruitment across all vacant positions, ensuring that administrative manpower aligns 

113 
 
with the increasing number of UG and PG seats, as well as teaching faculty requirements. 
Additionally, vacancies in non -faculty positions, particularly in the Academic Section and 
VMMC Administration, must be filled up without delay to support smooth operations. 
(Para 3.2.8 of the report) 
Action Taken 
 
3.6.2 The Creation of 1100 posts for various category of different cadre i.e. Teaching, Non -
Teaching, Nursing, Technical, Paramedical, and administrative Staff is under consideration.  
Recruitment is an ongoing process. The recruitment of 933 posts has been undertaken during 
the last FY 2024 -2025. Both the post of Senior and Junior Residents are tenure -based posts 
for a period  of three & one year respectively and recruitment to fill up the vacancies on 
regular basis are made every year for Senior Residents and twice in a year for Junior 
Residents. Posts are vacant, due to non -availability of candidates and non -joining of selected 
candidates and frequent resignations by resident doctors. 
 
 
Further Recommendation 
 
3.6.3 The Committee notes the Ministry’s action in initiating the creation of 1,100 
posts across various categories and the recruitment of 933 personnel being undertaken  
during 2024 –25 to address manpower shortages in Safdarjung Hospital (SJH) and 
Vardhman Mahavir Medical College (VMMC). While these steps indicate progress, the 
Committee remains concerned that substantial vacancies still persists, particularly in 
both tea ching and non -teaching cadres, which continue to affect the institutes’ 
operational efficiency, academic quality, and patient care. The Committee expresses 
concern that 32.6% of posts in Group A, 87.5% Group B Gazetted, 16.5 %in Group B 
Non-Gazetted and 35 .3% in Group C are lying vacant in the said Institute. The 
Committee, therefore, recommends that the Ministry finalize and implement the 
proposed creation of posts without further delay and establish a time-bound recruitment 
schedule to ensure that all san ctioned positions are filled up within a fixed period. The 
Committee further advises adopting a proactive retention strategy to address the 
recurring issue of resignations and non -joining, including incentives for long -term 
service, improved working condit ions, and streamlined recruitment procedures. The 
Committee, additionally, recommends introducing a centralized digital manpower 
management system to monitor vacancies, recruitments, and attrition trends in real time 
to ensure that faculty, technical, and administrative staffing levels remain adequate and 
responsive to the growing academic and healthcare workload at SJH and VMMC.

Further Recommendation 
 
3.7.4 The Committee notes the Ministry’s efforts to address space constraints throu gh 
the allotment of 8.871 acres of land near Birla Mandir for the expansion of healthcare 
infrastructure and the proactive steps taken to request additional vacant government 
land for staff residences, hostels, and support facilities. While these measures are 
encouraging, the Committee remains seriously concerned about the persistent delays in 
the completion of the Super Specialty Block (SSB) and other infrastructure projects, 
which have now faced multiple deadline extensions. The Committee, therefore, stro ngly 
recommends that the Ministry institute a strict project monitoring and accountability 
mechanism with clearly defined milestones, quarterly progress reviews, and real -time 
reporting to ensure adherence to the revised timelines. Further, the Committee a dvises 
that the Department must strengthen coordination with implementing agencies such as 
HITES, ensure timely release and utilization of funds, and impose performance -linked 
accountability measures on contractors and executing agencies responsible for de lays. 
The Committee also recommends that the Ministry prepare a comprehensive master 
plan for long -term campus development to optimize the use of newly allotted and 
proposed land, thereby addressing both current space shortages and future institutional 
expansion needs. 
 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
3.7.5 The Committee notes with concern that nearly 23% of posts across various cadres 
remain vacant. Needless to say that such significant workforce shortage hinders the smooth 
functioning of the Institute. The Committee, specifically, observes that staffing deficiencies in 
Dr. RML Hospital and ABVIMS are affecting their operational efficiency. To address the 
issue, the Committee recommends the Department to take immediate measures to expedite the 
recruitment pr ocess for filling up of all vacant positions. It is essential to ensure that the 
administrative and teaching faculty strength keeps pace with the increasing number of 
undergraduate and postgraduate seats. 
(Para 3.3.9 of the report) 
Action Taken 
 
3.7.6 The hospital has already started the process to fill up these vacant posts. 
 
Further Recommendation 
 
3.7.7 The Committee notes the Ministry’s submission that the process to fill up the 
vacant posts in Dr. RML Hospital and ABVIMS has been initiated but without any 
details of steps taken by the Department  in that direction. However, given that nearly 
one-fourth of the sanctioned posts across various cadres remain unfilled, the Committee 
emphasizes that mere initiation of the recruitment process is insufficient without defined 
timelines and measurable progress. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the 
Department must prepare and adhere to a time -bound recruitment plan with clear 

117 
 
quarterly targets for filling up vacancies in both teaching and non -teaching positions. 
Regular monitoring of recruitment progress should be undertaken at the highest 
administrative le vel, and periodic status reports may be submitted to the Committee 
until all vacancies are filled up. 
 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
3.7.8 Furthermore, the Committee notes that premier hospitals like Dr. RML Hospital, 
particularly in metropolitan cities, are struggling with high patient inflow in OPD, facing 
challenges in managing large patient volumes leaves long waiting periods for surgeries, and 
delays in diagnostic tests. The Committee recommends that these critical issues be prioritized 
in policy-making to ensure accessible, affordable, and high-quality healthcare for the public. 
(Para 3.3.11 of the report) 
Action Taken 
 
3.7.9 ABVIMS &Dr. Ram Manohar Lohia Hospital has not been given any fixed population 
coverage.  Patients from all over Delhi and outs ide Delhi come to this hospital for treatment.  
No patient is deprived of treatment due to overcrowding/rush.  The average daily OPD 
attendance of patients in this hospital is about 7000. Dr. RML Hospital, New Delhi provides 
services to about 18 lakhs pati ents as OPD cases, admits about 67000 patients in Indoor and 
about 2.75  lakhs patients are attended in the Emergency. About 10000 yellow fever 
vaccination and 915 delivery cases. Similarly, about 5,000 CT scan, 1.70 lakhs X -Ray cases, 
28 lakhs laboratory t ests and about 17,000 Ultrasound are done. Hospital conducts about 
11,000 Major and 49,000 Minor operations during a year.  Special clinics are conducted in 
different department to give more attention to patients suffering from specific disease which 
are chronic in nature so that they can be investigated properly and their follow-up can be done 
on a regular basis and proper counselling can be given to the patient and their families in 
these clinics.  All these services are not possible in the regular OPD Cli nics, where there is 
heavy crowd of patients suffering from all kinds of illness. 
 
Further Recommendation 
 
3.7.10 The Committee takes note of the data furnished by the Department regarding the 
large patient inflow and extensive range of services being provided by Dr. RML Hospital 
and ABVIMS. While the Committee appreciates the hospital’s commitment to ensuring 
that no patient is denied treatment despite overcrowding, it remains concerned about 
the sustainability of system due to heavy patients load and the potential strain on 
infrastructure and human resources. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the 
Department should undertake a comprehensive workload assessment of the hospital to 
identify gaps in manpower, infrastructure, and equipment. It further re commends the 
introduction of a structured patient load management system, including strengthening 
referral linkages with other Central and State hospitals, expansion of satellite OPD 
centres, and enhancement of digital and telemedicine services to reduce u nnecessary 
congestion. The Committee also urges the Department to consider allocating additional 

118 
 
resources and budgetary support to augment diagnostic, surgical, and emergency 
capacities to ensure timely, quality, and equitable healthcare delivery. 
 
 
3.8 Lady Hardinge Medical College (LHMC) & Smt. S. K. Hospital

3.8.1 The Committee observes that there is significant manpower crunch across various 
groups in the LHMC and SKH institutions impacting the institute‘s optimal functio ning. The

staff. Some of the critical cadre like Neurology and Cardiology have zero in-position strength. 
The Committee takes a serious view of this manpower cr unch and recommends the

(Para 3.4.4 of the report)

Welfare that efforts are being made to fill up vacant posts on a regular basis in the

sanctioned posts are still vacant which indicates that about 36% of total sanctioned posts 
are vacant. The vacancy is due to various reasons in cluding court cases, revision of 
recruitment rules, etc. The Committee expresses concern that mere ongoing efforts, 
without clear timelines or accountability mechanisms, may not adequately address the 
acute manpower shortage that is severely affecting the functioning of LHMC and SKH.

bound action plan for filling up all vacant positions, with particular priority given to 
critical specialties such as Neurology and Cardiology. It further urges the Department to 
explore measures such as incentivized recruitment drives to bridge immediate gaps. 
Additionally, the Committee recommends instituting a regular monitoring mechanism

cadres remain optimally staffed to maintain the quality and continuity of patient care 
and academic activities.
