3.10 Pradhan Mantri Swasthaya Suraksha Yojana (PMSSY) 
 
Recommendations/Observations 
3.10.1 The Committee notes the provisons made by the Department for augmenting faculty 
and non -faculty strength in the newly set -up AIIMS viz. Standing Selection Committees, 
Centralised Nursing Officer Recruitment Common Eligiblity Test (NORCET), CRE, rolling 
advertisement for recruitment, INI -CET, INI-SS and the Committee hopes that measures so 
undertaken would yield outcome in a time -frame manner. The Committee urges upon the 
Ministry to implement comprehensive st rategies for the recruitment and retention of faculty 
and staff in new AIIMS, particularly in remote locations. The Department should offer 
attractive compensation packages and incentives to attract and retain qualified personnel; 
developing robust mentoring programs; addressing the challenges of creating adequate living 
conditions and infrastructure in remote areas. The Committee is of the considered view that 
shortages of faculty and staff, especially in remote AIIMS, certainly impede the delivery of 
quality healthcare and medical education, therefore, addressing these human resource 
challenges is crucial for imbibing professional pursuits in new AIIMS for the successful 
functioning of these institutions. 
(Para 3.6.17 of the report) 
Action Taken 
 
3.10.2 The Ministry agrees with the views of the Committee that adequate faculty and staff 
are crucial to deliver quality healthcare services and medical education, by new AIIMS.  
Creation of posts and recruitment (filling up of the posts) in AIIMS is, however, a continuous 
process. An AIIMS like institute typically takes 6 -8 years to become fully functional with all 
Departments. The Ministry is making all out efforts for ensuring creation of posts and timely 
recruitment of faculty and non -faculty in all AIIMS. Rec ently with the approval of 
Department of Expenditure 3,668 posts at various levels have been created in 10 AIIMS.  The 
Ministry has taken efforts to make the recruitment process easier by adopting rolling 
advertisement, wherein the advertised vacancy remain s available to applicants, for a long 
period of time. Visiting faculty scheme has been formulated to allow faculty from outside the 
Institute to work in newly established AIIMS, for teaching and academic purposes.  Further, 
the Ministry has approved the pol icy of engagement of retired faculty at the level of 
Professor, Additional Professor and Associate Professor, on contract basis, in new AIIMS, up 
to the age of 70 years, from Institutes of National Importance (INIs) and government Medical   
Colleges (GMCs). 
3.10.3 In addition, the following measures initiated by the Ministry/Institutes facilitates 
regular filling up of vacant posts: - 

122 
 
(i) Conduct of Centralized Nursing Officer Recruitment Common Eligibility Test 
(NORCET) by AIIMS Delhi for recruitment of Nursing Offices twice a year for all 
AIIMS including Central Government Hospitals.  
(ii) Conduct of Common Recruitment Examination (CRE) by AII MS Delhi for recruitment 
of Group ‗B‘ and ‗C‘ non-faculty for AIIMS including Central Government Hospitals. 
(iii) Conduct of Institute of National Importance Combined Entrance Test (INI -CET) and 
Institute of national Importance Super Specialty (INI -SS) exam, twi ce a year to select 
Junior Residents (Academic) and Senior Residents (Academic) for various AIIMS. 
3.10.4 Institutes, through review meetings and advisories from time to time, are being 
directed to fill the vacant posts in a time bound manner. Further, nee d-based engagement of 
outsourced and contractual staff is also allowed with the approval of Standing Finance 
Committee of the Institute after assessing requirement.  The Ministry has been considering 
proposals from AIIMS for expanding residential facilities  to accommodate more faculty/staff 
on the hospital campuses.  Faculty pay packages at AIIMS are generally considered higher 
than those at other State Government Medical Colleges. 
Further Recommendation 
 
3.10.5 The Committee notes with appreciation the steps  taken by the Department of 
Health and Family Welfare to address faculty and staff shortages in the newly 
established AIIMS, including the creation of 3,668 new posts at various levels in 10 
AIIMS, adoption of rolling advertisements, implementation of the Visiting Faculty 
Scheme, engagement of retired faculty, and centralized recruitment mechanisms such as 
NORCET and CRE. However, the Committee remains concerned that despite these 
initiatives, persistent vacancies and delays in recruitment continue to hinde r the timely 
operationalization of departments, particularly in remote and newly functional AIIMS. 
The Committee, therefore, recommends the Department to devise a structured time -
bound roadmap for achieving full faculty and non -faculty strength across all AIIMS 
within a specified period. Further, the Committee reiterates the need to institutionalize 
targeted retention strategies, including hardship allowances, housing and schooling 
facilities, academic incentives, and professional development opportunities for faculty 
and staff posted in remote locations. The Committee also recommends the Department 
to conduct quarterly reviews to monitor recruitment progress and ensure that new 
AIIMS achieve functional parity with established institutions at the earliest. I n this 
regard, detailed action taken notes along with outcome thereof may be furnished to the 
Committee. 
 
 
Recommendations/Observations 
3.10.6 The Department presented the status of the PMSSY which aims to upgrade 
Government Medical Colleges/Institutions ( GMCIs). Out of 75 approved GMCIs, 70 have 
been completed. However, 5 remain either partially completed or have not started at all. 
Specifically, PMCH Patna (91% complete), GMC Surat (73.5% complete), and STCIMST 
Kerala (99.9% complete, scheduled for inaugu ration) are in advanced stages. However, 

123 
 
UCMS-GTB Hospital in Delhi and SCBMCH Cuttack, Odisha have not commenced up -
gradation due to funding issues and location changes, respectively. The Committee strongly 
recommends that the Department of Health and Fam ily Welfare, in collaboration with the 
respective state/UT governments, establish a strict, time -bound action plan with clear 
milestones and regular monitoring to expedite the completion of the remaining five GMCIs 
under the PMSSY. 
(Para 3.6.19 of the report)

3.11 National AIDS and STD Control Programme (NACP) 
 
Recommendations/Observations 
3.11.1 While the National AIDS and STD Control Programme (NACP) program has 
allocated funds to the tune of Rs. 2858 crore in RE 2024 -25, the actual expenditure in 2024 -
25 up to 4th February, 2025 is Rs. 2256.47 crore, just 79% of the revised estimate (RE). The 
reduction is especially remarkable for "Su pplies & Materials", indicating potential 
inefficiencies or delays in procurement and distribution. The Committee, therefore, 
recommends conducting a detailed analysis of the "Supplies & Materials" expenditure to 
identify and address bottlenecks in procure ment and distribution. The Committee, in nutshell, 
recommends the Department for optimal utilization of funds under NACP. 
(Para 3.7.3 of the report) 
Action Taken 
 
3.11.2 The recommendation of the Committee has been noted for implementation. The 
reduction of expenditure in ―Supply & Materials‖ in 2024 -25 is attributable to change -in 
procurement cycle by CMSS which caused delay in payment schedule. Further, the reason for 
less expenditure in ―Supply & Materials‖ in 2024-25 was due to stiff competition in tendering 
by CMSS, the prices of drugs, kits and condom was discovered lower than previous years. 
However, there is no shortage of drugs in NACP programme. However, a detaile d analysis on 
the ―Supply & Materials‖ expenditure will be conducted to get rid of bottlenecks in 
procurement and distribution.   
Further Recommendation 
 
3.11.3 The Committee takes note of the clarification tendered by the Department of 
Health and Family W elfare regarding reduction of expenditure under ―Supplies & 
Materials‖ in 2024 -25 due to changes in the procurement cycle and competitive price 
discovery through CMSS. The Committee is satisfied and appreciates the assurance 
given by the Department that th ere has been no shortage of essential drugs under 

125 
 
NACP. However, the Committee remains concerned that delays in the procurement 
cycle and payment schedules might have potentially affected timely availability and 
distribution therefore should be taken care of in the future. The Committee, therefore, 
recommends that the Department should institutionalize a robust procurement 
monitoring mechanism to ensure timely tendering, payment, and supply chain efficiency 
under the NACP. The Committee further urges the De partment to expedite the 
proposed detailed analysis of ―Supplies & Materials‖ expenditure and submit a time -
bound action plan outlining measures taken to eliminate bottlenecks. The Committee 
also suggests adopting digital tracking tools for end -to-end monitoring of procurement 
and distribution and strengthening coordination between CMSS and NACO to ensure 
optimal and timely utilization of funds under the Programme. 
 
3.12 Establishment and Strengthening of NCDC Branches and Health Initiatives,

Other Neglected Tropical Diseases, Surveillance of Viral Hepatitis, Anti Microbial 
Resistance 
  
Recommendations/Observations 
3.12.1 The Committee notes that for construction of NCDC branch es across the country, the 
land has not yet been identified in the State of Chhattisgarh. Further, the construction has 
been started in eight States only. The Committee recommends that the NCDC prioritize and 
expedite the establishment of fully functional branch infrastructure across all states, 
particularly focusing on Chhattisgarh where land identification is still pending and 
accelerating construction in the remaining states where it has not yet commenced. This is 
crucial for maintaining the continuity a nd effectiveness of the NCDC's national disease 
surveillance and response capabilities, ensuring timely implementation of the Integrated 
Disease Surveillance Programme (IDSP) and the Integrated Health Information Platform 
(IHIP), and strengthening the national public health framework. 
(Para 3.10.8 of the report) 
Action Taken 
 
3.12.2 The recommendations of the committee are noted. Efforts are being made to expedite 
the setting up of NCDC branches. 
Further Recommendation 
 
3.12.3 The Committee takes into account assurance committed by the Department that 
efforts are being made to expedite the establishment of NCDC branches and 
infrastructure across all States; however, it expresses concern over the continued delay 
in land identific ation in the State of Chhattisgarh and the slow pace of construction in 
several States, which may adversely affect the NCDC’s ability to strengthen disease 
surveillance and rapid response mechanisms across the country. The Committee, 

126 
 
therefore, reiterates its recommendation for the Department to set up specific timelines 
and measurable milestones for land acquisition, approval, and construction of the 
pending NCDC branches, particularly in Chhattisgarh. The Committee further 
recommends that a dedicated moni toring cell be established within the Department to 
regularly review progress, address inter -departmental coordination issues, and ensure 
periodic reporting on the status of each branch. The Committee suggests the 
Department for exploring interim operation al setups or rented facilities in States where 
land identification is delayed, so that surveillance, laboratory, and outbreak response 
activities are not hindered. 
 
 
3.13 National Pharmacovigilance Programme 
Recommendations/Observations 
3.13.1 The Committee has been apprised of the indigenously developed Adverse Drug 
Reaction Monitoring Software (ADRMS) of Pharmacovigilance Program of India (PvPI) 
launched on 19th August 2024. The Committee recommends a strategic focus on improving 
the functionality  and accessibility of this indigenously developed ADRM software to 
strengthen the efficacy and safety database for new drugs. The key measures in this direction 
should include implementing rigorous data validation and quality control protocols; 
expanding targeted training programs and developing user -friendly interfaces and advanced 
data analytics tools within ADRMS to enable timely signal detection and benefit -risk 
analysis, reinforcing the program‘s leadership in international drug monitoring and 
safeguarding public health. The whole purpose is to create effective nation -wide system for 
patient safety reporting in respect to adverse drug reactions (ADRs) of medical products. 
(Para 3.11.4 of the report) 
Action Taken 
 
3.13.2 The recommendations of the committee have been noted. The indigenously developed 
Adverse Drug Reaction Monitoring Software (ADRMS) has been launched on 19th August 
2024 to facilitate reporting of Adverse Events related to medicines and medical devices. This 
software streamlines the reporting process by patients/their caregivers and healthcare 
professionals in India and also empowers Indian Pharmaceutical Industries/Marketing 
Authorizations Holders (MAHs) to report adverse events after giving them access.  The 
software has been develop ed by Centre for Development of Advanced Computing (C -DAC), 
Noida enables data validation and quality control of Individual Case Safety Reports. 
Trainings will be provided for healthcare professional and non -health care professional on 
how to get registered in ADRMS, and submit reports. Data analytics tools for signal detection 
and benefit-risk analysis will be developed/ upgraded, from time to time. 
Further Recommendation 
 
3.13.3 The Committee appreciates the initiative taken by the Department of Health an d 
Family Welfare in launching the indigenously developed Adverse Drug Reaction 

127 
 
Monitoring Software (ADRMS) and takes notes of the planned measures for capacity 
building and system enhancement to facilitate reporting of adverse events related to 
medicines and medical devices, specifically individual case safety reports. However, the 
Committee emphasizes that the success of ADRMS will depend on its wide -scale 
adoption, data quality, and analytical efficiency. The Committee, therefore, recommends 
the Department to establish a clear implementation roadmap with defined timelines for 
nationwide integration of ADRMS across hospitals, medical colleges, and 
pharmaceutical entities. The Committee further urges the Department to ensure 
continuous system upgrades throug h collaboration with C -DAC and other technical 
institutions, develop AI-enabled data analytics for faster signal detection, and strengthen 
interoperability with global pharmacovigilance databases. The Committee hopes that 
regular performance audits and use r feedback mechanisms should also be 
institutionalized to ensure accuracy, responsiveness, and long -term sustainability of the 
platform, thereby enhancing India’s pharmacovigilance capacity and public health 
safety. 
 
3.14 Health Sector Disaster Preparednes s and Response and Human Resource 
Development for Emergency Medical Services  
 
Recommendations/Observations 
3.14.1 The Committee notes the under -utilization of funds under Health Sector Disaster 
Preparedness and Response and Human Resource Development for Emergency Medical 
Services during the past two years. In the FY 2023 -24, Rs. 128.14 crore was allocated at BE 
stage which was reduced to Rs. 70 crore at RE stage. Moreover, the actual expenditure was 
just Rs. 30.50 crore (43.57% of the already reduced revi sed estimate). Similarly, in the next 
FY 2024-25, the amount of Rs. 94 crore allocated at BE stage was reduced to Rs. 15 crore at 
RE stage, and again, the actual expenditure has been reported to the tune of Rs. 7.57 crore as 
on 07.02.2025. The Committee, t herefore, recommends the Government to implement a 
rigorous, time -bound monitoring and evaluation framework for the "Health Sector Disaster 
Preparedness and Response and Human Resource Development for Emergency Medical 
Services" scheme, specifically focusing on the timely execution of infrastructure projects and 
Capacity Building Initiatives that inter -alia include setting up of National Emergency Life 
Support (NELS) skill centers in medical colleges/institutions under Central/State Government 
and setting u p of Chemical, Biological, Radiological and Nuclear (CBRN) medical 
management centers besides imparting skills training of doctors, nurses and paramedics on 
NELS and CBRN, and fund utilization by grantee institutions. 
(Para 3.13.6 of the report) 
 
Action Taken 
3.14.2 The observations/recommendation of the Hon‘ble Committee are duly noted.For the 
rigorous, time -bund monitoring and evaluation of the implementation of scheme, ―Health 
Sector Disaster Preparedness and Response and Human Resource Development for 
Emergency Medical Services‖ regular monitored by Project Monitoring Committees at senior 

128 
 
level are held. Additionally, schemes are also reviewed regularly in meeting with concerned 
States/UTs under senior officials of the Ministry of Health and Family Welfare. 
Further Recommendation 
 
3.14.3 The Committee takes note of the submission of the Department that regular 
monitoring and review meetings are being conducted by senior -level Project Monitoring 
Committees besides regular interactions with States and UTs concerned. However, the 
Committee observes that despite these mechanisms, fund utilization under the ―Health 
Sector Disaster Preparedness and Response and Human Resource Development for 
Emergency Medical Services‖ scheme continues to remain significantly l ow. The 
Committee, therefore, reiterates that the Ministry must strengthen the existing 
monitoring framework by introducing measurable performance indicators, quarterly 
progress reviews, and digital dashboards to track physical and financial achievements in 
real time. The Committee further recommends that specific accountability mechanisms 
be instituted to identify and address delays in project execution and fund disbursement. 
Greater coordination with States and implementing institutions should be ensured 
through dedicated nodal officers and periodic field evaluations to achieve tangible 
progress in establishing NELS and CBRN centers, and to ensure optimal utilization of 
allocated funds in the ensuing financial years.

3.15.1 The Committee notes significant under -utilization of fund under the National Tele

believes in immediate and targeted interventions to resolve bottlenecks in fund utilization to 
ensure the sustained growth and effectiveness of Tele MANAS. Further, the recruitment 
processes for human resou rces at the State and Union Territory levels must be expedited 
through streamlined procedures and proactive central government support. 
(Para 3.15.3 of the report) 
Action Taken 
3.15.2The observations/recommendation of the Hon‘ble Committee are duly noted. 
 
Further Recommendation 
 
3.15.3 The Committee expresses concern that no concrete action plan or timeline has 
been outlined to address the persistent under -utilization of funds under the National 
Tele Mental Health Programme. The Committee is of the conside red view that 
immediate steps must be taken to identify and resolve administrative, procedural, and 
capacity-related constraints impeding fund utilization and effective programme rollout. 
It further recommends the Department to develop a detailed implement ation roadmap 

129 
 
with specific milestones for recruitment, training, and operationalization of human 
resources at the State and UT levels. Regular progress reviews should be conducted, 
supported by a digital monitoring system to track financial and physical p erformance. 
The Committee also emphasizes the need to enhance inter -agency coordination, 
strengthen State-level infrastructure, and ensure that Tele MANAS achieves its intended 
goal of providing accessible and timely mental health services across the country. 
 
 
Recommendations/Observations 
3.15.4 The Committee urges upon the Department for an intensified focus on targeted 
outreach and awareness campaigns to build on the increasing engagement of female callers 
and extend services to vulnerable populations. T hese efforts should leverage the Tele 
MANAS mobile application and diverse media platforms to address specific barriers 
preventing access to services. Furthermore, the nationwide expansion of video consultation 
services, currently available in select state s, should be prioritized to enhance accessibility, 
particularly for individuals in remote and underserved areas. This expansion must be 
supported by comprehensive training and technical assistance to ensure effective 
implementation and utilization of the video consultation platform. 
(Para 3.15.4 of the report) 
Action Taken 
3.15.5 The observations/recommendation of the Hon‘ble Committee are duly noted. 
Further Recommendation 
 
3.15.6 The Committee expresses concern over too brief and evasive response of the 
Department that merely stating that the recommendations have been duly noted without 
specifying any concrete actions or timelines for implementation of Tele MANAS mobile 
application and diverse media platforms. The Committee is of the firm view that the 
objectives of the National Tele Mental Health Programme can only be realized through 
proactive measures to expand outreach, accessibility, and service delivery. The 
Committee, therefore, recommends the Ministry to prepare and implement a 
comprehensive strate gy for targeted outreach and awareness campaigns, particularly 
focusing on vulnerable groups such as women, youth, elderly, and marginalized 
communities, using the Tele MANAS mobile application, social media, and community -
based communication channels. Fur ther, the Committee urges the Department to 
formulate a clear roadmap for the nationwide roll -out of video consultation services 
with defined milestones and to ensure adequate training, capacity building, and 
technical support for healthcare professionals to enhance the quality and reach of tele -
mental health interventions. 
 
 
 

130 
 
3.16 National Digital Health Mission (NDHM)/Ayushman Bharat digital Mission 
(ABDM): 
Recommendations/Observations 
3.16.1 The Committee, on examination of the Demands for Grants (2025-26), observes with 
concern persistent and declining trend of utilisation of funds allocated under the ABDM. In 
the FY 2023-24, in BE an amount of Rs. 341.02 crore was allocated to the scheme which was 
reduced to Rs. 200 crore at RE stage and at the e nd of the FY, the actual expenditure came to 
Rs. 175.36 crore (just 51.42% of BE allocated initially). In FY 2024 -25, the BE allocation 
was reduced to Rs. 200 crore (which is a reduction by 41.35% from the BE 2023 -24), 
however, at RE stage the allocation was increased to Rs. 225 crore, but the actual expenditure 
up to 31.01.2025 comes to Rs. 112.97 crore only (which is just 50.21% of the RE allocation).  
This pattern of under -expenditure, coupled with fluctuating budget allocations, directly 
hampers the sch eme's ability to achieve its targeted objectives within the stipulated 
timeframe, potentially leading to time delays and cost overruns. To rectify this situation, the 
Committee recommends immediate and decisive action to address the root causes of these 
inefficiencies and streamline the fund utilization for successful implementation of Mission. In 
the FY 2025 -26, the scheme has been allocated an enhanced amount of Rs. 340.11 crore at 
BE stage. The Committee, therefore, urges upon the Department to address t he issues with 
strategic planning and project execution to ensure proper utilization of the funds so that 
intended targets are achieved within stipulated time-frame. 
(Para 3.16.5 of the report) 
Action Taken 
3.16.2 In FY 2024 –25, the Budget Estimate (BE) allocation was Rs. 200 crore, which was 
subsequently revised to Rs. 225 crore at the Revised Estimate (RE) stage. The actual 
expenditureincurredforFY24-25isRs.212.58crore, amounting to approximately 94.48% of the 
RE allocation. The observations/recommendation of the Hon‘ble Committee for streamlining 
fund utilization are duly noted.  NHA is taking proactive measures to utilize the fund for 
achieving the project objectives. Wherein, States/UTs are being monitored on fortnightly 
basis for the fund utilization along with follow ups for timely submission of proposals and 
utilization certificates. NHA has also decided to proactively approve and process the release 
of the fund requirements of the State/UTs as per the requirement raised in tranches for GIA. 
Furthermore, Treasury Single Accounts for all the States have been mapped to the Scheme 
which will help NHA in real-time monitoring and utilization of funds. 
 
Further Recommendation 
 
3.16.3 The Committee appreciates the significant improvement in fund utilization under 
the Ayushman Bharat Digital Mission (ABDM) during FY 2024 –25, reflecting better 
financial management and monitoring mechanisms. However, the Committee 
emphasizes that sustained progress requires consistent implementation of strategic, 
process-driven measures to prevent any recurrence of under -utilization or delays in 
project execution. The Committee, therefore, recommends the Department to 
institutionalize the fortnightly monitoring and real -time fund tracking through the 
Treasury Single Account (TSA)  mechanism, ensuring transparency and accountability 

131 
 
at all levels. The Committee further suggests that the National Health Authority (NHA) 
develop performance -linked indicators to assess State/UT progress in digital health 
infrastructure creation and serv ice delivery under ABDM. Additionally, capacity -
building initiatives and technical support for States/UTs should be strengthened to 
ensure timely submission of proposals and utilization certificates. The Committee 
further recommends the periodic publication of utilization and implementation progress 
reports to enable data -driven decision -making and enhance public confidence in the 
Mission’s execution. 
 
 
Recommendations/Observations 
3.16.4 Given the recurring issues of underutilization of funds within the Ay ushman Bharat 
Digital Mission (ABDM), specifically due to delays in state -level Project Monitoring Unit 
(PMU) establishment, inefficient management of advertising contracts, performance issues 
with Managed Service Providers (MSPs), and complications in pro fessional service contracts, 
the Committee recommends a robust fund disbursement and monitoring mechanism should be 
instituted. This should include strict timelines and performance -linked incentives for 
States/UTs to expedite the establishment of Project M onitoring Units (PMUs), along with 
real-time tracking of fund allocation and utilization. Furthermore, ensuring timely on -
boarding of competent service providers and enforcing clear contractual obligations with 
penalties for non-performance will help strea mline expenditure under Digital Equipment and 
Professional Services. Strengthening coordination between NHA, States/UTs, and 
implementing agencies will be critical for optimizing fund flow and ensuring effective 
implementation of ABDM objectives. 
(Para 3.16.7 of the report) 
Action Taken 
 
3.16.5 The observations/recommendation of the Hon‘ble Commit tee are duly noted. NHA is 
monitoring timely release and utilisation of funds through regular meeting with 
States/Uts.NHA has already on boarded two PMUs and NI SG for providing 
professionalservicesandhaveclearcontractfortimelydisbursementoffund including the 
provision of penalty in the contract. The same is being followed for Digital Equipment. 
Further Recommendation 
 
3.16.6 The Committee acknowledges the steps taken by the National Health Authority 
(NHA) to onboard Project Monitoring Units (PMUs) and the National Institute for 
Smart Government (NISG) for professional services, as well as the inclusion of penalty 
provisions in contracts to ensure timely fund disbursement and accountability. However, 
the Committee emphasizes that these measures must translate into measurable 
improvements in fund utilization and project execution efficiency across all States and 
Union Territories . The Committee, therefore, recommends that the NHA should 
institutionalize a standardized performance monitoring framework with clearly defined 

132 
 
key performance indicators (KPIs) for PMUs and service providers, linking fund 
disbursement to demonstrable progress. The Committee also urges the NHA to enhance 
inter-agency coordination through a centralized digital dashboard for real -time 
monitoring of fund flow, contract compliance, and implementation milestones. Further, 
periodic performance audits and third-party evaluations should be conducted to identify 
systemic bottlenecks and ensure that the objectives of the ABDM are achieved 
effectively and within stipulated timelines. 
 
3.17 Statutory and Regulatory Bodies 
Recommendations/Observations 
3.17.1 The Committ ee notes that the reduction in budgetary allocation for the Food Safety 
and Standards Authority of India (FSSAI) in the RE 2024 -25 was primarily due to delayed 
submission of Utilization Certificates (UCs) from States/UTs. This delay hinders the timely 
disbursement of funds for strengthening the food safety ecosystem. The committee, therefore, 
recommends the Department for implementing a streamlined UC submission and tracking 
system. To ensure consistent and timely fund utilization, the FSSAI should establis h a 
mandatory, digitally enabled system for States/UTs to submit and track their UCs. This 
system should include automated reminders and alerts for pending submissions, and provide 
real-time visibility of UC status to both FSSAI and the respective States/U Ts. Furthermore, 
the FSSAI should conduct periodic workshops and training sessions for State/UT officials to 
improve their understanding of UC requirements and submission processes, thereby 
preventing future delays and ensuring efficient fund utilization. This proactive approach will 
facilitate smoother fund flow and enable FSSAI to effectively carry out its mandate of 
ensuring food safety across the country. 
(Para 3.17.6 of the report) 
Action Taken 
 
3.17.2 FSSAI currently captures data related to various activities from States/UTs through 
two portals – the Quarterly Portal ( https://sites.fssai.gov.in/stateportal/home.php) and the 
annual Portal ( https://sites.fssai.gov.in/annualreport/home.php). The provision for Digital 
tracking system for funds released to States/UTs would be strengthened by implementing 
necessary modifications in the two portals.The present tracking system of receiv ing UC from 
State/UTs is being digitized.Issues related to pending utilization certificates (UCs) with 
State/UTs are regularly reviewed at length with the State Governments in every Central 
Advisory Committee (CAC) meeting attended by all the State Food Sa fety Commissioners 
quarterly.Training sessions for States/ UTs officials to improve understanding of UC 
requirement is an ongoing process. 
Further Recommendation 
 
3.17.3 The Committee commends the initiatives taken by FSSAI to digitize the tracking 
of Util ization Certificates (UCs) and to strengthen its existing quarterly and annual 

133 
 
reporting portals. However, it emphasizes that the ongoing efforts must be translated 
into a fully integrated, user -friendly, and real -time digital monitoring system to ensure 
accountability and transparency in fund utilization. The Committee, therefore, 
recommends that FSSAI should expedite the completion of the digital UC tracking 
system with features such as automated alerts, dashboards for real -time fund flow 
visualization, and analytics to identify delays or bottlenecks. The Committee also urges 
FSSAI to issue guidelines directing clear timelines for States/UTs to submit UCs and to 
link future fund releases to strict adherence with these timelines, terms and conditions. 
Further, the Committee recommends periodic capacity -building workshops and 
dedicated helpdesk support for State/UT officials to enhance their technical proficiency 
in digital submissions and compliance with financial reporting requirements. These 
measures will help institutionalize efficiency, ensure timely fund utilization, and 
strengthen the overall food safety governance framework. 
 
 
Recommendations/Observations 
3.17.4 FSSAI faces a significant number of vacant positions, which could impact its 
operational efficiency. The Committee believes that to improve the operational efficiency of 
FSSAI and enhance the consistency of food safety regulation across the country, FSSAI 
should be directed to prioritize and expedite the direct recruitment process to fill up the  
significant number of vacant positions. Additionally, FSSAI should implement strategies to 
improve employee retention and recruitment on regular basis must be adhered to instead of 
hiring staff on contract basis as contractual staff lacks sense of organiz ational belongingness 
and commitment to work and there remain sense of being on ad -hoc at work that too without 
responsibility and accountability. The Committee, therefore, believes that efforts be made to 
grant regularization of contractual staff in FSSAI. 
(Para 3.17.14 of the report) 
Action Taken 
 
3.17.5 Food Safety and Standards Authority of India (FSSAI) published its Recruitment and 
Appointment Regulations on 1st October 2018. The total sanctioned posts in 2018 were 824. 
The details of FSSAI‘s Sanctioned Posts (Grade wise) and Vacant Posts are given below: -

3.17.6 Total vacant posts as on 09.04.2025 are 241, out of which 158 posts are to be filled 
through Direct Recruitment and remaining are promotional posts. Recruitment to the vacant 
position is in the process. Besides, 824 sanctioned strengths; there are 73 contractual  
employees as on 09.04.2025; who are continuing in the service based on stay order on Writ 
Petition pending in the Delhi High Court. 
Further Recommendation 
 
3.17.7 The Committee takes into account  the steps taken by FSSAI to initiate 
recruitment against vacant posts and notes the existence of 241 vacancies (about 30% of 
total sanctioned posts), including 158 posts to be filled through direct recruitment. The 
Committee expresses concern over the persisting high vacancy levels, which must have 
been adversel y affecting the Authority’s capacity to effectively implement food safety 
regulations and monitoring activities nationwide. The Committee, therefore, strongly 
recommends that FSSAI must set a clear and time -bound roadmap for completing the 
recruitment proc ess, particularly for direct recruitment posts, and ensure that 
promotional vacancies are filled up expeditiously through timely convening of DPCs. 
Further, the Committee emphasizes that FSSAI should develop a long -term human 
resource management plan focus ing on employee retention, career progression, and 
motivation to minimize attrition. While the issue of contractual employees is sub judice, 
the Committee advises the Authority to explore feasible administrative measures to 
safeguard the job security and w elfare of contractual staff within the existing legal 
framework. Strengthening institutional manpower on a regular basis will be critical to 
enhancing FSSAI’s operational efficiency and ensuring consistent enforcement of food 
safety standards across the country. 
 
 
3.18 All India Institute of Medical Sciences (AIIMS), New Delhi 
Recommendations/Observations 
3.18.1 The Committee recommends that AIIMS, New Delhi, establish a robust project 
monitoring mechanism with clearly defined milestones and regular progres s reviews. This 
mechanism should include a dedicated team to proactively address regulatory hurdles, such 

135 
 
as environmental clearances and tree-cutting approvals, by engaging with relevant authorities 
at the earliest stages of project planning. Furthermore, AIIMS should streamline its tendering 
process to minimize/eliminate delays, potentially through pre -qualifying vendors and 
implementing a more efficient evaluation system to ensure that projects like the 150 bedded 
Critical Care block, and the super speci alty OPD block are not delayed due to avoidable 
administrative issues. 
(Para 3.18.5 of the report) 
Action Taken 
 
3.18.2 The recommendation of the Committee, for robust project monitoring system has been 
noted. The Institute is committed to strengthening its project monitoring framework by 
introducing clearly defined milestones, conducting regular progress reviews for ongoing 
construction projects and working to ensure the timely identification and resolution of issues.  
While there are challenges posed by regulatory hurdles, the Institute is proactively 
approaching the concerned authorities to ensure that all regulatory and statutory requirements 
are met promptly. AIIMS is committed to optimizing its tendering process to reduce or 
eliminate delays. AIIMS, New Delhi has consistently engaged in reputed Project Management 
and Execution Agencies (PMCs) such as NBCC, HSCC and CPWD for the effective 
management and execution of projects. These agencies bring valuable expertise in overseeing 
large-scale projects. Ho wever, it is important to note that delays have been caused by 
statutory approvals and legal challenges such as the need of tree -cutting approvals which is 
sub judice and the necessary direction by way of Court order is essential for further action in 
the matter. Despite these hurdles, AIIMS, New Delhi remains committed to overcoming such 
challenges through proactive collaboration with the relevant authorities. 
Further Recommendation 
 
3.18.3 The Committee takes note of the steps initiated by AIIMS, New Delh i to 
strengthen its project monitoring framework and acknowledges the engagement of 
reputed project management agencies such as NBCC, HSCC, and CPWD for effective 
execution. The Committee, nevertheless, remains concerned about the persistent delays 
caused by statutory and legal hurdles, including pending approvals related to 
environmental and tree-cutting clearances. Subject to the decision/direction of the High 
Court, Delhi, the Committee views that the Department may institutionalize a high -level 
coordination mechanism with the Ministries and regulatory bodies concerned to 
expedite removal of other hurdles. Further, AIIMS should establish a digital project 
tracking dashboard with milestone -based progress indicators to ensure real -time 
monitoring and transp arency in project implementation. The Committee also advises 
that pre-qualification of vendors and time -bound tender evaluation processes be strictly 
enforced to minimize administrative delays. Strengthening accountability mechanisms 
and periodic review by  a dedicated oversight monitoring system will be crucial to 
ensuring timely completion of critical infrastructure projects such as the 150 -bedded 
Critical Care Block and the Super Specialty OPD Block.

Ayur Vigyan Nagar, however, the same was not in consonance with the Cabinet approval.

the continued delays arising from statutory approvals. The Committee, in this

leads to ad -hoc sense at work. This drive should focus on filling up critical roles to alleviate

3.20 Jawaharlal Institute of post Graduation Medical Education and Research 
(JIPMER), Puducherry 
 
Recommendations/Observations 
 
3.20.1 The Committee wishes to draw attention that JIPMER, Puducherry experiences 
significant reductions in allocated funds at the Revised Estimate (RE) stage, impacting 
project timelines. The key infrastructure pro jects, including the Karaikal Hospital, Trauma 
Center, and Faculty Quarters, face delays due to technical and administrative issues. The 
Committee recommends that the Department, in collaboration with JIPMER and the CPWD, 
establish a streamlined and transp arent mechanism for budget allocation and project 
execution. The Committee is of the firm opinion that there is requirement of rigorous pre -
planning of infrastructure projects to minimize technical and administrative delays; a 
contingency fund to mitigate the impact of unforeseen delays on allocated budgets; and 
regular inter -departmental meetings to ensure timely tender processing and administrative 
approvals. Furthermore, the Committee strongly suggests that the Department, when 
finalizing the revised bud get estimates for the FY 2025 -26, take into account the project 
timelines, and ensure that the critical projects like the JIPMER Karaikal Hospital and the 
Critical Care Hospital Block proceed as scheduled, and do not get delayed due to lack of 
funding. 
(Para 3.20.5 of the report) 
Action Taken 
 
3.20.2 The construction of the Critical Care Hospital Block (CCHB) experienced a delay of 
approximately six months due to the relocation of essential service lines, including the 
medical oxygen plant. These works have  since been completed, and the project is now 
progressing as per the revised schedule. 
Regarding the JIPMER Karaikal Hospital, the initial tender process was cancelled after bidder 
had been blacklisted by the Government of Tamil Nadu. The re -tendering process has been 
done and the contract was awarded in December 2024. At present, construction activities are 
proceeding on schedule and are being closely monitored by the Dean (Karaikal), Project 
Coordinator, and the Director of JIPMER. There are no delays att ributable to funding 
constraints. 
Further Recommendation 
 
3.20.3 The Committee notes with satisfaction that the construction of the Critical Care 
Hospital Block and the JIPMER Karaikal Hospital has resumed its construction work 
and is progressing as per the revised schedule following earlier delays caused by 
technical issues and tendering challenges. However, the Committee emphasizes that the 
recurrence of such issues highlights the continuing need for a more structured and 
anticipatory project management framework. The Committee, therefore, reiterates its 
earlier recomm endation that JIPMER, in coordination with the Department and 
CPWD, institutionalize a comprehensive project planning and monitoring mechanism 
that includes risk assessment, timeline tracking, and financial utilization reviews on a 

142 
 
quarterly basis. The Com mittee further recommends that a contingency and risk 
mitigation plan be integrated into all major infrastructure projects to prevent 
disruptions due to administrative or technical contingencies. The Committee also 
advises the Department to ensure sustaine d financial flow and timely release of funds 
aligned with physical progress milestones to avoid delays in project implementation at 
future stages. 
 
 
Recommendations/Observations 
3.20.4 The Committee notes the land acquisition issues and specifically, the r evocation of 
land designated for a trauma and organ transplant center poses a major obstacle to expansion 
of the Institute. Concerning the revoked land, the Committee urges the Department to 
intensify efforts in collaboration with the Puducherry Government  to reclaim the designated 
50 acres for JIPMER. The committee suggests that a high level meeting between the JIPMER 
administration and the Puducherry Government may be facilitated, by the Department, in 
order to resolve the land issue as quickly as possibl e. These actions will enable JIPMER to 
enhance its patient care capacity and expand its services. 
(Para 3.20.7 of the report) 
Action Taken 
 
3.20.5 The Suggestions of the Hon‘ble Committee are duly noted. 
Further Recommendation 
 
3.20.6 The Committee is of t he view that the Department of Health and Family Welfare 
has not indicated any concrete steps taken to resolve the long -pending issue of land 
acquisition for the proposed trauma and organ transplant center at JIPMER, 
Puducherry. The Committee understands t hat the matter warrants urgent and result -
oriented intervention at the highest level, as the unavailability of the designated land 
continues to hinder the Institute’s expansion and the augmentation of its critical care 
services. The Committee, therefore, r ecommends that the Department convene a high -
level meeting with the Government of Puducherry within a stipulated timeframe to 
expedite the resolution of the land issue. The Committee, in this regard, desires that the 
Department submit a detailed status rep ort indicating the timeline, actions taken, and 
progress made in securing the 50 acres of land for the Institute’s expansion. 
 
3.21 National Institute of Mental Health and Neuro Sciences (NIMHANS), Bengaluru

staffing challenges. The Committee urges the Department to develop and execute a strategic 
recruitment and retention plan to address the significant number of vacant positions, 
particularly among professors, assistant professors, and other key personnel in the institute. 
This plan should include targeted recruitment campaigns on regular basis and not on 
contractual basis, competitive compensation packages, and professional development 
opportunities to attract and retain qualified staff . Addressing these staffing gaps is essential to 
ensure the institute can maintain its high standards of service delivery, research output, and 
training programs, and continue to serve the mental health needs of the nation effectively.

international conferences, opportunities for advanced training, and access to cutting-edge 
research facilities. NIMHANS offers intramural grants to young faculty. 
 Mentorship programs and career progression pathways will be introduced to nurture 
junior faculty and staff, ensuring long-term retention and institutional loyalty. 
Addressing the staffing gaps is a top priority. By strengthening the human resource 
framework, NIMHANS aims to safeguard and enhance the institute‘s capacity to deliver

Further Recommendation

however, the Committee observes that the response lacks clarity on concrete timelines

key positions. While professional growth opportunities are commendable, the 
Committee emphasizes that a structured and time -bound recruitment strategy remains 
crucial to fill up the vacant posts of faculty and other essential personnel on a regular 
and permanent basis rather than on a contractual basis that gives birth to ad -hocism in 
the system. The Committee, therefore, is of the considered view that the Department, in 
coordination with NIMHANS, should finalize and implement a comprehensive 
recruitment and retention plan with defined milestones, periodic monitoring, and 

144 
 
outcome-based evalu ation to ensure timely filling up of vacancies and long -term 
workforce stability.

National Health Mission 
3.25 Budgetary Allocation 
  
Recommendations/Observations 
3.25.1 The Committee notes that the National Health Mission (NHM) aims to achieve 
universal access to equitable, affordable, and quality healthcare, aligning with Sustainable 
Development Goal 3. The NHM has two sub-missions, NRHM and NUHM, and its objectives 
include reducing child and maternal mortality, controlling diseases, ensuring access to 
primary healthcare, and promoting healthy lifestyles. The program has shown progress in 
several areas, such as achieving and sustaining Total Fertility Rate (TFR) at 2.0 and 
exceeding targets for Ayushman Arogya Mandirs and full immunization coverage. However, 
challenges remain in reducing Maternal Mortality Rate (MMR) and Infant Mortality Rate 
(IMR) to target levels, and achieving 90% of districts meeting TB case notification and 
treatment success targets. The Committee expresses concern on the fact that the MMR and 
IMR are still above the targeted levels and, therefore, recommends the Department for a 
targeted and data-driven approach with focused intervention for maternal and infant mortality 
reduction. This should involve detailed micro -level analysis of the regions with the highest 
MMR and IMR, focusing on the specific factors contributing to these rates and chalking out 
sustained & resilient strategy to combat those factors so as to achieve TB elimination, MMR 
and IMR to target level within specified period. 
(Para 4.2.4 of the report) 
Action Taken 
 
3.25.2 The comments of the Parliamentary Standing Committee have been duly noted. 
As per the Special Bulletin on Maternal Mortality released by the Registrar General of India 
(RGI), the current Maternal Mortality Ratio (MMR) of the country is 88 per lakh live births. 
India has seen a significant decline of 42 points in MMR from 130 in 2 014-16 to 88 in 2020 -
22. India has already achieved the National Health Policy (NHP) target of MMR less than 

150 
 
100/lakh live births by 2020 and is on track of achieving the SDG targets of MMR less than 
70 lakh live births by 2030.  
MMR trends in the country as per Sample Registration System (SRS): - 
 
Furthermore, as per the current United Nation Maternal Mortality Estimation Inter -Agency 
Group (UN -MMEIG) Report 2000 -2023, Maternal Mortality Ratio (MMR) of India has 
reduced by 23 points; from 103 per lakh liv e births in 2020 to 80 per lakh live births in 2023. 
Concerted efforts are being directed towards ensuring a continuum of care for maternal and 
child health-related issues and India remains on course to achieve SDG target 3.1, which aims 
to reduce the maternal mortality ratio to less than 70 per 100,000 live births.  
Total 8 States have achieved the Sustainable Development Goal (SDG) target of MMR less 
than 70 per lakh live births viz. Kerala (18), Maharashtra (36), Tamil Nadu (38),Andhra 
Pradesh (47), Tela ngana (50), Jharkhand (50), Gujarat (55), Karnataka (58). In addition to 
these 8 States, substaintial reduction in MMR is also observed for other States like Rajasthan 
(87), Haryana (89), Bihar (91), Punjab (92), Assam (125) as per SRS 2020-22. 
As per the latest Sample Registration System (SRS) Bulletin released by the Registrar 
General of India, the Infant Mortality Rate (IMR) in India stands at 26 per 1,000 live births in 
2022. This marks a significant improvement from 39 per 1,000 live births in 2014, 
representing a Compound Annual Decline Rate (CADR) of 4.94%. 
 
India has made substantial progress in reducing IMR over the past decade, reflecting 
concerted efforts under theNationalHealth Mission (NHM) and targeted health interventions. 
Key highlights of this progress include: - 
 
 10 States/UTs have already achieved single-digit IMR. 
 29 States/UTs have met the National Health Policy (NHP) target of IMR, which is 28 
per 1,000 live births by 2019. 
 
The Ministry of Health and Family Welfare (MoHFW) supports all States/UTs in 
implementation of Reproductive, Maternal, Newborn, Child, Adolescent Health and Nutrition 
(RMNCAH + N) strategy under National Health Mission (NHM) based on the Annual 
Program Implementation Plan (APIP) submitted by States / UTs. All the inte rventions are 
universally implemented in all the States/UTs to improve the healthy survival of children. The 
details of interventions for improving child survival are as follows: - 
  
 Facility Based New-born Care: 1087 Neonatal Intensive Care Units (NICUs)/  Special 
New-born Care Units (SNCUs) are established at Medical College and District
