Further Recommendation 
 
3.29.4 The Committee takes note of the detailed response of the Department and 
appreciates the efforts made to strengthen fund utilization mechanisms under PM -
ABHIM through measures such as the introduction of Quarterly Expenditure Plans 
(QEPs), onboarding of 28 States under the SNA SPARSH platform, and enhanced 
monitoring through review meetings, workshops, and Common Review Missions. The 
Committee, however, observes that despite these initiatives, the pace of expenditure still 
reflects mid -year surges and delaye d implementation cycles, particularly for capital -
intensive infrastructure projects. The Committee, therefore, recommends the 
Department to institutionalize a quarterly performance-linked fund release mechanism , 
wherein early and efficient utilizers among the States/UTs receive priority in subsequent 
tranches. Further, the Committee urges the Department to develop an online project 
tracking dashboard  integrated with the SNA SPARSH system to provide real -time 
visibility on project progress, fund utilization,  and bottlenecks. The Committee also 
recommends the Department for establishing regional technical support cells to provide 
on-ground assistance to States with weaker administrative and technical capacity. 
Furthermore, the Department should ensure that pro ject implementation plans are 
finalized and approved within the first quarter of the fiscal year to enable front-loaded 
disbursal and early initiation of works , thereby improving fund absorption efficiency 
and ensuring the timely realization of PM -ABHIM’s objectives to build resilient, self -
reliant healthcare infrastructure across the country. 
 
 
3.30 Ayushman Bharat- Pradhan Mantri Jan Arogya Yojana (AB-PMJAY) 
Recommendations/Observations 
3.30.1 The Committee appreciates that the Government has recently exp anded the AB -
PMJAY to cover 6 crore senior citizens of age 70 years and above belonging to 4.5 crore 
families under AB-PMJAY Vay Vandana scheme, irrespective of their socio-economic status. 

166 
 
The Committee observes that an amount to the tune of Rs. 1443.00 c rore has been earmarked 
for treatment of senior citizens of the age of 70 years and above holding 
AyushmanVayVandna Cards.  The Committee is of the view that age criteria of 70 years and 
above for AyushmanVayVandna Cards should be rationalized to 60 years and above 
irrespective of their socio -economic status for widening the coverage of the scheme in the 
better interest of common masses. 
(Para 4.8.9 of the report) 
Action Taken 
 
3.30.2 The recent expansion of AB-PMJAY to cover senior citizens aged 70 years and above 
under the AyushmanVay Vandana card initiative aims to provide equitable healthcare access 
to India‘s elderly population, regardless of their socio -economic status. Under this initiative, 
launched in October 2024, all senior citizens aged 70 years  and above, from approximately 
4.5 crore families (estimated 6 crore individuals), are eligible for free annual treatment 
benefits of up to Rs. 5 lakh per family per year for secondary and tertiary care hospitalization.  
As of 23rd April 2025, over 58 lakh senior citizens have already been enrolled under this 
scheme and a total of Rs. 1,443 crore has been earmarked in budget estimate 2025 -26 
specifically for the treatment of beneficiaries under the AyushmanVay Vandana Card 
expansion. The Department remains c ommitted to enhancing the inclusiveness and 
effectiveness of AB-PMJAY and will continue to explore ways to extend healthcare coverage 
to vulnerable sections of the population in a financially sustainable manner. 
Further Recommendation 
 
3.30.3 The Committee  appreciates the steps taken by the Department of Health and 
Family Welfare in implementing the Ayushman Vay Vandana initiative under AB -
PMJAY and acknowledges the impressive enrolment of over 58 lakh senior citizens and 
the dedicated budgetary provision o f ₹1,443 crore for their treatment. The Committee, 
however, observes that while the current coverage for citizens aged 70 years and above 
is a commendable beginning, it still excludes a large segment of the elderly population 
between 60 to 69 years who are  equally vulnerable to chronic and age -related diseases 
and may not have adequate financial means for quality healthcare. The Committee is 
pleased to observe that the Department is positive enough to continue and explore to 
extend healthcare coverage to vu lnerable section of the population. The Committee, 
therefore, reiterates its earlier recommendation to revisit and rationalize the age criteria 
to include citizens aged 60 years and above , ensuring equitable healthcare protection for 
all senior citizens ir respective of socio -economic status. Further, the Committee 
recommends the Department to undertake a feasibility study  assessing the financial, 
infrastructural, and operational implications of such expansion. The Committee also 
suggests periodic evaluation of the scheme’s utilization patterns and health outcomes  to 
ensure that the benefits reach the intended beneficiaries and to support data -driven 
decisions for future expansions of universal health coverage under AB-PMJAY. 
 
 

167 
 
Recommendations/Observations 
3.30.4 The Committee, keeping in view huge expenditure on the critical healthcare, should 
revise healthcare coverage from existing Rs. 5 lakh per family per year to Rs. 10 lakh per 
family per year. 
(Para 4.8.11 of the report) 
Action Taken 
 
3.30.5 The Committee‘s recommendation to increase the healthcare coverage under the 
Ayushman Bharat-Pradhan Mantri Jan Arogya Yojana (AB -PMJAY) from Rs. 5 lakh to Rs. 
10 lakh per family per year, considering the high costs of critical healthcare, is noted. As per 
current utilization trends, 99.4% of cases involve wallet utilization of less than Rs. 2,00,000 
per family, demonstrating that the existing Rs. 5 lakh cover adequately addresses the 
hospitalization needs of the vast majority of beneficiaries. For rare cases i nvolving prolonged 
illnesses or high -end procedures requiring additional financial support, the Rashtriya Arogya 
Nidhi (RAN) scheme provides assistance of up to Rs. 15 lakh to patients from poor families, 
including those eligible under SECC criteria for AB -PMJAY, for treatments not covered by 
the scheme. This complementary mechanism ensures that critical healthcare needs are met 
without imposing financial burdens on beneficiaries. Therefore, it can be understood that AB 
PM-JAY along with other schemes of Go vt of India are able to cater to the hospitalization 
requirement of the majority of the eligible beneficiaries. 
Further Recommendation 
 
3.30.6 The Committee takes into consideration  the plea of the Department that the 
existing coverage of ₹5 lakh per famil y under AB -PMJAY meets the hospitalization 
needs of most beneficiaries, and that additional financial protection is available under 
the Rashtriya Arogya Nidhi (RAN) for critical cases. The Committee is , however, of the 
considered view that the rising cost of advanced medical procedures, specialized  
treatments, and long -term care  necessitates a progressive enhancement of financial 
coverage under AB-PMJAY. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Ministry 
undertake a comprehensive actuarial and utilizati on study  to assess the financial and 
operational feasibility of increasing the insurance ceiling to ₹10 lakh per family, keeping 
in view high cost of treatment of patients with chronic, life -threatening disease. 
Alternatively, as an interim measure the Department may consider for strengthening the 
integration between AB -PMJAY and RAN  to ensure seamless support for beneficiaries 
requiring extended or repeated treatments, and to develop an automatic top -up 
mechanism for families exhausting their coverage limi t under AB -PMJAY. This 
calibrated approach would ensure enhanced financial protection and accessible 
treatment of patients under PMJAY while maintaining fiscal sustainability and 
equitable access to critical healthcare. 
 
 
 

168 
 
3.31 Coverage of OPD Services and Cost of Medicine under the Ayushman Bharat 
 
Recommendations/Observations 
3.31.1 The Committee recommends the phased inclusion of essential OPD services and a 
broader range of post -hospitalization medication coverage within the Ayushman Bharat 
PMJAY scheme. While acknowledging the scheme's focus on secondary and tertiary inpatient 
care, the current exclusion of routine OPD services and limited post -hospitalization 
medication coverage creates a significant gap in accessible healthcare. To ensure continuity 
and build upon the existing framework, the recommendation should propose a phased 
approach, starting with the inclusion of essential OPD services for chronic diseases and 
commonly reported ailments. Furthermore, extending post -hospitalization medication 
coverage beyond the current 15 -day limit, particularly for conditions requiring prolonged 
treatment, would significantly reduce the financial burden on beneficiaries while addressing a 
need for comprehensive healthcare access. 
(Para 5.11.2 of the report) 
Action Taken 
 
3.31.2 The Committee's recommendations regarding the phased inclusion of essential OPD 
services and extended post -hospitalization medication coverage under the Ayushman Bharat 
PMJAY scheme are duly noted.  It is to be noted that in the current  framework, medication 
costs are covered during hospitalization and for 15 days‘ post -discharge under AB PM -JAY. 
Additionally, free OPD services are available through the Ayushman Arogya Mandir (AAM) 
network as part of the broader Ayushman Bharat initiativ e. AB PM-JAY presently focuses on 
secondary and tertiary inpatient care. 
Further Recommendation 
 
3.31.3 The Committee notes the response of the Department that outpatient services are 
currently provided through the Ayushman Arogya Mandir (AAM) network and that 
AB-PMJAY focuses primarily on secondary and tertiary inpatient care. While 
acknowledging the complementary role of AAM in providing OPD services, the 
Committee is of the view that the absence of integrated OPD and extended post -
hospitalization medicat ion coverage within AB -PMJAY limits continuity of care  and 
increases the out -of-pocket burden, especially for patients with chronic and long -term 
conditions. The Committee, therefore, urges the Department to initiate a pilot project in 
select districts to assess the feasibility of including essential OPD consultations, 
diagnostic services, and post-hospitalization medication coverage beyond the current 15-
day limit, viz.up to 30 or 45 days,  under AB -PMJAY for continuity of treatment of 
disease under single umbrella. This pilot should focus particularly on non-
communicable diseases and geriatric care , where follow -up treatment and medication 
adherence are critical. Based on the outcomes of this pilot, a phased national roll -out 
scheme may be undertaken to ens ure a seamless continuum of care between AAM and 

169 
 
AB-PMJAY, thereby enhancing accessibility, affordability, and overall health outcomes 
for beneficiaries. 
 
3.32 Misuse of Fund under AB-PMJAY Scheme 
 
Recommendations/Observations 
3.32.1 The Committee recommends the National Health Authority (NHA) implement a 
proactive, data -driven, and transparent monitoring system focusing on early detection of 
potential misuse patterns in AB -PMJAY. This system should go beyond reacting to reported 
fraud by continuously analysing claim data, hospital activity, and patient feedback to identify 
statistical anomalies and emerging trends indicating potential abuse. Specifically, the NHA 
should develop and publicly report on key performance indicators related to fraud detec tion, 
including the frequency of pre -authorization requests for specific procedures, the ratio of 
medical camp referrals to hospital admissions, and the rates of hospital de -empanelment and 
penalties by state. In the collection and analysis of statistical data and feedback, the extensive 
use of AI and digital tools should be considered upon by the Department. This transparent, 
data-driven approach will not only allow for quicker intervention but also serve as a deterrent, 
ensuring the scheme's resources are  used for their intended purpose and maintaining public 
trust.  
(Para 5.12.4 of the report) 
Action Taken 
 
3.32.2 A proactive, data -driven, and technology -enabled monitoring framework has been 
established under the Ayushman Bharat Pradhan Mantri Jan Arogya Yojana (AB-PMJAY) to 
enable early detection and deterrence of misuse and fraudulent activities. NHA has developed 
a multi -layered surveillance ecosystem that goes beyond reactive investigation and ensures 
continuous monitoring of claim patterns, hospital b ehaviours, and beneficiary interactions.  
Key Measures and Systems Implemented: - 
(i) National and State Anti -Fraud Units (NAFU and SAFUs):  
Dedicated anti-fraud teams have been established to investigate irregularities, coordinate 
with State Health Agencies (SH As), and enforce penal actions such as de -empanelment, 
blacklisting, and financial recovery. 
(ii) FACTS and RADAR Dashboards:The Fraud Control Tracking System (FACTS) and Risk 
Assessment Detection and Analytical Reporting (RADAR) dashboards support real -time 
tracking, reporting, and resolution of suspicious activities. These tools identify anomalies 
in hospitalization trends, claim volume surges, and procedural patterns. 
 
(iii) Advanced Fraud Detection Triggers: The fraud detection framework has evolved from 
basic rule-based filters to a sophisticated system of AI -enabled triggers, including:  
 
 Image Deduplication: Detects use of duplicate or manipulated images to prevent multiple 
enrolments and fraudulent claims. 

170 
 
 Forged Document Sampling: Applies image processing al gorithms to identify tampering 
techniques like copy-move or external element insertions in medical documentation. 
 Document Similarity Analysis (OCR): Extracts text from scanned documents and 
performs word-level comparisons to identify manipulated or recycled documents based on 
similarity percentages. 
 Social Network Analysis: Uses Graph Theory to detect interconnected fraud networks, or 
cartels, through linkages among beneficiaries, hospitals, and service providers. 
 Image Segmentation: Segregates and validat es clinical elements within medical 
documents to ensure authenticity of diagnostic and treatment-related data. 
 Facial Comparison Technology: Matches reference photos with claim -related images to 
detect impersonation and identity fraud during admissions and card creation. 
 These automated triggers enable real -time detection and intervention, ensuring that 
fraudulent entities are swiftly identified and deterred. 
(iv) Public Reporting and Transparency:Periodic reporting on fraud detection outcomes, de -
empanelment, penalties imposed, and recoveries made are encouraged to promote 
accountability. As of date, over 1,100 hospitals have been de -empanelled and significant 
financial recoveries and penalties have been imposed across various States. 
 
3.32.3 The Department con tinues to strengthen its anti -fraud capabilities through emerging 
technologies and advanced analytics to ensure optimal utilization of public resources and to 
safeguard the integrity of the scheme. 
Further Recommendation 
 
3.32.4 The Committee appreciates t he comprehensive and technology -driven measures 
adopted by the Department and the NHA for fraud detection and prevention under AB -
PMJAY, including the establishment of National and State Anti -Fraud Units (NAFU 
and SAFUs), deployment of AI -enabled analytical tools, and creation of the FACTS and 
RADAR dashboards. These efforts mark a commendable shift toward proactive 
surveillance and deterrence of misuse and fraudulent activities. The Committee observes 
that while significant progress has been made in intern al monitoring , however, there 
remains a need to institutionalize periodic public disclosure of performance indicators  
related to fraud detection, de -empanelment, and recovery actions to enhance 
transparency and public confidence. The Committee, therefore, recommends that NHA 
should publish a biannual ―Fraud and Integrity Report‖  detailing fraud detection 
trends, actions taken, and improvements in monitoring mechanisms at both national 
and state levels. Further, capacity-building programmes for State Anti -Fraud Units  
should be strengthened to ensure uniform standards of investigation and enforcement 
across all States. The Committee also suggests the integration of beneficiary feedback 
analytics and hospital audit outcomes  into the AI -driven monitoring framewo rk to 
create a more holistic, responsive, and publicly accountable system for safeguarding the 
integrity and trustworthiness of AB -PMJAY so as to make AB -PMJAY a popular 
scheme amongst common masses especially, rural, semi-urban and tribal areas. 
 
 
 
 

171 
 
3.33 Pradhan Mantri National Dialysis Programme (PMNDP) 
 
Recommendations/Observations 
3.33.1 The Committee is of the view that the Dialysis facility under PMNDP should be given 
to the needy persons. The facility is being availed by persons who can afford treatment from 
their own pocket in private hospitals. The scheme provisions need to be reviewed. The 
Committee finds that the dialysis centres are at the district level at present, however, the 
Committee desires the Department should make strategy for sett ing up of Dialysis facility at 
the Taluka level also as kidney patients are increasing. 
(Para 5.13.3 of the report) 
Action Taken 
 
3.33.2 The database for the Below Poverty Level (BPL) population is based on the 
Population Census held by Government of India  in 2011. The current assessment report for 
the number of BPL population is not available. The exact prevalence and incidence rate of 
chronic kidney disease is not known due to the absence of renal registry in the country.   The 
provision available under Pr adhan Mantri National Dialysis Programme (PMNDP) mandates 
free dialysis to the BPL category patients and the other patients (Non -BPL) who can afford 
dialysis in the private at subsidized cost. Currently, under NHM 100 % of the service 
procedure fees for patients from BPL economic group is covered. The PMNDP portal has the 
facility to register the BPL and Above Poverty Level (APL) financial status based on the 
entitlement card issued by the States/UTs at the time of new patient registration. A total of 
60,489 BPL patients and 16976 APL patients have been registered on the PMNDP portal 
since 1st January 2024 to 31st  December 2024. PMNDP was launched in FY 2016 -17 by 
Ministry of Health and Family Welfare with the objective to have a dialysis centre in all the 
district hospitals across the States/UTs in the country. The programme has the provision for 
scaling up the dialysis services down below the District Hospital level till Taluka 
(Community Health Centres) based on demand gap assessment by the States/UTs. Few  States 
have already established dialysis care units at the Taluka level as implemented in the states of 
Gujarat, Andhra Pradesh, Assam, Goa, Himachal Pradesh, Karnataka, Maharashtra, Odisha, 
Tamil Nadu, Telangana, West Bengal & Kerala. 
Further Recommendation 
 
3.33.3 The Committee appreciates the efforts of the Department under the Pradhan 
Mantri National Dialysis Programme (PMNDP) in ensuring free dialysis services to BPL 
patients and the initiative taken by several States to extend dialysis facilities to the 
Taluka level. The Committee, however, notes with concern that the reliance on the 
outdated 2011 Census data for determining BPL status may lead to the exclusion of 
genuinely needy patients and the inclusion of non -eligible beneficiaries. The Committee, 
therefore, recommends that the Department, in coordination with the States/UTs, 
should develop an updated, verifiable, and dynamic beneficiary database  leveraging 
digital health platforms such as ABHA ID and SECC data to ensure accurate 

172 
 
identification of economically weaker patients. Further, to address the growing burden 
of chronic kidney disease, the Committee urges the Department to expedite the scaling 
up of dialysis centres to all Taluka -level Community Health Centres (CHCs)  through a 
time-bound natio nal expansion plan, ensuring equitable geographic access. The 
Committee also emphasizes the need for the creation of a National Renal Registry  to 
generate reliable epidemiological data for effective planning, monitoring, and resource 
allocation under PMNDP  and take into stock actual number of renal patients so that 
fresh policy initiative can be undertaken. 
 
3.34 Trauma and Emergency Care Services 
Recommendations/Observations 
3.34.1 The Committee understands that NPPMT&BI scheme aims at reducing the mortality 
and morbidity occurring due to injuries (including trauma and burns), by developing a system 
of services at pre- hospital & hospital level and continuum of care through rehabilitation. The 
Committee desires that adequate funds may beallocated for  Trauma and Emergency Care 
Services to achieve the significant goals of the scheme that includes creating center of 
excellence from trauma and Burn Care in existing Government Medical Colleges. 
(Para 5.16.3 of the report) 
Action Taken 
 
3.34.2 During 11th Five Year Plan (2007 -12), 116 TCFs & 9 Burns Units were created.  
During 12th FYP Plan (2012 -17), 80 TCFs & 38 Burn Units were created.  Out of total 196 
TCFs & 47 Burn Units created so far, 163 TCFs and 31 Burn Units have become functional 
and are providing services to trauma/burn victims respectively.  Beyond 12th plan, there is no 
provision of identification of new trauma care facilities and financial assistance is in terms of 
release of the balance share of the central assistance within the total approved cost of the 
approved facilities/units. The scheme has the approval till the year 2025 -26. Rs.134 crore has 
been allocated from 2021-2022 to 2025-2026 and Rs.93.92 crore has been spent till 2024-25. 
3.34.3 07 Medical Colleges/ Hospitals have been declared a s Centre of Excellence (CoE) 
under the programme and Rs 8.4 crore has been released to 07 COEs for strengthening the 
emergency, trauma and burn care services. CoE will provide high quality advanced 
comprehensive care to all emergency, trauma and burn patie nts, Hand holding for the other 
medical colleges and district hospitals for clinical care, Quality Rehabilitation services for 
post injury management, academic & research. Training will help in creating experts in the 
field of emergency trauma and burns by  developing various training courses and modules. 
These institutes will provide leadership to local and regional Emergency Medical Service 
(EMS) System in terms of innovation, public awareness campaigns and periodic feedback. 
Trauma & Burn care management trainings have been conducted in AIIMS, New Delhi, 
Safdarjung Hospital, New Delhi and Dr. R.M.L. Hospital. 
 
 

173 
 
Further Recommendation 
 
3.34.4 The Committee notes with appreciation the progress made under the National 
Programme for Prevention and Management of Trauma and Burn Injuries 
(NPPMT&BI), including the establishment of Trauma Care Facilities (TCFs), Burn 
Units, and seven Centres of Excellence (CoEs) for advanced trauma and burn care. 
Despite these achievements, the Committee observes with concern that  out of total 196 
TCFs and 47 Burn units created so far, only a total of 163 TCFs and 31 Burn units are 
functional. The Committee is surprised over the absence of provisions for identifying 
and funding new TCFs beyond the 12th Plan period (2012 -2017) that might have 
hindered the expansion of trauma care infrastructure, particularly in underserved 
regions. The Committee, therefore, recommends the Department  should formulate a 
comprehensive roadmap for the next phase of the programme beyond 2025 -26, with 
dedicated budgetary provisions for the establishment of new trauma and burn care 
facilities at strategic locations, especially along national highways and in high -accident-
prone districts. The Committee further emphasizes that the existing CoEs should be 
adequately funded and networked with district and sub -district hospitals  through 
telemedicine, referral, and training linkages to ensure an integrated continuum of 
trauma care. The Committee urges the Department to undertake regular monitoring 
and performance audits of functional units  to assess outcomes, resource utilization, and 
preparedness, thereby ensuring that the objectives of reducing trauma -related 
morbidity and mortality are effectively realized. 
 
3.35 Public Private Partnership (PPP) Model for Promoting Medical Tourism 
 
Recommendations/Observations 
3.35.1 The Committee understands that the promotion of medical tourism requires multi -
disciplinary and multi -pronged approach, thereby seeks the involvement of Ministry of 
Tourism and the Ministry of Externa l Affairs but no one can exclude the pivotal role of the 
Department of Health and Family Welfare because the subject health where upon the focus is 
to be made for promoting medical tourism. The Committee perceives that the rate of medical 
tourism growth is  much higher than the normal tourism growth, therefore the role of the 
Department of Health and Family Welfare is of pivotal importance. The Committee observes 
that growth of medical tourism in private sector is better than the public sector, therefore, 
there is enormous scope for promoting medical tourism in public sector. The Committee 
further recommends that the Department of Health and Family Welfare, in tandem with the 
Ministry of Tourism and the Ministry of External Affairs, leave no stone unturned in  
emphasising Public Private Partnership (PPP) model for promoting medical tourism. 
(Para 5.18.2of the report) 
Action Taken 
 
3.35.2 The Ecosystem for medical and wellness tourism includes a host of service providers, 
facilitators, commercial agencies such as hotels and airlines, regulatory agencies and the 

174 
 
Government. There is a need to establish linkages and understanding amongst the 
stakeholders about the role and responsibilities of different stakeholders and forge 
partnerships for promoting the sector. It involves healthcare service providers, VISA 
requirements, insurance, MVT facilitators etc. It is again submitted that as per the 
Government of India (Allocation of Business) Rules, 1961, the subject matter of 
‗Development and Promotion of Tourism‘ is allocated to the Ministry of Tourism. As such, 
the matter of boosting Medical Tourism falls under the purview of the Ministry of Tourism.  
3.35.3 Ministry honours the suggestions of Hon‘ble Committee for promoting medical 
tourism in public sector. In this regard it is humbly submitted that the public sector hospitals 
are presently heavily burdened on account of the domestic demand. MoHFW  recognises the 
importance of stakeholder consultations with trade bodies and other private sector players for 
promoting medical tourism. Ministry of Health and Family Welfare is exploring possible 
engagements with stakeholders for development of capacity of medical professionals and 
healthcare workers for promotion of the sector and will take further necessary action as 
emanating from the discussion. It is also informed that in order to ensure capacity building in 
medical tourism sector through PPP Mode, MoHFW is actively engaging with various private 
players like FICCI, NATHEALTH, etc. 
Further Recommendation 
 
3.35.4 The Committee acknowledges the pivotal role of inter -ministerial coordination 
and stakeholder engagement in promoting medical tourism. The Com mittee also 
appreciates the role of the Department in its ongoing efforts to collaborate with industry 
bodies such as FICCI and NATHEALTH. The Committee observes that despite 
significant potential, public sector participation in medical tourism remains min imal 
due to high domestic demand and lack of a structured framework. The Committee, 
therefore, recommends that the Ministry of Health and Family Welfare, in coordination 
with the Ministries of Tourism and External Affairs, develop a comprehensive National 
Medical and Wellness Tourism Strategy  with a clear roadmap for leveraging both 
public and private healthcare infrastructure. Such strategy should include identification 
of select public hospitals with spare capacity to serve international patients , 
establishment of Medical Tourism Facilitation Cells , and formulation of standardized 
service and pricing protocols  to ensure quality and transparency. The Committee 
further recommends creating a Public-Private Partnership (PPP) model for capacity 
building, accreditation, and international promotion, supported by targeted training for 
healthcare workers and seamless visa facilitation for medical travelers. Such a 
coordinated and institutionalized approach will not only enhance India’s global 
reputation in medical a nd wellness tourism but also generate additional revenue for 
reinvestment in the public healthcare system. 
 
 
 
 

175 
 
3.36 Accessible and Affordable Treatment of Rare Diseases 
Recommendations/Observations 
3.36.1 The Committee notes that 63 rare diseases are covered under National Policy for Rare 
Diseases (NPRD) which of course needs to be reviewed and expanded from time to time. In 
order to make the treatment of rare diseases accessible and affordable, the Department may 
make provision for cheaper diagnosis, prevention and treatment. The Committee also desires 
that the Department should launch national campaign/awareness programme for seeking 
treatment of the rare diseases. 
(Para 5.19.3 of the report) 
Action Taken 
 
3.36.2 Under the National policy of Rare Dise ases (NPRD) 2021, there is a mechanism for 
review and expansion of Rare Disease. The Central Technical Committee for Rare Diseases 
(CTCRD) under the Chairmanship of DGHS is mandated to periodically review the list of 
diseases under Group 1, Group 2 and Gro up 3 based on updated scientific data by the 
Technical Committee which includes experts from the field. The Committee after discussion 
within Institutional Rare Disease Committee at CoE, Nodal Officer submit a detailed write -up 
about the disease, for discu ssion and consideration for inclusion of the disease under NPRD 
2021 to Central Technical Committee for Rare Diseases (CTCRD). Since the inception of the 
NPRD 2021, 14 rare diseases in addition to existing mentioned in the policy, have been 
included under NPRD 2021, following a robust and scientific process.  To promote Research in 
the field of rare diseases, DHR -ICMR has launched two major initiatives for the prevention and 
control of rare diseases: - 
 
 DHR-ICMR Program for Indigenous Therapeutics for Rare D iseases: This program 
promotes industry -academia partnerships. Academia and R&D organizations receive 
funding through ICMR‘s extramural program, while industry partners focus on drug 
development, with ICMR supporting preclinical, clinical research, and reg ulatory 
approvals. The goal is to develop indigenous therapies for clinical use, with intellectual 
property remaining with the inventor and drugs being marketed at affordable prices. 
 
 DHR-ICMR-National Registry for Rare and Other Inherited Disorders: Estab lished in 
2019, this ongoing, multi -centre hospital-based registry collects comprehensive data on 
individuals diagnosed with rare diseases. Currently, it holds information on over 15,400 
patients. The registry includes data on various disorders like Storag e Disorders, Inborn 
Errors of Metabolism, Skeletal Dysplasia, and more. Contributing centres from 14 states 
and union territories ensure nationwide representation. The registry aligns with the 
National Policy on Rare Diseases (NPRD) and informs government efforts, with plans to 
integrate the registry with a National Rare Disease Information Portal, which will be 
completed in three months. 
 
3.36.3 The MoHFW identified 8 Centres of Excellence (CoE) for Rare Diseases under NPRD 
2021, which have now been extend ed to 13 in 12 States/UTs across the country. These are 
premier Government tertiary hospitals with facilities for diagnosis, prevention and treatment 

176 
 
of rare diseases.  The responsibilities and activities of the Centres of Excellence (COEs) 
identified under the policy are as follows: - 
 Education & Training at all levels  
 Screening – Antenatal, neonatal (specified disorders), High risk screening (Both antenatal 
& in new-borns and children)  
 Diagnostics- Cytogenetic, molecular, Metabolic  
 Prevention by prenatal screening &diagnosis  
 Research in the area of low-cost diagnostics & therapeutics.  
 Treatment of rare diseases.  
 
3.36.4 As per NPRD 2021, based on the recommendations of the technical committee, more 
CoEs can be added for regional o utreach if they are found to be suitable in terms of 
infrastructure and human resources. To strengthen the infrastructure of the CoEs identified 
under the policy and to facilitate early diagnosis to enable further course of treatment the 
COEs are given one -time financial support up to a ceiling of Rs 5 crore for procurement of 
equipment as per individual centers need for screening, diagnosis and prevention (prenatal 
diagnosis) of rare diseases based on a gap analysis. In addition, financial support for purc hase 
of consumables for diagnosis is also supported under ‗Materials and Supplies‘ budget head to 
CoEs. The Central Government has given full exemption from Basic Custom Duty (BCD) and 
Integrated Goods and Services Tax (IGST) on most of the drugs and Food for Special 
Medical Purposes (FSMP) imported for personal use or through CoEs for rare diseases for 
treatment of all rare diseases listed under the NPRD 2021 through a general exemption 
notification. To increase the awareness about Rare Diseases, the MoHFW  observe Rare 
Disease Day on the last day of February. This year in 2025, sensitization workshops for 
raising awareness of medical professionals were organized in 11 States/UTs by CoEs from the 
month of Jan – Feb 2025 with support from MoHFW.  
Further Recommendation 
 
3.36.5 The Committee acknowledges the significant progress in expanding the scope of 
the National Policy for Rare Diseases (NPRD) 2021, viz. the inclusion of additional 
diseases, establishment of 13 Centres of Excellence (CoEs), and the launch of initiatives 
such as the National Registry and the Indigenous Therapeutics Programme. The 
Committee observes that awareness, early diagnosis, and affo rdability of treatment 
remain key challenges , particularly in rural and underserved areas. The Committee, 
therefore, recommends that the Ministry intensify its national awareness and outreach 
campaign through collaboration with State Governments, medical i nstitutions, and civil 
society organizations to ensure greater public and professional understanding of rare 
diseases. Further, the Committee urges the Department to expedite the 
operationalization of the National Rare Disease Information Portal  and integrate it with 
the CoEs and the National Registry for real -time data sharing, patient tracking, and 
referral facilitation. The Committee also recommends increasing the number of CoEs 
across all regions , particularly in the North -East and smaller States, to im prove 
accessibility. In addition, affordable diagnostic and therapeutic solutions should be 

177 
 
prioritized under the DHR -ICMR Indigenous Therapeutics Programme  with defined 
timelines for clinical translation. The Committee emphasizes that a dedicated National 
Awareness and Prevention Mission on Rare Diseases  may be launched and promote 
early detection, genetic counseling, and social support mechanisms for affected families. 
 
3.37 Other Recommendations of the Committee 
 
Recommendations/Observations 
3.37.1 The s anctioning of Medical Colleges should be on the basis of population and not 
district-wise as this creates imbalance in Hospital to population ratio. Moreover, the quality of 
Medicine and Nursing education should be as per National Quality Assurance Standar ds 
(NQAS) and the global best practices; 
(Para 5.20(i) of the report) 
Action Taken 
 
3.37.2 Objective Clause of Chapter-1 of UG-MSR 2023 notified by UGMEB on 16.06.2023, 
mandates that ―Provided that medical college shall follow the ratio of 100 MBBS seats over 
10 lakh population in that State/UT.‖, but the same has been deferred from implementation 
from academic year (AY) 2024 -25 vide letter dated 01.11.2023 and for AY 2025 -26 vide 
order dated 19.12.2024.  The medical education through the colleges accredite d to the 
National Medical Commission (NMC) is expected to have uniform standards. This is 
achieved by way of uniform curriculum as well uniform inputs that are required for education. 
The education follows accepted international model. For this reason, NMC  made an 
application to an international accreditation body, World Federation of Medical Education 
(WFME). The organization visited the NMC on two different occasions and evaluated 
different parameters. This also included visit to colleges which are runnin g under the NMC 
Regulations. After the inspection and verifying the compliance, the WFME has accredited the 
NMC for a period of 10 years. This accreditation is a testimony to the quality of education 
from the Indian Medical Colleges under the national Medi cal Commission. Students educated 
from these colleges are now seen on par with the medical graduates across the world in most 
countries. They can compete to get higher education or jobs in those countries. 
3.37.3 As per Indian Nursing Council, the National  Quality Assurance Standards of NHSRC, 
MoHFW have been developed keeping in view of the specific requirements for public health 
facilities as well global best practices. NQAS are currently available for district hospitals, 
CHCs, PHCs and Urban PHCs. Variou s regulations to maintain quality of nursing education 
in various nursing institutions has been formulated by Indian Council which are gazettes and 
placed in INC website as MINIMUM PRE -REQUISITES FOR GRANTING SUITABILITY 
TO NURSING PROGRAMS  REGULATIONS, 20 20 dated 
12.03.2021.https://indiannursingcouncil.org/uploads/pdf/16179447791151563446606fe0cb41
610.pdf Indian Nursing Council has signed a MoU with Quality cou ncil of India on 
05.12.2023 for assessment and rating of nursing educational institutions under INC by QCI 
with the aim to ensure the delivery of Quality Education, provide excellence in nursing 

178 
 
education and foster continuous, improvement in nursing educa tion institutions leading to 
‗Quality Assurance‘ & quality Sustenance in nursing education sector. In this regard, pilot 
project is over and the process is underway for starting assessment for all Nursing Institutions. 
Indian Nursing Council (INC) had also  undertaken various comprehensive initiatives to 
strengthen the quality of pre -service education (PSE) for nursing -midwifery cadre in India. 
INC, with the support from Jhpiego initiated a programme for strengthening the quality of 
pre-service education. 
3.37.4 The strengthening of the nursing educational institutions was a practical management 
approach for improving performance and quality. It consistedof systematic, consistent, and 
effective utilization of operational performance standards as the basis for  on-site assessment 
and improvement in quality of Pre-Service Nursing-Midwifery Education. The standards were 
organized in five sections that included classroom teaching, clinical instruction, school 
management practices, physical and training infrastructure and the clinical site practice. 
Further Recommendation 
 
3.37.5 The Committee takes note of the measures taken by the Department and the 
regulatory bodies such as National Medical Commission (NMC) and Indian Nursing 
Council (INC) to improve the quality and standards of medical and nursing education in 
alignment with global benchmarks, including accreditation of NMC by WFME for a 
period of 10 years indicating quality of the Indian Medical Colleges and implementation 
of NQAS-based frameworks providing excel lence in nursing education sector leading to 
Quality Assurance and Quality Sustenance in nursing education sector. The Committee 
observes with deep concern that the population-based approach for sanctioning medical 
colleges, as mandated under the UG -MSR 2023, has been deferred for successive 
academic years 2024 -25 and 2025 -26, thereby delaying the correction of regional 
imbalances in medical education and healthcare accessibility. The Committee, therefore, 
recommends that the Department should expedite the implementation of the population-
based criterion  for establishing medical colleges to ensure equitable distribution of 
healthcare infrastructure across States and regions, especially in underserved and 
densely populated are as. Further, the Committee recommends that periodic quality 
audits and independent assessments  be institutionalized for both medical and nursing 
colleges to ensure adherence to NQAS and global best practices. The Committee also 
urges the Ministry to fast-track the nationwide rollout of the Quality Council of India 
(QCI)-led accreditation of nursing institutions  and develop a centralized digital 
dashboard for monitoring institutional ratings, faculty adequacy, and training 
outcomes. In addition, the Committe e emphasizes the need for continuous professional 
development programmes, faculty exchange, and integration of simulation -based and 
digital learning tools  to further enhance the competency and global competitiveness of 
India’s healthcare education system. 
 
Recommendations/Observations 
3.37.6 Provision of sufficient staff, essential diagnostics and quality generic medicines must 
be made available in Ayushman Arogya Mandirs (AAMs); 

179 
 
(Para 5.20(v) of the report) 
Action Taken 
3.37.7 Through Ayushman Arogya Mandir, the Government is expanding primary healthcare 
facilities, improving service delivery by supporting the availability of sufficient staff, 
essential diagnostics and quality generic medicines, thus enhancing the overall resilience of 
the health system. There are 1,76,981 operational AAM, and 1,39,085 CHOs are in position at 
AAM SHCs. (as of 6th April, 2025, Source: AAM Portal)  66% (1,09,320) of AAM have at 
least 80% of the essential medicines available and 77% (1,26,777) of AAM have at least 80% 
of the ess ential diagnostics available. Moreover, under the Free Drugs and Diagnostics 
Services Initiative, support is being given to the States for the provision of 106 medicines at 
AAM- SHC, and 172 at AAM -PHC as per the Essential Medicine List. Further, support i s 
provided for 14 tests at the AAM-SHC and 63 tests at the AAM- PHC level. 
Further Recommendation 
 
3.37.8 The Committee appreciates the progress achieved in operationalizing a large 
network of Ayushman Arogya Mandirs (AAMs) and ensuring availability of sta ff, 
essential diagnostics, and generic medicines under the Free Drugs and Diagnostics 
Services Initiative. The Committee notes that despite the commendable coverage 
achieved, a considerable proportion of AAMs are yet to attain full compliance in the 
availability of essential medicines and diagnostic services, which are vital for effective 
primary healthcare delivery. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the 
Department intensify its efforts to achieve 100% availability of essential drugs and 
diagnostics across all AAMs  through improved supply chain management, real -time 
monitoring, and periodic stock audits using digital dashboards. The Committee also 
recommends strengthening human resource deployment  by filling the remaining 
vacancies of Community Health Officers (CHOs) and allied health staff through 
targeted recruitment drives and skill enhancement programmes. The Committee 
suggests that the Department institutionalize a performance-linked incentive mechanism 
for States/UTs based on availability of essential supplies and service delivery standards 
at AAMs. The Committee also emphasizes the need for integration of telemedicine, e -
diagnostics, and central procurement systems  to ensure timely and equita ble access to 
quality healthcare and medicines, particularly in remote and rural areas. 
 
 
Recommendations/Observations 
3.37.9 Keeping in view the pivotal role of ASHA workers in the effective implementation of 
the various schemes of the Government on the g round level but being paid ranging from Rs. 
5000- 9000 per month which is, of course, not sufficient for earning bread of two times for a 
single person, leave apart maintaining good health and basic needs, there is urgent need to 
enhance the honorarium of ASHA workers; 
(Para 5.20(viii) of the report) 
 
 

180 
 
Action Taken 
 
3.37.10 ASHAs are community health volunteers providing preventive, promotive and 
basic curative are in a role complementary to other health functionaries expected to dedicate 
approximately 2 to  4 of work per day. ASHAs are community health volunteers providing 
preventive, promotive and basic curative care in a role complementary to other health 
functionaries expected to dedicate approximately 2 to 4 hours of work per day.  ASHAs 
receive a fixed i ncentive for routine and recurring activities on monthly basis. In addition to 
routine and recurring activities, they are provided performance -based incentives for a varied 
set of activities under various National Health Programmes which are revised timely  based on 
need and priority. States/ UTs are also providing additional State -specific incentives over and 
above those approved by the Government of India. To further support ASHAs, ASHA Benefit 
Package was introduced in 2018 that includes: - 
 
 Revision of ASHA incentive for routine and recurring activities from Rs.1000/ - to 
Rs.2000/-. 
 
 Coverage include Pradhan Mantri Jeevan Jyoti Beema Yojana (PMJJBY) with a benefit 
Rs. 2.00 lakh in case of death of the insured, coverage of Pradhan Mantri Suraksha Beema 
Yojana (PMSBY) with a benefit of Rs.2.00 lakh for accidental death or permanent 
disability and Rs. 1.00 lakh for partial disability. 
 
 After the launch of the Ayushman Bharat scheme with the operationalization of 
Ayushman Arogya Mandir (AAM), ASHAs are additio nally eligible for Team Based 
Incentives (TBIs) along with ANMs based on monitored performance indicators (up to 
Rs.1000 per month). Also, ASHA/ASHA Facilitator under ASHA certification 
programme are eligible for Rs.5000/ - additional incentive on getting c ertified in each of 
the components. Additionally, ASHAs are entitled to non -monetary incentives like - 
ASHA Uniform, CUG Sim etc. 
 
 Recently, MoHFW has undertaken revision/addition of programme -specific incentives 
given to ASHAs based on the decision of the  MSG meeting held on 4th March, 2025, 
which includes the following: 
 
 Revision of ASHA incentive for routine and recurring activities from Rs. 2000/ - to Rs. 
3,500/-. 
 
 Revised provision of recognition of ASHAs who leave/opt to leave the program after 10 
years of services in the NHM from Rs. 20,000/ to Rs. 50,000/-. 
 
 Revision of Enhaced Drug Administrators (ASHA) Honorarium under Lymphatic 
Filariasis(NCVBDC) from Rs 600/ - for 3 days to Rs 3000/ - for 15 days for a population 
of a village (approx. 1000 population or 250 houses).  
 

181 
 
 Revision of ASHA transportation incentive for accompanying women for availing safe 
and legal abortion services: 
 Rs. 200/- transportation incentive for abortion by Surgical Method (conducted in OT) at a 
public health facility 
 
 Rs. 400/- transportation incentive for abortion by Medical Methods of Abortion (MMA). 
 
 Revision in ASHA incentive under National Family Planning Programme: 
 Escorting/facilitating beneficiary for PPIUCD/PAIUCD insertion: Rs. 300 per case 
(previously Rs. 150). 
 
 Enhanced incentive for the 4th dose of lnjectable Contraceptive MPA: Rs 200 per dose 
(Previously Rs. 100). 
 
 NayiPehal Kit distribution: Rs. 200 per kit (previously Rs. 100). 
 
 Saas Bahu Sammelan mobilization: Rs. 200 per meeting (previously Rs.100). 
 
3.37.11 Further, ASHAs across the States/UTs are eligible to be covered under 
Pradhan Mantri Shram Yogi MaanDhan (PM -SYM) with pension benefit of Rs. 3,000/ - pm 
after age of 60 years. Also, ASHAs along with their family members are provided with health 
care annual cov erage of Rs 5 Lakhs under Ayushman Bharat Pradhan Mantri Jan Arogya 
Yojana (AB-PMJAY). 
 
Further Recommendation 
3.37.12 The Committee takes note of the various benefits and  payment made to 
ASHA workers intended towards enhancing the incentives and social security benefits 
including the recent upward revision of their routine and recurring activity incentive 
from ₹2,000 to ₹3,500, benefits under the ASHA Benefit Package, and their inclusion 
under schemes such as PMJJBY, PMSBY, PM -SYM, and AB -PMJAY. The Committee 
is of the view that despite these revision in remuneration and benefits, the total 
remuneration received by ASHAs continues to remain inadequate considering the 
critical and multifaceted role ASHAs play in delivering primary healthcare services at 
the grassroots level and their increasing workload with the expansion of government 
health programmes. The Committee, ther efore, recommends the Department to 
formulate a comprehensive policy framework to ensure fair, uniform, and sustainable 
remuneration for ASHAs across all States/UTs , taking into account regional cost -of-
living variations and inflation. The Committee furthe r recommends that the 
Department should consider transitioning ASHAs from a purely incentive -based model 
to a mixed model with a fixed monthly honorarium supplemented by performance -
linked incentives , thereby ensuring income stability and recognition of th eir essential 
public health contribution. The Committee also urges the Department to institutionalize 
mechanisms for periodic review of incentives , strengthen health insurance and pension 

182 
 
coverage, and ensure timely disbursement of dues to maintain ASHAs’ motivation and 
retention in the community health workforce. 
 
 
Recommendations/Observations 
3.37.13 Continuous Glucose Monitors (CGMs) and diabetic medicines are essential for 
effective diabetes management which is a chronic disease condition. However, the high cost 
of CGMs, their limited lifespan and the lack of long -term insurance coverage for these 
devices and medicines create significant challenges for patients. The Committee, therefore, 
recommends increasing the lifespan of CGM sensors, implementing pri ce controls and price 
caps on CGM sensors, cardiac stents, etc., mandating insurance coverage for CGMs and 
diabetic medicines on a long term basis to ensure affordability and accessibility. The 
Committee further desires that ‗Make in India‘ initiative shou ld be promoted to reduce prices 
and achieve self-sufficiency in CGM production. 
(Para 5.20(xii) of the report) 
Action Taken 
 
3.37.14 Continuous Glucose Monitoring devices (CGM) device provides real -time 
glucose readings, helping individuals and healthcare providers better manage blood sugar 
levels and prevent complications. CGM devices are primarily used by individuals with 
diabetes, particularly those who use insulin to manage their condition, to help them monitor 
and manage their blood sugar levels in rea l-time.CGMs are especially beneficial for people 
with type 1 diabetes who rely on insulin to manage their condition. The lifespan of a CGMS 
(Continuous Glucose Monitoring System) sensor, like most CGM sensors, is designed to 
function optimally for 7 to 14 days. However, the implantable sensors life span can last for a 
longer period, such as 90 days.  The National Pharmaceutical Pricing Authority (NPPA) has 
focused on regulating prices of essential medicines and medical devices. Specific information 
available about a rate ceiling for CGM devices in not available, however, the proposal can be 
sent to NPPA for their consideration.  The "Make in India" initiative aims to reduce import 
costs and make CGM technology more accessible by promoting local manufacturing. Make in 
India initiatives are driving the development and manufacturing of CGM devices, with 
companies like Beurer India and Abbott launching products like the GL 22 and Free Style 
Libre, respectively, to cater to the Indian market.  
Further Recommendation 
 
3.37.15 The Committee acknowledges the response of the Department 
highlighting the current operational lifespan of CGM sensors especially beneficial in 
type-I diabetes, the potential for longer -lasting implantable sensors, the role of NPPA in 
price regul ation, and the efforts under the ―Make in India‖ initiative to encourage 
indigenous production of CGMs. The Committee observes with deep concern over the 
absence of concrete regulatory intervention on pricing and insurance coverage that 
continue to make CG Ms and essential diabetic medicines unaffordable for a large 

183 
 
segment of patients especially who rely on insulin to manage , particularly those 
requiring lifelong monitoring and medication. The Committee, therefore, recommends 
that the Department, in coordin ation with NPPA and IRDAI, formulate a 
comprehensive framework for price regulation, insurance inclusion, and procurement of 
CGMs and diabetic medicines under national health schemes, ensuring affordability and 
equitable access. The Committee further recom mends that the Department should 
support domestic research and development, and large -scale production of CGMs 
through targeted incentives, public –private partnerships, and expedited approval 
processes under the ―Make in India‖ initiative , to reduce import  dependency and 
achieve long -term self -reliance in diabetes management technologies. The Committee 
also desires that the Department must explore mechanisms to extend the lifespan and 
cost-effectiveness of CGM sensors through innovation and standardization , in 
consultation with industry and scientific experts.

RECOMMENDATIONS/OBSERVATIONS IN RESPECT OF WHICH FINAL

4.1 Central Government Health Scheme (CGHS) 
 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
4.1.1 The Committee notes the Government‘s initiative to integrate the Indian system of 
medicine with the CGHS wellness centre network. However, in its 155th Report, the 
Committee notes that the number of Ayush centres increased from 85 in March 2014 to 107 in 
August 2023, a modest 25% growth over nine years. As of February 2025, this number has 
just risen slightly to 111. The Committee observes with concern that such limited slow pace 
of expansion does not align with the Gove rnment‘s vision to promote indigenous healthcare 
to under-served regions especially in remote areas. The Committee, therefore, recommends 
the speedy operationalization of the remaining 23 Ayush units out of the targeted 53 and 
urges the Government to estab lish additional Ayush centres nationwide while actively 
promoting traditional Indian medicine among CGHS beneficiaries. 
(Para 3.1.6 of the Report) 
Action Taken 
 
4.1.2 In order to expedite the expansion of Ayush Units and operationalization of remaining 
23 Ayush Units, an Inter -Ministerial Convergence Meeting between Ministry of Ayush and 
MoHFW was convened on 20.12. 2024 wherein various issue related to CGHS Ayush 
Wellness Centres were discussed and deliberated upon. 
National Health Mission 
4.2 Budgetary Allocation 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
4.2.1 The Committee recommends prioritizing the strengthening of District Early 
Intervention Centres (DEICs) to match the significant increase in children screened and 
treated under the Rashtriya Bal Swasthya Karyakr am (RBSK). While screening numbers are 
rising, ensuring timely and effective treatment at DEICs is crucial. The Committee suggests a 
comprehensive review of DEIC functionality, including staffing, equipment, and referral 
systems. Based on the review, the D epartment should provide targeted support to DEICs, 
including increased funding, training for personnel, and streamlined referral pathways. This 
will ensure that children identified through RBSK receive the necessary interventions 
promptly, maximizing the program's impact on child health outcomes. Furthermore, the 
committee suggests that the Department create a system of feedback from the families that 
have used the DEIC services, so that the DEIC can be improved based on the users feedback. 
(Para 4.2.13 of the Report) 

185 
 
Action Taken 
 
4.2.2 The Department acknowledges the Committee‘s recommendations regarding the 
strengthening of District Early Intervention Centres (DEICs) under the Rashtriya Bal 
Swasthya Karyakram (RBSK). 
 
4.3 Regular Nursing Cadre 
 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
4.3.1 The Committee recommends a significant increase in the earmarked funds for nursing 
training programs, coupled with the establishment of a dedicated, centrally managed nursing 
cadre. There is need for advanced medical technology and emergency care training to the 
nurses. Therefore, the Committee advocates for a substantial rise in funding for expansion of 
training programs to include specialized modules on cutting -edge medical technologies and 
emergency response protocols. Furthermore, to ensure continuity and attract and retain skilled 
nurses, a regular nursing cadre should be created, offering standardized pay scales, benefits, 
and career progression opportunities across both public and private healthcare sectors, 
building on the existing Supreme Court expert committee recommendations. This would not 
only improve working conditions and incentives but also establish a stable and skilled nursing 
workforce capable of handling complex medical challenges. 
(Para 5.4.2 of the Report) 
Action Taken 
 
4.3.2 The Central Sector scheme of Development Nursing Services (DNS) has been 
formulated considering the need to upgrade quality of nursing education. The same was 
introduced during 11th Five Year Plan and continued with revised patt ern of assistance. The 
scheme has been further continued for the period 2021 -22 till 2025 -26 with an outlay of Rs. 
215.76 crore with the following components: - 
(i) Training of Nurses - To update the knowledge and skills of the in -service nursing 
personnel, funds to the tune of Rs. 2,28,500/ - per course of 7 days duration for 30 
participants is provided for conducting short term training courses in three categories -
Clinical Specialty, Education Technology Nursing Administration. 
 
(ii) Strengthening of existing Sch ool of Nursing - Upgradation of School of Nursing into 
College of Nursing is one of the components of the Central Sector Scheme for 
Development of Nursing. Under this sub -component of the scheme, a onetime financial 
assistance of Rs. 7.00 crore per institut ion is given for upgrading School of Nursing 
attached to a Medical College of Nursing into college of Nursing received from State 
Government in the specified format are considered subject to availability of funds. The 
State Government/Institutions are to bear the recurring expenditure beyond three years.

4.5 Cancer Day Care Centers 
 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
4.5.1 The Committee has come across that a provision of 4 -6 beds is proposed in Day Care 
Centres for cancer Chemotherapy and Chemotherapy and medicines are proposed to be 
provided to the patients in Day Care Centres. The Committee recommends that the said 
proposal gets actualised on the ground level, sooner the better. The Committee also desires 
that separate human Resources (including health professional and nurse) may be provided 
instead of seeking from the pool of District Hospital for smooth functioning of the centre 
since there are already scarcity of health professionals in the District Hospitals where such 
professionals must made available. 
(Para 5.14.3 of the Report) 
Action Taken 
4.5.2 As per the announcement of Union Budget 2025 -26, the government plans to set up 
Day Care Cancer Centres (DCCCs) in district hospitals in the next 3 years, out of which 200 
centres are proposed to be set up in 2025 -26. A comprehensive  gap analysis has been 
undertaken to assess the availability of cancer care infrastructure, medical personnel, and 
essential equipment in district hospitals. Based on the findings, the Ministry, in consultation 
with State Governments, plans to establish 200 Day Care Cancer Centers (DCCCs) in districts 
with a high cancer burden and limited access to cancer care services.
