RECOMMENDATIONS/OBSERVATIONS  - AT   A  GLANCE 
 
Staff Strength in the Department 
 
1. The Committee appreciates the efforts made by the Ministry in pursuing the 
matter of vacancies with the Cadre Controlling Authorities concerned, thereby resulting 
into reduction of 123 vacant posts  due to posting of 63Assistant Statistical Officers and 
60 Multi Tasking Staff. The Committee,  however, observes that despite the  efforts so 
made, a substantial number of 305 posts still remains  vacant, which may yet continue to 
hamper the administrative efficienc y and timely execution of the Ministry’s 
programmes and schemes. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Ministry 
adopt a time-bound action plan, in coordination with the Cadre Controlling Authorities 
and recruiting agencies, to fill up the remaining  305 vacant posts at the earliest so that 
the functional proficiency of the Ministry is optimised and set objectives of various plans 
are achieved without any stumbling block. 
(Para 3.1.3 of the ATR) 
 
Budgetary Projection vis-a-vis Allocation and Expenditure 
 
2. The Committee is of the opinion that health is a multiplier sector of the economy, 
determining productive capital formation and capacity building.  Therefore, the 
Government needs to invest maximally to build other significan t superstructures of the 
economy. The Committee is not appreciative of the plea of the Department of 
highlighting budgetary allocation of too wide base years that is BE 2013 -14 and BE 
2025-26.The Committee observes that despite the increase in the absolute  budgetary 
figures i.e., from Rs. 35,063 crore in BE 2013 -14  to Rs. 95,957. 87 crore  in BE 2025-26, 
the health expenditure continues to remain low as a proportion of GDP i.e. 0.3% at 
current price. The Committee, therefore,  emphasizes that sustained under -projection of 
demand may constrain the human capacity building of the nation to address emerging 
challenges and achieve national health targets. The Committee,  in this regard, 
recommends that the Ministry adopt need-driven approach while projecting its financial 
requirement, ensuring realistic estimation of financial requirement under continuing 
health plans and Schemes/ programmes/ projects as well as futuristic plans and projects. 
The Committee further urges the Ministry to engage with the Ministry of Fi nance to 
progressively enhance the projected demand of health sector with a view to achieving 
the national goal of increasing public health expenditure to at least 2.5% of GDP in line 
with the National Health Policy,2017. 
(Para 3.2.3 of the ATR) 
 
3. The Committee applauds the initiatives taken by the Ministry, including its 
engagement with States to enhance their health budgets to 8% of total State budget, the 
provision of health grants through the 15th Finance Commission, and the 
implementation of th e Pradhan Mantri –Ayushman Bharat Health Infrastructure 

189 
 
Mission (PM-ABHIM) to strengthen healthcare infrastructure and preparedness. While 
these steps are commendable, the Committee observes that the overall public health 
expenditure continues to remain bel ow the National Health Policy, 2017 target of 2.5% 
of GDP. The Committee, therefore, reiterates the need for the Ministry to pursue the 
matter vigorously with the Ministry of Finance to secure higher allocations in 
forthcoming budget cycles, particularly i n light of rising inflation, population growth, 
and post -pandemic healthcare needs.  The Committee believes that the Department 
would work upon further to eliminate gaps by addressing lacuna in the healthcare 
system and seek requisite fund from the 16 th Finance Commission. The Committee 
further recommends the Ministry to establish measurable outcome indicators to monitor 
the effective utilization of funds under PM -ABHIM and ensure that both Central and 
State governments align their health budget priorities with the broader goal of achieving 
equitable, accessible, and resilient healthcare infrastructure across the country. 
(Para 3.2.6 of the ATR) 
 
4. The Committee understands that  capital expenditure inherently fluctuates based 
on the pace and stage of project s, and that the social service nature of the Department 
limits its scope, however, the Committee emphasizes the importance of forward-looking 
planning for raising healthcare infrastructure in far flung rural, semi rural and tribal 
areas and strengthening the primary, Secondary and tertiary healthcare hospitals where 
centrally sponsored schemes are required to be launched and implemented with due  
impetus. The Committee, therefore, feels that the Ministry should develop a 'pipeline' 
document that, while accounting for the fluctuating nature of capital demands, maps out 
the planned trajectory of major infrastructure and capital creation projects  (including 
GIA-Capital) over a three to five -year period. This strategic document should clearly 
articulate the projected annual requirement and justification  for capital funds, thereby 
enabling more predictable and sustained year -on-year growth  in capital allocations 
(even if actual expenditure fluctuates) and  thereby ensuring that the budget process i s 
driven by pre-determined development goals  besides considering the recurring annual 
demands and project status. 
(Para 3.2.9 of the ATR) 
 
Government Health Expenditure in India 
 
5. The Committee appreciates the sustained effortsof the Department of Health  and 
Family to expand healthcare access and affordability through flagship initiatives such as 
NHM, PMJAY, PM -ABHIM, ABDM, and PMBJP, which have collectively contributed 
to the reduction in Out -of-Pocket Expenditure (OOPE) and enhancement of healthcare 
infrastructure. The Committee also acknowledges the steady rise in Government Health 
Expenditure as a share of GDP and Total Health Expenditure. However, the Committee 
observes that OOPE at 39.4%in 2021 -22 still remains considerably high, particularly 
given I ndia’s large population living in poverty and vulnerable socio -economic 
conditions. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Ministry intensify its focus 
on strengthening primary healthcare delivery through Ayushman Arogya Mandirs, 

190 
 
ensuring wider coverage of free drugs and diagnostics, and improving the efficiency and 
reach of Jan AushadhiKendras in rural and remote areas. Further, the Committee urges 
the Ministry to establish a clear roadmap with measurable milestones for reducing 
OOPE below 20%within the next two years by deepening financial protection under 
PMJAY, enhancing State -level health spending, and improving public awareness of 
available government health schemes and services. 
(Para 3.3.4 of the ATR) 
 
6. The Committee commends the steady increase in Government Health 
Expenditure (GHE) both as a share of GDP and of Total Health Expenditure (THE), as 
well as the corresponding decline in Out -of-Pocket Expenditure (OOPE), reflecting the 
Government’s commitment to strengthening public health fi nancing.  The 
Committee,however,observes that despite this progress, India’s GHE remains 
significantly below global benchmarks, particularly those of high -income countries 
where the higher volumeof health expenditure is fundedby Governments of the 
countries. The Committee, therefore, reiterates that the Government should continue to 
enhance GHE in a phased and sustained manner to achieve the National Health Policy 
target of 2.5% of GDP from existing 1.84% as share of GDPwithin projected time 
frame. The Comm ittee further recommends that the Departmentmustundertake a 
comparative study of successful international health financing models to identify 
adaptable best practices for India’sinvestment on healthcare sector. The Committee 
alsourges the Department to ens ure effective convergence and coordination among key 
national health missions and schemes so that increased expenditure directly translates 
into improved service delivery, equitable access, and further reduction of OOPE for the 
wholepopulation, especially among vulnerable and low-income groups. 
(Para 3.3.7 of the ATR) 
 
7. The Committee takes into account the response of the Department which 
attributes that the data lag to the inherent process of compiling actual expenditure from 
Central and State Governments. The Committee notes with concern that the NHA 
Estimates are available at an 18-month lag , while currently preparing the NHA for 
2022-23, confirms the systematic nature of the delay but the Department fails to outline 
any measure to mitigate its impact. The Committee does not agree with plea of the 
Department for the operational necessity for an 18 -month lag in utilizing confirmed 
actual expenditure data, the Committee, however, recommends that the Department 
must concurr ently develop and publish Provisional Health Expenditure Estimates  for 
the most recent financial year. These provisional estimates, based on Revised Estimates 
(RE) for the Central Government and the latest available Budget Estimates (BE)  or 
expenditure trend data for the States, should be released no later than six months  after 
the completion of specific financial year to provide policymakers and analysts with a 
timely, albeit interim, view of key indicators like GHE, THE, and OOPE, thereby 
overcoming the i ssue of critical information deficit that currently hampers effective 
policy assessment. 
(Para 3.3.10 of the ATR) 

191 
 
 
8. The Committee assesses that Ministry's response, stating that "best efforts have 
been made" and that the adoption of digital tools has "al ready initiated," is vague and 
fails to provide any concrete plan, timeline, or measurable targets  for the complete 
digitization of the data collection process, particularly regarding the timely release of 
health expenditure data. The Committee understands  that  the Ministry has simply 
noted the intent of the Committee’s recommendation but without addressing the specific 
concern for a definitive annual data release deadline  (i.e., by the last quarter of the 
following financial year). The Committee, therefore, strongly urges the Department to 
establish and formally announce a clear, documented, and phased Digital Data 
Integration Roadmap . Such roadmap must include specific mi lestones for the full 
deployment of MIS/digital tools across all major schemes, a guaranteed calendar date  
for the publication of provisional health expenditure data, and an assurance that the 
final, actual expenditure data  will be made available no later than the end of the third 
quarter (December) of the financial year immediately following the reporting period, 
thereby moving beyond mere initiation and ensuring accountability in data timeliness. 
(Para 3.3.13 of the ATR) 
 
15th Finance Commission Health Grants 
9. The Committee accords the  initiation made by the Department for a study on the 
implementation effectiveness of the 15thFinance Commission Health Grant through 
NHSRC and NIPFP, complete with initial field visits, which are steps toward 
understanding the underlying causes of under -utilization, however, the action taken is 
silent on establishing the dedicated communication channel for continuous dialogue and 
immediate resolution of challenges. Therefore, the Committee recommends that the 
Ministry must immediately operationalize the proposed dedicated communication 
mechanism, specifying whether it will be a high -level Empowered Working Group or a 
Joint Steering Committee involving both Central and State Finance/Health Secretaries, 
which should meet quarterly. This body should be tasked not merely with reviewing 
fund utilization, but empowered specifically, for adjudicating and resolving financial, 
procedural, and procurement bottlenecks identified during the ongoing study and 
regular review meetings, thereby translating the diagnostic findings into proactive, time-
bound remedial action to accelerate the optimal utilization of the  fund allocated to 
address gaps in crucial primary healthcare facilities across the country. 
 
(Para 3.4.3 of the ATR) 
Central Government Health Scheme (CGHS) 
 
10. The Committee notes with appreciation the steps taken by the Department to 
enhance access to advanced medical care for CGHS beneficiaries through the 
empanelment of a large number of tertiary care and multi -specialty hospitals, including 
reputed institutions such as Tata Memorial Cancer Hospital and AIIMS. Nevertheless, 
the Committee observes that  reliance solely on empaneled hospitals may not be a 

192 
 
sustainable long -term solution to meet the growing healthcare demands of CGHS 
beneficiaries, particularly in major metropolitan areas where patient load is already too 
high and also because of indifferen ce of high profile profiteering healthcare 
metropolitan private hospitals towards CGHS beneficiaries due to low OPD and 
procedural rate of CGHS. The Committee, therefore, reiterates its earlier 
recommendation for establishing dedicated CGHS hospitals with state-of-the-art 
facilities and integrated research capabilities in major cities across the country. These 
hospitals should focus on critical and lifestyle -related diseases such as cancer, 
cardiovascular ailments, renal disorders, and other non -communicable diseases. The 
Committee further recommends that the Ministry prepare a detailed roadmap with 
timelines, funding strategy, and inter-agency coordination plan to operationalize at least 
ten such advanced CGHS hospitals in a phased manner to ensure comprehe nsive, 
affordable, and high -quality healthcare services for government employees and 
pensioners. 
(Para 3.5.3 of the ATR) 
 
11. The Committee is aware of the distinction between medicines supplied under the 
Pradhan Mantri Bhartiya Janaushadhi Yojana (PMBJP) and those procured through 
the Central Government Health Scheme (CGHS) system notwithstanding the fact stated 
the Medicine procured under PMBJP are supplied at CGHS centres.  The Committee 
takes note of the notification of a revised schedule by the Central Drugs Standard 
Control Organisation (CDSCO) but with no detail. However, the Committee emphasizes 
that the assurance of drug quality within the CGHS framework remains of utmost 
importance to safeguard beneficiary health and trust in the system. The Committ ee, 
therefore, recommends the Department to establish a robust and transparent 
mechanism for periodic quality testing of medicines supplied through the Medical Store 
Organisation (MSO) and other CGHS procurement channels, ensuring full compliance 
with WHO-GMP and national quality standards. Further, the Committee advises that a 
centralized complaint redressal and pharmacovigilance system be developed to 
promptly address and track quality -related grievances, supported by random sampling, 
third-party audits, and public disclosure of test results to enhance accountability and 
confidence in the medicines dispensed at CGHS dispensaries. 
(Para 3.5.6 of the ATR) 
 
12. The Committee notes the Ministry’s assurance that drugs procured through 
MSO/GMSD are subjected to random sampling and tested at NABL -accredited 
laboratories before being supplied to CGHS Wellness Centres. While this is a positive 
step toward maintaining quality assurance, the Committee emphasizes that a more 
comprehensive, continuous, and transparent q uality control mechanism is essential to 
prohibit spurious drugs and maintaining high quality medicine for upholding the 
highest standards of patient safety and trust. The Committee, therefore, recommends 
that the Department must institutionalize a structu red, periodic drug testing and audit 
framework at the Wellness Centre level, including unannounced and surprise 
inspections and random post -distribution sampling of medicines. Further, the 

193 
 
Committee advises the Department to expedite the empanelment of the  Quality Council 
of India or any other competent independent agency for enhanced oversight, introduce a 
digital tracking system to record test results and quality compliance, and publicly 
disclose periodic quality audit summaries to ensure transparency and  accountability in 
the drug supply chain under CGHS. 
(Para 3.5.9 of the ATR) 
 
13. The Committee appreciates the progressive relaxation of consultation norms that 
now allow CGHS beneficiaries aged 70 years and above to directly consult specialists at 
empaneled hospitals, as well as the introduction of digital health services through the e -
Sanjeevani portal to facilitate remote consultations. However, the Committee observes 
that a large proportion of CGHS pensioners and beneficiaries aged 60 years and above 
continue to face difficulties in accessing specialist care due to procedural requirements 
and physical limitations. The Committee, therefore, reiterates its earlier 
recommendation that the age threshold for direct specialist consultation be further 
reduced to 60 years to ensure timely and convenient access to medical care for senior 
citizens. The Committee also recommends that the Ministry strengthen the integration 
between e-Sanjeevani services and CGHS records to enable seamless continuity of care, 
including digital referrals, e -prescription management, and follow -up consultations. 
Further, awareness campaigns should be conducted to familiarize elderly beneficiaries 
with the use of teleconsultation services, thereby reducing their dependency on in-person 
visits to Wellness Centres for routine medical needs. 
(Para 3.5.12 of the ATR) 
 
14. The Committee acknowledges the submission of the Department of Health and 
Family Welfare that recruitment for vacant positions in CGHS dispensaries is being 
carried out regularly through the Combined Medical Services Examination and that an 
Integrated Recruitment System has been introduced to streamline the process for Group 
B and C posts. While these steps are commendable, the Committee still remains 
concerned about the persi stently high vacancy levels i.e. 2023 positions (28.81% of the 
sanctioned strength) in Allopathic dispensaries and 111 positions in Ayush dispensaries, 
which continue to affect the quality and accessibility of healthcare services for CGHS 
beneficiaries. Th e Committee, therefore, recommends that the Ministry must adopt a 
time-bound recruitment plan with clearly defined annual targets to fill up all existing 
vacancies and formulate strategic plan to avoid accumulation of vacancies in future. 
Further, the Comm ittee advises the Ministry to conduct a comprehensive manpower 
assessment to rationalize staffing patterns across CGHS cities based on beneficiary load 
and service demand. The Committee also recommends exploring the measures such as 
telemedicine support and task-sharing with AYUSH practitioners to bridge critical gaps 
until regular recruitment is completed, thereby ensuring uninterrupted and quality 
healthcare delivery to all CGHS beneficiaries. 
(Para 3.5.15 of the ATR) 
 
 

194 
 
Safdurjung Hospital (SJH) & Vardhman Mahavir Medical College (VMMC) 
 
15. The Committee notes the Ministry’s action in initiating the creation of 1,100 
posts across various categories and the recruitment of 933 personnel being undertaken 
during 2024 –25 to address manpower shortages in  Safdarjung Hospital (SJH) and 
Vardhman Mahavir Medical College (VMMC). While these steps indicate progress, the 
Committee remains concerned that substantial vacancies still persists, particularly in 
both teaching and non -teaching cadres, which continue to  affect the institutes’ 
operational efficiency, academic quality, and patient care. The Committee expresses 
concern that 32.6% of posts in Group A, 87.5% Group B Gazetted, 16.5 %in Group B 
Non-Gazetted and 35.3% in Group C are lying vacant in the said Inst itute. The 
Committee, therefore, recommends that the Ministry finalize and implement the 
proposed creation of posts without further delay and establish a time-bound recruitment 
schedule to ensure that all sanctioned positions are filled up within a fixed p eriod. The 
Committee further advises adopting a proactive retention strategy to address the 
recurring issue of resignations and non -joining, including incentives for long -term 
service, improved working conditions, and streamlined recruitment procedures. Th e 
Committee, additionally, recommends introducing a centralized digital manpower 
management system to monitor vacancies, recruitments, and attrition trends in real time 
to ensure that faculty, technical, and administrative staffing levels remain adequate a nd 
responsive to the growing academic and healthcare workload at SJH and VMMC. 
 
(Para 3.6.3 of the ATR) 
 
Dr. Rammanohar Lohia Hospital (RML), New Delhi and ABVIMS 
 
16. The Committee notes the Ministry’s efforts to address space constraints through 
the allotment of 8.871 acres of land near Birla Mandir for the expansion of healthcare 
infrastructure and the proactive steps taken to request additional vacant government 
land for staff residences, hostels, and support facilities. While these measures are 
encouraging, the Committee remains seriously concerned about the persistent delays in 
the completion of the Super Specialty Block (SSB) and other infrastructure projects, 
which have now faced multiple deadline extensions. The Committee, therefore, strongly 
recommends that the Ministry institute a strict project monitoring and accountability 
mechanism with clearly defined milestones, quarterly progress reviews, and real -time 
reporting to ensure adherence to the revised timelines. Further, the Committee advises 
that the Department must strengthen coordination with implementing agencies such as 
HITES, ensure timely release and utilization of funds, and impose performance -linked 
accountability measures on contractors and executing agencies responsible for delays. 
The C ommittee also recommends that the Ministry prepare a comprehensive master 
plan for long -term campus development to optimize the use of newly allotted and 
proposed land, thereby addressing both current space shortages and future institutional 
expansion needs. 
(Para 3.7.4 of the ATR) 
 

195 
 
17. The Committee notes the Ministry’s submission that the process to fill up the 
vacant posts in Dr. RML Hospital and ABVIMS has been initiated but without any 
details of steps taken by the Department  in that direction. Howeve r, given that nearly 
one-fourth of the sanctioned posts across various cadres remain unfilled, the Committee 
emphasizes that mere initiation of the recruitment process is insufficient without defined 
timelines and measurable progress. The Committee, theref ore, recommends that the 
Department must prepare and adhere to a time -bound recruitment plan with clear 
quarterly targets for filling up vacancies in both teaching and non -teaching positions. 
Regular monitoring of recruitment progress should be undertaken at the highest 
administrative level, and periodic status reports may be submitted to the Committee 
until all vacancies are filled up. 
(Para 3.7.7 of the ATR) 
 
18. The Committee takes note of the data furnished by the Department regarding the 
large patient inflow and extensive range of services being provided by Dr. RML Hospital 
and ABVIMS. While the Committee appreciates the hospital’s commitment to ensuring 
that no patient is denied treatment despite overcrowding, it remains concerned about 
the sustainabil ity of system due to heavy patients load and the potential strain on 
infrastructure and human resources. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the 
Department should undertake a comprehensive workload assessment of the hospital to 
identify gaps in manpo wer, infrastructure, and equipment. It further recommends the 
introduction of a structured patient load management system, including strengthening 
referral linkages with other Central and State hospitals, expansion of satellite OPD 
centres, and enhancement  of digital and telemedicine services to reduce unnecessary 
congestion. The Committee also urges the Department to consider allocating additional 
resources and budgetary support to augment diagnostic, surgical, and emergency 
capacities to ensure timely, quality, and equitable healthcare delivery. 
(Para 3.7.10 of the ATR)

infrastructure and equipment upg rades. The Committee, therefore, recommends the

defined timelines to ensure timely completion of tendering and approval processes. The 
Committee further suggests the Department to engage with GeM to resolve competition-
related issues by encouraging vendor participation and prequalifying suppliers for 
commonly procured items. In addition, the Committee recommends that, where 
redevelopment plans constrain capital spending, the Dep artment should adopt a phased 
investment approach to ensure maintenance and safety standards are not compromised 
while awaiting redevelopment. Regular quarterly reviews of capital expenditure 
progress should be institutionalized to enhance accountability a nd improve utilisation 
efficiency.

impacts the hospital’s capacity to deliver qualit y pediatric care. The Committee,

recruitment processes and ensure timely convening of DPCs to fill up promotional 
vacancies. The Committee further urges the Department to  fast-track the revision and 
approval of Recruitment Rules (RRs) to prevent administrative delays in recruitment 
process. Additionally, the Committee recommends instituting a periodic manpower 
review mechanism to monitor staffing adequacy and ensure that vacancies do not persist 
for prolonged periods, thereby maintaining optimal doctor -to-patient and staff -to-
patient ratios in the hospital.

further urges the Department to explore alternative funding or partnership models to 
avoid delays arising from non -release of State shares. The Committee also recommends 
that a consolidated progress report, including reasons for delay and updated timelines, 
be placed in  the public domain to enhance transparency and ensure that all remaining 
GMCI upgradation projects under PMSSY are completed in a mission -mode within the

35. The Committee takes note of the substantial delay in the residential 
redevelopment projects at West Ansari Nagar (WAN) and Ayur Vigyan Na gar (AVN) 
campuses, due to ban on approval of trees felling/transplantation by Hon’ble High 
Court of Delhi and slump in the market due to outbreak of Covid -19 pandemic. The 
Committee is concerned over persisting financial and regulatory impediments, 
particularly the restriction imposed by DDA on monetization of commercial space and 
the continued delays arising from statutory approvals. The Committee, in this 
connection, recommends that the Department should, in consultation with NBCC and 
the Ministry of Hou sing and Urban Affairs, revisit the project’s financial structuring 
and explore alternative funding and monetization mechanisms within the existing 
regulatory framework to ensure project viability. The Committee further urges the 
Department to establish a clear roadmap with revised timelines, cost projections, and 
progress milestones, along with a periodic monitoring mechanism to the project. The 
Committee emphasizes that inter -departmental coordination and timely resolution of 

203 
 
regulatory bottlenecks are es sential to prevent further time and cost overruns and 
ensure the expeditious completion of the redevelopment project. 
(Para 3.18.6 of the ATR)

Residents. However, the Committee notes w ith concern that despite these measures, a 
large number of vacancies across faculty, non-faculty, and resident cadres persist, which 
must have adversely impacting the functioning and quality of healthcare delivery at the

(Para 3.18.10 of the ATR)

National Health Mission 
Budgetary Allocation 
  
45. The Committee appreciates the comprehensive resp onse of the Department and 
acknowledges the substantial progress achieved under the National Health Mission 
(NHM) in reducing Maternal Mortality Ratio (MMR) and Infant Mortality Rate (IMR), 
as well as expanding newborn and child health interventions across  the country. The 
Committee commends that India has already achieved the National Health Policy target 
for MMR and is on track to achieve the SDG target ahead of 2030. However, the 
Committee remains concerned about the persisting regional disparities, part icularly in 
States where MMR and IMR remain significantly higher than the national average, 
such as Assam, Bihar, and Rajasthan. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the 
Department must adopt a region-specific, evidence -based strategy  with enhanced 
financial and technical support to high -burden MMR and IMR States. The Committee 
further urges the Department to strengthen data analytics and monitoring systems 
under NHM to enable real-time tracking of maternal and infant health outcomes, and to 
ensure con vergence of RMNCAH+N initiatives with social determinants such as 
nutrition, sanitation, and women’s education. The Committee also desires that specific 
Action Plan be formulated and periodic progress reports highlighting measurable 
improvements in high -burden incidence of MMR and IMR in States be prioritized for 
review and revisit for requisite interventions till desirable result is not achieved under 
NHM, as a whole. 
(Para 3.25.3 of the ATR) 
 
Functioning of Central Drugs Standard Control Organisation (CDSCO) 
 
46. The Committee acknowledges the various initiatives undertaken by the Ministry 
and CDSCO, such as the implementation of the MD Online portal, creation of a Medical 
Devices Vertical , establishment of testing laboratories and notified bodies , and the  
launch of the MedTech Mitra platform to support innovators and startups. While these 
measures are commendable steps towards improving regulatory efficiency and 
transparency, the Committee observes that persistent industry concerns  remain 
regarding delays,  inconsistent timelines, and lack of end -to-end real -time tracking of 
licensing applications. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Department 
must ensure the complete digitization and automation of the licensing process  through a 
unified and integrated digital platform linking central and state licensing authorities, 
with mandatory public disclosure of application status, query logs, and processing 
timelines. The Committee further urges that a robust performance monitori ng and 
accountability framework be instituted to track adherence to defined service timelines 
and penalize undue delays. Additionally, the Committee desires that CDSCO leverage 
AI-driven analytics to identify procedural bottlenecks, reduce manual interventions, and 
improve predictability in approvals so as to foster a globally competitive and innovation-
friendly medical device ecosystem in India. 
(Para 3.26.4 of the ATR) 
 

208 
 
47. The Committee appreciates the steps taken by the Department and CDSCO to 
enhance transparency and reduce procedural ambiguities, including the introduction of 
tooltips on the MD Online portal , publication of updated FAQs and guidance 
documents, establishment of Public Relations Offices (PROs) , and regular stakeholder 
consultations and o pen meetings by the DCGI. However, the Committee observes that 
despite these initiatives, applicants —particularly startups and small -scale 
manufacturers—continue to face multiple and sequential queries, causing delays and 
uncertainty in approval timelines.  The Committee, therefore, recommends the 
Department for implementing a ―single comprehensive query‖ system  to ensure that all 
observations are raised in one go, thereby minimizing repetitive communication and 
expediting approvals. The Committee further recommends the adoption of a time-bound 
conditional approval mechanism , especially for products possessing recognized 
international certifications such as CE or USFDA, to promote ease of doing business and 
encourage innovation. The Committee also desires CDS CO to establish a dedicated 
regulatory facilitation cell for startups within the MedTech Mitra framework to provide 
real-time query assistance and guidance, ensuring that India’s regulatory environment 
becomes more predictable, responsive, and innovation-friendly. 
(Para 3.26.8 of the ATR) 
 
48. The Committee notes with appreciation the establishment of a dedicated Medical 
Device Vertical within CDSCO and the initiation of the recruitment process under the 
newly notified Recruitment Rules (GSR 689 E, dated 06 .11.2024), as well as the 
extensive training and international exposure programs conducted to enhance 
regulatory capacity. However, the Committee emphasizes that the recruitment process 
for key technical and regulatory positions must be expedited to ensure the vertical 
operates at full strength and delivers consistent, timely outcomes. The Committee 
further recommends that the lateral induction of domain experts  from the medical 
device industry, academia, and research institutions be actively purs ued to bridge the 
skill gap and introduce global best practices. Additionally, CDSCO may consider 
establishing a structured, continuous professional development framework with periodic 
certification and assessment modules to ensure sustained competency and  regulatory 
excellence. The Committee understands the pivotal role of a robust and transparent 
rotation policy to prevent complacency and ensuring that officers gain diverse field and 
headquarters experience, thereby strengthening regulatory integrity and fostering a 
more proactive, collaborative engagement with the medical device industry. 
 
(Para 3.26.11 of the ATR) 
 
49. The Committee appreciates the initiatives undertaken by the Department of 
Health and Family Welfare and CDSCO to strengthen stakeholder e ngagement through 
the establishment of Public Relations Offices (PROs) , the MedTech Mitra portal , 
regular walk-in consultations , industry awareness programs , and the creation of the 
Medical Devices Technical Advisory Group (MDTAG) . These steps indicate a p ositive 
movement towards greater transparency and industry collaboration. The Committee is 
of the view that these mechanisms, while commendable, largely serve as consultative or 

209 
 
facilitative platforms and do not fully meet the intent of the Committee’s ear lier 
recommendation for a dedicated and independent Industry Advisory Board . The 
Committee, therefore, recommends that the Ministry institutionalize such a board with 
statutory or formal advisory status , comprising balanced representation from startups, 
MSMEs, major manufacturers, healthcare providers, and independent experts , to 
provide structured and periodic feedback on regulatory practices, policy evolution, and 
dispute resolution. The Committee further recommends that the outcomes of 
consultations, inc luding minutes of stakeholder meetings and action taken reports , be 
made publicly accessible to enhance transparency and accountability. A fixed biannual 
schedule for stakeholder consultations  should also be adopted to ensure regular and 
meaningful dialogu e, thereby fostering a culture of trust, responsiveness, and shared 
responsibility between the regulator and the medical device industry. 
 
(Para 3.26.16 of the ATR)

technical support units should be strengthened further at the state level to assist with 
financial management and reporting. The Committee also rec ommends that the 
Department of Expenditure and Department of Health and Family Welfare must  
streamline procedural requirements for fund release under centrally sponsored 
schemes, particularly for mental health interventions, so that programme delivery is n ot 
hampered by administrative delays. A mid-year utilization review  mechanism may also 
be instituted to identify underperforming components and allow timely reallocation of 
unspent funds to more efficient programmes.

Ayushman Bharat- Pradhan Mantri Jan Arogya Yojana (AB-PMJAY) 
54. The Committee appreciates th e steps taken by the Department of Health and 
Family Welfare in implementing the Ayushman Vay Vandana initiative under AB -
PMJAY and acknowledges the impressive enrolment of over 58 lakh senior citizens and 
the dedicated budgetary provision of ₹1,443 crore for their treatment. The Committee, 
however, observes that while the current coverage for citizens aged 70 years and above 
is a commendable beginning, it still excludes a large segment of the elderly population 
between 60 to 69 years who are equally vulner able to chronic and age -related diseases 
and may not have adequate financial means for quality healthcare. The Committee is 
pleased to observe that the Department is positive enough to continue and explore to 
extend healthcare coverage to vulnerable sectio n of the population. The Committee, 
therefore, reiterates its earlier recommendation to revisit and rationalize the age criteria 
to include citizens aged 60 years and above , ensuring equitable healthcare protection for 
all senior citizens irrespective of s ocio-economic status. Further, the Committee 
recommends the Department to undertake a feasibility study  assessing the financial, 
infrastructural, and operational implications of such expansion. The Committee also 
suggests periodic evaluation of the scheme’s utilization patterns and health outcomes  to 
ensure that the benefits reach the intended beneficiaries and to support data -driven 
decisions for future expansions of universal health coverage under AB-PMJAY. 
 
(Para 3.30.3 of the ATR) 
 
55. The Committee takes into consideration the plea of the Department that the 
existing coverage of ₹5 lakh per family under AB -PMJAY meets the hospitalization 
needs of most beneficiaries, and that additional financial protection is available under 
the Rashtriya Arogya Nidhi (RAN) for critical cases. The Committee is, however, of the 

212 
 
considered view that the rising cost of advanced medical procedures, specialized  
treatments, and long -term care  necessitates a progressive enhancement of financial 
coverage under AB-PMJAY. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Ministry 
undertake a comprehensive actuarial and utilization study  to assess the financial and 
operational feasibility of increasing the insurance ceiling to ₹10 lakh per family, keeping 
in vie w high cost of treatment of patients with chronic, life -threatening disease. 
Alternatively, as an interim measure the Department may consider for strengthening the 
integration between AB -PMJAY and RAN  to ensure seamless support for beneficiaries 
requiring extended or repeated treatments, and to develop an automatic top -up 
mechanism for families exhausting their coverage limit under AB -PMJAY. This 
calibrated approach would ensure enhanced financial protection and accessible 
treatment of patients under PMJAY while maintaining fiscal sustainability and 
equitable access to critical healthcare. 
 
(Para 3.30.6 of the ATR) 
 
Coverage of OPD Services and Cost of Medicine under the Ayushman Bharat 
 
56. The Committee notes the response of the Department that outpatient services are 
currently provided through the Ayushman Arogya Mandir (AAM) network and that 
AB-PMJAY focuses primarily on secondary and tertiary inpatient care. While 
acknowledging the complementary role of AAM in providing OPD services, the 
Committee is of the view that the absence of integrated OPD and extended post -
hospitalization medication coverage within AB -PMJAY limits continuity of care  and 
increases the out -of-pocket burden, especially for patients with chronic and long -term 
conditions. The Committee, therefore, urges the Department to initiate a pilot project in 
select districts to assess the feasibility of including essential OPD consultations, 
diagnostic services, and post-hospitalization medication coverage beyond the current 15-
day limit, viz.up to 30 or 45 days,  under AB -PMJAY for continuity of treatment of 
disease under single umbrella. This pilot should focus particularly on non-
communicable diseases and geriatric care , where follow -up treatment and medication 
adherence are critical. Based on t he outcomes of this pilot, a phased national roll -out 
scheme may be undertaken to ensure a seamless continuum of care between AAM and 
AB-PMJAY, thereby enhancing accessibility, affordability, and overall health outcomes 
for beneficiaries. 
(Para 3.31.3 of the ATR) 
 
Misuse of Fund under AB-PMJAY Scheme 
 
57. The Committee appreciates the comprehensive and technology -driven measures 
adopted by the Department and the NHA for fraud detection and prevention under AB -
PMJAY, including the establishment of National a nd State Anti -Fraud Units (NAFU

213 
 
that while significant progress has been made in internal monitoring , however, there 
remains a need to institutionalize periodic public disclosure of performance indicators  
related to fraud detection, de -empanelment, and recovery act ions to enhance 
transparency and public confidence. The Committee, therefore, recommends that NHA 
should publish a biannual ―Fraud and Integrity Report‖  detailing fraud detection 
trends, actions taken, and improvements in monitoring mechanisms at both nati onal 
and state levels. Further, capacity-building programmes for State Anti -Fraud Units  
should be strengthened to ensure uniform standards of investigation and enforcement 
across all States. The Committee also suggests the integration of beneficiary feedba ck 
analytics and hospital audit outcomes  into the AI -driven monitoring framework to 
create a more holistic, responsive, and publicly accountable system for safeguarding the 
integrity and trustworthiness of AB -PMJAY so as to make AB -PMJAY a popular 
scheme amongst common masses especially, rural, semi-urban and tribal areas. 
 
(Para 3.32.4 of the ATR) 
 
Pradhan Mantri National Dialysis Programme (PMNDP) 
 
58. The Committee appreciates the efforts of the Department under the Pradhan 
Mantri National Dialysis Programme (PMNDP) in ensuring free dialysis services to BPL 
patients and the initiative taken by several States to extend dialysis facilities to the 
Taluka l evel. The Committee, however, notes with concern that the reliance on the 
outdated 2011 Census data for determining BPL status may lead to the exclusion of 
genuinely needy patients and the inclusion of non -eligible beneficiaries. The Committee, 
therefore, recommends that the Department, in coordination with the States/UTs, 
should develop an updated, verifiable, and dynamic beneficiary database  leveraging 
digital health platforms such as ABHA ID and SECC data to ensure accurate 
identification of economically  weaker patients. Further, to address the growing burden 
of chronic kidney disease, the Committee urges the Department to expedite the scaling 
up of dialysis centres to all Taluka -level Community Health Centres (CHCs)  through a 
time-bound national expansio n plan, ensuring equitable geographic access. The 
Committee also emphasizes the need for the creation of a National Renal Registry  to 
generate reliable epidemiological data for effective planning, monitoring, and resource 
allocation under PMNDP and take in to stock actual number of renal patients so that 
fresh policy initiative can be undertaken. 
(Para 3.33.3 of the ATR) 
 
Trauma and Emergency Care Services 
59. The Committee notes with appreciation the progress made under the National 
Programme for Prevention  and Management of Trauma and Burn Injuries 
(NPPMT&BI), including the establishment of Trauma Care Facilities (TCFs), Burn 
Units, and seven Centres of Excellence (CoEs) for advanced trauma and burn care. 
Despite these achievements, the Committee observes w ith concern that out of total 196 
TCFs and 47 Burn units created so far, only a total of 163 TCFs and 31 Burn units are 

214 
 
functional. The Committee is surprised over the absence of provisions for identifying 
and funding new TCFs beyond the 12th Plan period ( 2012-2017) that might have 
hindered the expansion of trauma care infrastructure, particularly in underserved 
regions. The Committee, therefore, recommends the Department  should formulate a 
comprehensive roadmap for the next phase of the programme beyond 20 25-26, with 
dedicated budgetary provisions for the establishment of new trauma and burn care 
facilities at strategic locations, especially along national highways and in high -accident-
prone districts. The Committee further emphasizes that the existing CoEs  should be 
adequately funded and networked with district and sub -district hospitals  through 
telemedicine, referral, and training linkages to ensure an integrated continuum of 
trauma care. The Committee urges the Department to undertake regular monitoring 
and performance audits of functional units  to assess outcomes, resource utilization, and 
preparedness, thereby ensuring that the objectives of reducing trauma -related 
morbidity and mortality are effectively realized. 
(Para 3.34.4 of the ATR) 
 
Public Private Partnership (PPP) Model for Promoting Medical Tourism 
 
60. The Committee acknowledges the pivotal role of inter -ministerial coordination 
and stakeholder engagement in promoting medical tourism. The Committee also 
appreciates the role of the Department in its ongoing efforts to collaborate with industry 
bodies such  as FICCI and NATHEALTH. The Committee observes that despite 
significant potential, public sector participation in medical tourism remains minimal  
due to high domestic demand and lack of a structured framework. The Committee, 
therefore, recommends that the Ministry of Health and Family Welfare, in coordination 
with the Ministries of Tourism and External Affairs, develop a comprehensive National 
Medical and Wellness Tourism Strategy  with a clear roadmap for leveraging both 
public and private healthcare infrastructure. Such strategy should include identification 
of select public hospitals with spare capacity to serve international patients , 
establishment of Medical Tourism Facilitation Cells , and formulation of standardized 
service and pricing protocols  to ensure quality and transparency. The Committee 
further recommends creating a Public-Private Partnership (PPP) model for capacity 
building, accreditation, and international promotion , supported by targeted training for 
healthcare workers and seamless vi sa facilitation for medical travelers. Such a 
coordinated and institutionalized approach will not only enhance India’s global 
reputation in medical and wellness tourism but also generate additional revenue for 
reinvestment in the public healthcare system. 
 
(Para 3.35.4 of the ATR) 
Accessible and Affordable Treatment of Rare Diseases 
61. The Committee acknowledges the significant progress in expanding the scope of 
the National Policy for Rare Diseases (NPRD) 2021, viz. the inclusion of additional 
diseases, establishment of 13 Centres of Excellence (CoEs), and the launch of initiatives 
such as the National Registry and the Indigenous Therapeutics Programme. The 

215 
 
Committee observes that awareness, early diagnosis, and affo rdability of treatment 
remain key challenges , particularly in rural and underserved areas. The Committee, 
therefore, recommends that the Ministry intensify its national awareness and outreach 
campaign through collaboration with State Governments, medical i nstitutions, and civil 
society organizations to ensure greater public and professional understanding of rare 
diseases. Further, the Committee urges the Department to expedite the 
operationalization of the National Rare Disease Information Portal  and integrate it with 
the CoEs and the National Registry for real -time data sharing, patient tracking, and 
referral facilitation. The Committee also recommends increasing the number of CoEs 
across all regions , particularly in the North -East and smaller States, to im prove 
accessibility. In addition, affordable diagnostic and therapeutic solutions should be 
prioritized under the DHR -ICMR Indigenous Therapeutics Programme  with defined 
timelines for clinical translation. The Committee emphasizes that a dedicated National 
Awareness and Prevention Mission on Rare Diseases  may be launched and promote 
early detection, genetic counseling, and social support mechanisms for affected families. 
 
(Para 3.36.5 of the ATR) 
 
Other Recommendations of the Committee 
 
62. The Committee ta kes note of the measures taken by the Department and the 
regulatory bodies such as National Medical Commission (NMC) and Indian Nursing 
Council (INC) to improve the quality and standards of medical and nursing education in 
alignment with global benchmarks,  including accreditation of NMC by WFME for a 
period of 10 years indicating quality of the Indian Medical Colleges and implementation 
of NQAS-based frameworks providing excellence in nursing education sector leading to 
Quality Assurance and Quality Sustena nce in nursing education sector. The Committee 
observes with deep concern that the population-based approach for sanctioning medical 
colleges, as mandated under the UG -MSR 2023, has been deferred for successive 
academic years 2024 -25 and 2025 -26, thereby delaying the correction of regional 
imbalances in medical education and healthcare accessibility. The Committee, therefore, 
recommends that the Department should expedite the implementation of the population-
based criterion  for establishing medical colleges to ensure equitable distribution of 
healthcare infrastructure across States and regions, especially in underserved and 
densely populated are as. Further, the Committee recommends that periodic quality 
audits and independent assessments  be institutionalized for both medical and nursing 
colleges to ensure adherence to NQAS and global best practices. The Committee also 
urges the Ministry to fast-track the nationwide rollout of the Quality Council of India 
(QCI)-led accreditation of nursing institutions  and develop a centralized digital 
dashboard for monitoring institutional ratings, faculty adequacy, and training 
outcomes. In addition, the Committe e emphasizes the need for continuous professional 
development programmes, faculty exchange, and integration of simulation -based and 
digital learning tools  to further enhance the competency and global competitiveness of 
India’s healthcare education system. 
(Para 3.37.5 of the ATR) 

216 
 
 
63. The Committee appreciates the progress achieved in operationalizing a large 
network of Ayushman Arogya Mandirs (AAMs) and ensuring availability of staff, 
essential diagnostics, and generic medicines under the Free Drugs and Di agnostics 
Services Initiative. The Committee notes that despite the commendable coverage 
achieved, a considerable proportion of AAMs are yet to attain full compliance in the 
availability of essential medicines and diagnostic services, which are vital for e ffective 
primary healthcare delivery. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the 
Department intensify its efforts to achieve 100% availability of essential drugs and 
diagnostics across all AAMs  through improved supply chain management, real -time 
monitoring, and periodic stock audits using digital dashboards. The Committee also 
recommends strengthening human resource deployment  by filling the remaining 
vacancies of Community Health Officers (CHOs) and allied health staff through 
targeted recruitment drive s and skill enhancement programmes. The Committee 
suggests that the Department institutionalize a performance-linked incentive mechanism 
for States/UTs based on availability of essential supplies and service delivery standards 
at AAMs. The Committee also e mphasizes the need for integration of telemedicine, e -
diagnostics, and central procurement systems  to ensure timely and equitable access to 
quality healthcare and medicines, particularly in remote and rural areas. 
 
(Para 3.37.8 of the ATR) 
 
64. The Committee takes note of the various benefits and  payment made to ASHA 
workers intended towards enhancing the incentives and social security benefits 
including the recent upward revision of their routine and recurring activity incentive 
from ₹2,000 to ₹3,500, ben efits under the ASHA Benefit Package, and their inclusion 
under schemes such as PMJJBY, PMSBY, PM -SYM, and AB -PMJAY. The Committee 
is of the view that despite these revision in remuneration and benefits, the total 
remuneration received by ASHAs continues t o remain inadequate considering the 
critical and multifaceted role ASHAs play in delivering primary healthcare services at 
the grassroots level and their increasing workload with the expansion of government 
health programmes. The Committee, therefore, reco mmends the Department to 
formulate a comprehensive policy framework to ensure fair, uniform, and sustainable 
remuneration for ASHAs across all States/UTs , taking into account regional cost -of-
living variations and inflation. The Committee further recommend s that the 
Department should consider transitioning ASHAs from a purely incentive -based model 
to a mixed model with a fixed monthly honorarium supplemented by performance -
linked incentives , thereby ensuring income stability and recognition of their essenti al 
public health contribution. The Committee also urges the Department to institutionalize 
mechanisms for periodic review of incentives , strengthen health insurance and pension 
coverage, and ensure timely disbursement of dues to maintain ASHAs’ motivation and 
retention in the community health workforce. 
 
(Para 3.37.12 of the ATR) 
 

217 
 
65. The Committee acknowledges the response of the Department highlighting the 
current operational lifespan of CGM sensors especially beneficial in type -I diabetes, the 
potential for longer -lasting implantable sensors, the role of NPPA in price regulation, 
and the efforts under the ―Make in India‖ initiative to encourage indigenous production 
of CGMs. The Committee observes with deep concern over the absence of concrete 
regulatory intervention on pricing and insurance coverage that continue to make CGMs 
and essential diabetic medicines unaffordable for a large segment of patients especially 
who rely on insulin to manage , particularly those requiring lifelong monitoring and 
medication. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Department, in 
coordination with NPPA and IRDAI, formulate a comprehensive framework for price 
regulation, insurance inclusion, and procurement of CGMs and diabetic medicines 
under national health schemes , e nsuring affordability and equitable access. The 
Committee further recommends that the Department should support domestic research 
and development, and large -scale production of CGMs through targeted incentives, 
public–private partnerships, and expedited ap proval processes under the ―Make in 
India‖ initiative , to reduce import dependency and achieve long -term self -reliance in 
diabetes management technologies. The Committee also desires that the Department 
must explore mechanisms to extend the lifespan and co st-effectiveness of CGM sensors 
through innovation and standardization , in consultation with industry and scientific 
experts. 
(Para 3.37.15 of the ATR)
